--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening table of contents or summary for the work, following the blank flyleaf previously described. It establishes the structure of the book, which translates Giovanni Boccaccio’s Latin biographies of famous women into the German vernacular.</meta>

[Top right corner]
1

[Main text block]
¶ Here follows the brief summary of several women about whom Giovanni Boccaccio has written in Latin and Doctor Heinrich Steinhöwel has translated into German. <note>original: "geteütschet." Steinhöwel was a famous humanist physician who helped popularize classical literature in the German-speaking world.</note>

¶ Chapter 1. Concerning Eve, on page 1.
¶ Eve transgressed the single commandment of God, and some believe she was the first to discover spinning.

¶ Chapter 2. Concerning Semiramis, page 2.
¶ Semiramis ruled in the guise of her son. She won the land of the Moors and India. She built Babylon and subdued those rebels. She had many men in impurity, including her own son. She permitted unchastity to everyone. She invented the wearing of long dresses <note>original: "nyderkleyd." This refers to the introduction of floor-length garments to hide the legs.</note> for women.

¶ Chapter 3. Concerning Ops, page 4.
¶ Ops saved three sons from the death that their father Saturn had commanded for them. <note>In Roman mythology, Ops was a goddess of fertility and earth; she saved her children from being swallowed by Saturn.</note>

¶ Chapter 4. Concerning Juno, page 5.
¶ Juno was Jupiter's sister and wife, the wealthiest, and was called a goddess of heaven and honored above all goddesses; her cult <note>original: "jrzal," referring to the number of followers or the duration of her worship.</note> lasted long until after the birth of Christ.

¶ Chapter 5. Concerning Ceres, page 6.
¶ Ceres found agriculture in Sicily, with all its accessories, such as milling and baking.

¶ Chapter 6. Concerning Minerva, page 8.
¶ Minerva or Pallas kept her eternal chastity. She found how to make olive oil <note>original: "bomöl," literally "tree-oil."</note>. She found how to use sheep’s wool. She found chariot-driving, she found weapons, armor, and the order of battle. She found numbers. She found pipes <note>original: "pfeiffen," likely referring to flutes or wind instruments.</note>; a beautiful description of her form, signifying wisdom; she was honored as a goddess.

¶ Chapter 7. Concerning Venus, page 9.
¶ Venus had many husbands. She invented brothels <note>original: "frauenhewser," literally "women-houses."</note> and forced honorable women into them, and arranged for young women to be given to guests.

<margin>Chapter / Incunable / 828-</margin>

<margin>Boccaccio</margin>

<note>A circular library stamp at the bottom center reads "BIBLIOTHECA REGIA MONACENSIS," indicating this volume belonged to the Royal Library of Munich (now the Bavarian State Library).</note>

<insert>Hain * 3335</insert> <note>This is a handwritten bibliographic reference to Ludwig Hain’s "Repertorium bibliographicum," a standard catalog used to identify books printed before 1501 (incunables).</note>

[Small letter at the bottom right of the text block]
a

<summary>This page contains the table of contents for Heinrich Steinhöwel's German translation of Boccaccio's biographies of famous women, summarizing the lives of Eve through Venus. It serves as a medieval moral and historical guide, attributing various cultural inventions and social structures to these figures.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Heinrich Steinhöwel, Eve, Semiramis, Ops, Juno, Ceres, Minerva, Venus, mythology, inventions, agriculture, incunabula, Munich Royal Library</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents from the previous page, detailing the biographies of famous women from classical mythology and history, ranging from Chapter 8 to the beginning of Chapter 16.</meta>

¶ Chapter 8. Concerning Isis, page 11.
¶ Isis was violated by King Jupiter and sent to Egypt; there she was chosen as queen because she found the people wild and beast-like and taught them a human way of life. She discovered the first Egyptian letters. <note>Isis is depicted here as a "culture hero" who brings civilization and literacy to a primitive population.</note>

¶ Chapter 9. Concerning Europa, page 13.
¶ Europa was led astray by Jupiter. A lesson for young women to guard themselves against many roamers who wander here and there according to their own free will. <note>original: "hin vnnd her webern." This refers to predatory men or seducers who travel about without social restraint.</note>

¶ Chapter 10. Concerning Libya, page 13.
¶ Libya ruled so well that the entire kingdom of Libya kept its name from her.

¶ Chapter 11. Concerning Marsepia, page 14.
¶ Marsepia and Lampedo were warlike queens in Amazonia. How the rule of women had its origin. <note>Marsepia and Lampedo were legendary sisters who co-ruled the Amazons, a tribe of female warriors.</note>

¶ Chapter 12. Concerning Thisbe, page 15.
¶ Thisbe fell upon a sword because of her beloved Pyramus. <note>original: "priamo." The text likely contains a typesetter's error, confusing Pyramus with Priam, the King of Troy.</note>

¶ Chapter 13. Concerning Hypermnestra, page 18.
¶ Hypermnestra kept her husband Lynceus <note>original: "liniū."</note> alive when her forty-nine sisters murdered all their husbands. <note>In the myth of the Danaïdes, fifty sisters were ordered to kill their husbands on their wedding night; Hypermnestra was the only one who refused.</note>

¶ Chapter 14. Concerning Niobe, page 20.
¶ Niobe, in her pride, wished to be honored above the gods; for that reason her thirteen children died, and her husband Amphion killed himself out of sorrow. <note>original: "hochfart," meaning excessive pride or hubris.</note>

¶ Chapter 15. Concerning Hypsipyle, page 21.
¶ Hypsipyle saved her father when all the men were killed by the women. She was therefore driven into exile. <note>original: "ellend." While this word means "misery" today, in the 15th century it specifically referred to being "out of the land" or in exile.</note> A lesson on the kindness of children toward their fathers.

¶ Chapter 16. Concerning Medea, page 23.
¶ Medea was the greatest sorceress. She helped Jason... <note>The text breaks off here at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page continues the summary list of Boccaccio's biographies, focusing on classical figures like Isis, the Amazons, and Niobe, often framing their stories as moral lessons for the reader.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, Europa, Libya, Amazons, Thisbe, Hypermnestra, Niobe, Hypsipyle, Medea, Heinrich Steinhöwel, mythology, moral education, female rulers</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents and summaries of famous women. It concludes the entry for Medea (Chapter 16) and provides summaries for Chapters 17 through 21, covering figures such as Arachne, Orithyia, the Erythraean Sibyl, Medusa, and Iole.</meta>

...[to help Jason] win the golden fleece <note>original: "guldin schepfer." While "schepfer" literally means "creator," in this context it refers to the Golden Fleece (Goldenes Vlies) which Medea helped Jason steal from her father, King Aeetes.</note> from her father. She had her brother hacked into many pieces <note>According to myth, Medea killed her brother Absyrtus and scattered his limbs to delay her father’s pursuit as she fled with Jason.</note>. She killed Jason's wife and child. A good lesson for women to rule their eyes <note>original: "au gen tze regieren." This is a moral warning to women to control their desires and not be led astray by the sight of handsome strangers.</note>.

¶ Chapter 17. Concerning Arachne, page 25.
¶ Arachne discovered how to weave linen and knit nets. She was a master weaver. She hanged herself out of pride <note>original: "übermüt." Arachne famously challenged the goddess Minerva (Pallas) to a weaving contest; depending on the version of the myth, her suicide was a result of the goddess's wrath or Arachne's own despair at being surpassed.</note>, because she was overcome by Pallas.

¶ Chapter 18. Concerning Orithyia, page 26.
¶ Orithyia, on account of her warlike hands, was compared to Hercules <note>original: "herculi." Orithyia was an Amazon queen whose military prowess was said to rival that of the greatest male heroes.</note>.

¶ Chapter 19. Concerning Erythraea, page 27.
¶ Erythraea, or Eriphyla, was a Sibyl and prophesied many things <note>The text conflates the Erythraean Sibyl, a famous prophetess, with Eriphyle, who in mythology was the wife of Amphiaraus.</note>.

¶ Chapter 20. Concerning Medusa, page 27.
¶ Medusa became wealthy through her beauty and wisdom; yet Perseus took her riches away from her <note>This summary follows a "euhemeristic" tradition, which reinterprets myths as historical events. Here, Medusa is not a monster who turns men to stone, but a wealthy queen whose kingdom was conquered by Perseus.</note>.

¶ Chapter 21. Concerning Iole, page 29.
¶ Iole tamed and feminized the strongest Her... <note>Hercules. The text likely continues on the next page to describe how Iole (often confused with Omphale) forced the mighty Hercules to wear women's clothing and spin wool as a sign of his devotion to her.</note>

<summary>This page lists biographies of legendary women including Medea, Arachne, and Medusa, focusing on their skills, downfalls, and historical reinterpretations of their myths.</summary>
<keywords>Medea, Arachne, Orithyia, Sibyl, Medusa, Iole, Hercules, weaving, Golden Fleece, pride</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents and summaries of famous women, following the entry for Medusa. It covers chapters 26 through 35, focusing primarily on figures from the Trojan War and Greek mythology, such as Penthesilea, Cassandra, and Helen of Troy.</meta>

¶ Chapter 26. Concerning Procris, page 37.
¶ Procris’s utmost constancy was weakened by gold, and she was accidentally shot by her loving husband <note>Procris was the wife of Cephalus. According to the myth, Cephalus tested her fidelity by approaching her in disguise and offering her gold, which she eventually accepted. Later, while spying on him in the woods out of jealousy, he mistook her for a wild animal and killed her with a magical spear.</note>.

¶ Chapter 27. Concerning Argia, page 29.
¶ Argia showed exceptional love to her dead husband <note>Argia was the wife of Polynices. After her husband was killed in the war of the Seven Against Thebes, she defied the tyrant Creon’s orders to give him a proper burial, an act of extreme devotion that led to her own death.</note>.

¶ Chapter 28. Concerning Manto, page 40.
¶ Manto was a great prophetess through God. Mantua <note>The city of Mantua in Italy.</note> took its name from her.

¶ Chapter 29. Concerning the wives of the Minyans, page 41.
¶ The wives of the Minyans <note>original: "Menier weiben." This refers to the wives of the descendants of the Argonauts.</note> took the place of their husbands who were sentenced to death. A beautiful lesson of true love <note>When their husbands were imprisoned and sentenced to death by the Spartans, these women visited the prison, swapped clothes with their husbands, and remained in the cells so the men could escape.</note>.

¶ Chapter 30. Concerning Penthesilea, page 43.
¶ Penthesilea, queen of the women <note>The Amazons.</note>, invented the battle-axe and fought manfully against the Greeks to please Hector.

¶ Chapter 31. Concerning Polyxena, page 44.
¶ Polyxena was beheaded by Neoptolemus on the grave of his father, Achilles <note>In the aftermath of the Trojan War, the ghost of Achilles demanded the sacrifice of King Priam's daughter, Polyxena, to appease his spirit.</note>.

¶ Chapter 32. Concerning Hecuba, page 45.
¶ Hecuba was driven to madness by her misfortune, and the loss of her husband, her children, and the city of Troy <note>Hecuba, the Queen of Troy, witnessed the death of almost all her children and her husband, Priam. Legend says her grief was so great that she eventually turned into a dog.</note>.

¶ Chapter 33. Concerning Cassandra, page 46.
¶ Cassandra was strangled by Clytemnestra because of her prophecy <note>Cassandra was a prophetess cursed never to be believed. After the fall of Troy, she was taken as a war prize by Agamemnon and was murdered by his wife, Clytemnestra, upon their arrival in Mycenae.</note>.

¶ Chapter 34. Concerning Clytemnestra, page 47.
¶ Clytemnestra had her husband murdered, for which she and the murderer were most severely killed by her son <note>Clytemnestra and her lover Aegisthus murdered King Agamemnon. In revenge, her son Orestes killed them both.</note>.

¶ Chapter 35. Concerning Helen, page 48.
¶ Helen was abducted from the Greeks by Paris, and for that reason Troy was destroyed.

<summary>This page lists summaries for Chapters 26 through 35 of a biographical collection, focusing on the virtues and tragedies of women like Procris, Penthesilea, and the women of the Trojan War.</summary>
<keywords>Procris, Argia, Manto, Minyans, Penthesilea, Polyxena, Hecuba, Cassandra, Clytemnestra, Helen of Troy, Trojan War, fidelity, prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents and chapter summaries for Boccaccio's "Concerning Famous Women." It follows the previous page which concluded with the death of Polyxena (Chapter 31). This section spans Chapters 36 through 46.</meta>

3

¶ Chapter 36. Concerning Circe, page 51.
¶ Circe transformed men into irrational animals through sorcery, as the fables say <note>Circe is the famous enchantress from Homer's *Odyssey* who turned Odysseus's crew into swine; Boccaccio uses her story to warn against the "beastly" nature of lust.</note>.

¶ Chapter 37. Concerning Camilla, page 52.
¶ Camilla was driven out with her father and later restored. She preserved her purity and fought against the Trojans. A lesson on the discipline of young maidens <note>Camilla was a legendary Volscian warrior princess who appears in Virgil's *Aeneid*. She was famous for her speed and her dedication to the goddess Diana.</note>.

¶ Chapter 38. Concerning Penelope, page 54.
¶ Penelope maintained her holy state of widowhood in constancy for her husband Ulysses, waiting twenty years <note>While Ulysses (Odysseus) was away at the Trojan War and on his long journey home, Penelope famously fended off suitors to remain faithful to him.</note>.

¶ Chapter 39. Concerning Lavinia, page 56.
¶ Lavinia fled into a forest with her stepson and there gave birth to Aeneas Silvius <note>Lavinia was the wife of Aeneas. In some versions of the myth, she fled into the woods after Aeneas's death to escape her stepson Ascanius, though they were later reconciled.</note>.

¶ Chapter 40. Concerning Dido, page 57.
¶ Dido founded the city of Carthage. She maintained a pure state of widowhood until her death. A beautiful lesson in praise of the widow's estate <note>original: "wittwenstul" — literally "widow's chair," referring to the status or dignity of a widow. Boccaccio follows the historical tradition that Dido committed suicide to remain faithful to her dead husband rather than the Virgilian tradition where she dies for love of Aeneas.</note>.

¶ Chapter 41. Concerning Nicaula, page 63.
¶ Nicaula was the wisest in the arts and the wealthiest; a queen of the farthest land of the Moors <note>Nicaula is the name Boccaccio and other medieval authors used for the Queen of Sheba, who visited King Solomon.</note>.

¶ Chapter 42. Concerning Pamphile, page 63.
¶ Pamphile was the first to discover the use of cotton <note>original: "baumwol." Pamphile of Cos is credited in antiquity with the invention of silk weaving, though the German translator uses the term for cotton.</note>.

¶ Chapter 43. Concerning Rhea Ilia, page 63.
¶ Rhea Ilia was a nun and bore Remus and Romulus, the founders of the city of Rome, for which she was killed. A beautiful lesson concerning nuns <note>original: "kloster fraw" — literally "cloistered woman." Rhea Ilia was a Vestal Virgin, a priestess of Rome. The text uses "nun" as a contemporary equivalent for a woman dedicated to a religious life and vowed to chastity.</note>.

¶ Chapter 44. Concerning Gaia Cyrilla, page 65.
¶ Gaia Cyrilla was the most domestic and virtuous queen <note>Also known as Tanaquil, the wife of Tarquinius Priscus. She was held up as a model of domestic skill and spinning.</note>.

¶ Chapter 45. Concerning Sappho, page 65.
¶ Sappho was the most learned in Latin poems and all stringed instruments <note>original: "lateinischen gedichten." While Sappho was a Greek lyric poet from Lesbos, the text attributes Latin poetry to her, likely as a synonym for "classical" or "learned" verse in the translator's era.</note>.

¶ Chapter 46. Concerning Tullia, page 66.
¶ Tullia had her husband, sister, and father killed, and drove a wagon over him so that she was splattered with his blood <note>Tullia Minor was the daughter of Servius Tullius, the sixth king of Rome. Her "wickedness" is a standard historical example of filial impiety.</note>.

<summary>This page lists chapters 36 to 46 of the table of contents, summarizing the lives of legendary and historical women including Circe, Penelope, Dido, and Sappho. Each entry provides a brief moral or biographical takeaway, ranging from the domestic virtues of Gaia Cyrilla to the horrific crimes of Tullia.</summary>
<keywords>Circe, Penelope, Dido, Nicaula, Queen of Sheba, Rhea Ilia, Sappho, Tullia, female virtue, widowhood, chastity, mythology, Roman history</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents and chapter summaries for Boccaccio’s "Concerning Famous Women." It follows the previous page which covered Chapters 36 through 46. This section spans Chapters 47 through 53, though it includes a specific editorial correction regarding a numbering error in the original manuscript.</meta>

¶ Chapter 47. Concerning Lucretia, page 69.
¶ Lucretia killed herself in the presence of her father, husband, and friends so that she might exonerate herself from the forced adultery <note>Lucretia was a legendary Roman noblewoman whose suicide after being raped by the king's son sparked the revolution that overthrew the Roman monarchy and established the Republic.</note>.

¶ Chapter 48. Concerning Tomyris, page 71.
¶ Tomyris felled the most powerful emperor, Cyrus, in battle <note>Queen Tomyris of the Massagetae is famous for defeating Cyrus the Great in the 6th century BCE; legend says she dipped his severed head in a vessel of blood so he could finally "drink his fill."</note>.

¶ Chapter 49. Concerning Leena, page 74.
¶ Leena the great prostitute <note>original: "hůr" — In a historical context, Leena was a *hetaira* (courtesan) in ancient Athens.</note> bit off her own tongue so that she might not reveal the secrets of her lover <note>Leena was associated with the "Tyrannicides" who plotted against the Athenian ruling family; she endured torture without speaking by biting off her tongue so she could not physically betray her friends.</note>.

¶ Chapter 50. Concerning Athaliah, page 74.
¶ Athaliah, out of greed to rule without opposition, had all of King David's royal lineage killed. A lesson <note>original: "lee" (Lehre)</note> to despise [usurped] power and a sharp rebuke of the biting rule of lords <note>Athaliah was a Queen of Judah in the 9th century BCE; she is depicted in the Bible as a usurper who attempted to eliminate the house of David to secure her own power.</note>.

->¶ Chapter—again—50, though it should be 51. This happened by mistake; therefore, take note of it, because the count of women is still one hundred. This is the second [chapter] 50, concerning Cloelia, page 76, and the count will be restored by the time we reach Irene.<-

¶ Cloelia led her companions on a workhorse across the terrifying river of the Tiber to free them from captivity <note>Cloelia was a Roman hostage who escaped the Etruscan camp by swimming across the Tiber, leading a group of other young women to safety; Boccaccio specifies a "workhorse" here to emphasize the unglamorous, practical nature of her escape.</note>.

¶ Chapter 51. Concerning Hippo, page 77.
¶ Hippo jumped into the sea to preserve her chastity from the kidnappers <note>Hippo was a Greek woman captured by a fleet; she committed suicide by jumping overboard to avoid being raped by the sailors.</note>.

¶ Chapter 52. Concerning Veturia, page 78.
¶ Veturia saved Rome, for which reason all women were granted great freedoms, who before were little honored by the men <note>Veturia was the mother of the Roman general Coriolanus; her pleas successfully dissuaded him from attacking his own city, Rome.</note>.

¶ Chapter 53. Concerning Megullia—this is misplaced;
you will find it on page 84.
¶ Megullia was called *Dotata* <term>Dotata: "The Endowed One" from the Latin *dos* (dowry).</term>, which means well-gifted, because of her large dowry, which however would be considered small in these times <note>Megullia Dotata was an early Roman woman notable because her dowry was exceptionally large for the era, highlighting a historical shift in Roman economic and social customs.</note>.

<summary>This page provides summaries for chapters 47 through 53 of Boccaccio's "Of Famous Women," focusing on themes of female sacrifice, political power, and Roman virtue. It includes a notable printer's or scribe's note correcting a numbering error in the chapter sequence.</summary>
<keywords>Lucretia, Tomyris, Leena, Athaliah, Cloelia, Veturia, Megullia Dotata, Roman history, female virtue, dowry, manuscript errors</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the table of contents and chapter summaries for Boccaccio’s "Concerning Famous Women." It picks up at Chapter 54, indicating a skip in the numbering or a missing leaf from the previous section (which ended with Chapter 50/51). It covers notable women from antiquity, focusing on female artists, scholars, and figures of Roman virtue or notoriety.</meta>

[Page number in top right corner: 4]

¶ Chapter 54. Concerning **Thamyris**, page 80.
¶ Thamyris, a nun <note>original: "kloster fraw" – The 15th-century translator uses contemporary Christian terminology to describe Thamyris (also known as Timarete), an ancient Greek painter who remained a virgin.</note>, was the best painter.

¶ Chapter 55. Concerning **Artemisia**, page 80.
¶ Artemisia drank the ashes of her dead husband and had a tomb built for him; one of the seven wonders of the world <note>Artemisia II of Caria was so devoted to her husband, Mausolus, that she reportedly mixed his ashes into her daily drink and commissioned the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus.</note>.

¶ Chapter 56. Concerning **Virginia**, page 84; and you will find the proper image belonging to this on page 88.
¶ Virginia was killed by her father because of the false judgment of the wicked judges <note>This refers to the Roman legend of Verginia, whose father stabbed her to death to "save" her from being legally enslaved by the corrupt official Appius Claudius.</note>.

¶ Chapter 58. Concerning **Irene**, page 86.
¶ Irene was the best painter <note>Irene was an ancient Greek artist mentioned by Pliny the Elder; Boccaccio highlights her as a symbol of female intellectual and artistic capability.</note>.

¶ Chapter 59. Concerning **Leontium**, page 86.
¶ Leontium was the most learned woman; she would have been even greater had she not stained her skill with unchastity <note>Leontium was a philosopher in the garden of Epicurus. Ancient sources often slandered her reputation due to her presence in male-dominated philosophical circles.</note>.

¶ Chapter 60. Concerning **Olympias**, page 86.
¶ Olympias gave birth to Alexander through adultery. She was driven out and reinstated, but then captured and killed <note>Olympias was the mother of Alexander the Great. Medieval traditions often claimed Alexander was fathered by a god or a sorcerer rather than King Philip of Macedon.</note>.

¶ Chapter 61. Concerning **Claudia**, page 88.
¶ Claudia, a nun <note>Refers to Claudia the Vestal Virgin.</note>, killed her father <note>The text here says "töttet" (killed), though historical accounts describe her as *saving* her father from a tribune; this may be a transcription error or a specific editorial choice in this edition.</note>.

¶ Chapter 62. Concerning **Virginia, daughter of Aulus**, page 88; and you will find the proper image belonging to this on page 84.
¶ Virginia <note>original: "vgine alucij" – Virginia, daughter of Aulus (Alucius). This is a different figure from the Virginia in Chapter 56.</note> established a temple to feminine modesty and honor.

¶ Chapter 63. Concerning **Flora**, page 89.
¶ Flora the rich harlot <note>original: "hūr"</note> established a shameful festival <note>Flora was a legendary Roman courtesan whose wealth allegedly funded the "Floralia," a spring festival that Boccaccio describes with moral disapproval.</note>.

¶ Chapter 64. Concerning **The Roman Woman**, page 91.
¶ The Roman woman breastfed her mother, who had been sentenced to death by starvation <note>A famous example of filial piety known as "Roman Charity." In some versions of the legend, the prisoner is her father; here, Boccaccio specifies it is her mother.</note>.

¶ Chapter 65. Concerning **Marcia**, page 92.
¶ Marcia Varronis, the eternally pure nun <note>Refers to Marcia, daughter of Varro, an ancient artist who remained unmarried.</note>, was the best sculptor and painter.

¶ Chapter 66. Concerning **Sulpicia**, page 93.
¶ Sulpicia was recognized as the most venerable of all Roman women; a lesson in the honor of women <note>Sulpicia was chosen as the most virtuous woman in Rome to dedicate a statue to Venus Verticordia.</note>.

<summary>This page lists summaries for chapters 54 through 66 of a German edition of Boccaccio's "Of Famous Women," focusing on themes of artistic skill, chastity, and civic virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Thamyris, Artemisia, Virginia, Leontium, Olympias, Claudia, Flora, Roman Charity, Marcia Varronis, Sulpicia, female painters, modesty, Roman history</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents and chapter summaries for the German translation of Boccaccio’s "Concerning Famous Women." It skips from the previous page's Chapter 59 to Chapter 67, highlighting various women known for their loyalty, tragic deaths, or resistance to slavery.</meta>

¶ Chapter 67. Concerning **Harmonia**, page 94.
¶ Harmonia offered herself up to death in loyalty, just as her maiden had done for her <note>Harmonia was a Sicilian princess; her nurse/maiden dressed in the princess's clothes to be killed in her stead, and Harmonia, moved by this sacrifice, revealed herself to the assassins to share her servant's fate.</note>.

¶ Chapter 68. Concerning **Busa of Canosa**, page 95.
¶ Busa, out of generosity, provided food and provisions for ten thousand men <note>Paulina Busa was a noblewoman of Canusium who fed and clothed the Roman survivors of the Battle of Cannae.</note>.

¶ Chapter 69. Concerning **Sophonisba**, page 96.
¶ Sophonisba's morning gift <note>original: "morgengab" – a traditional Germanic wedding gift from a husband to his bride, here used ironically to describe the poison sent to her.</note> was a poisoned drink. She drank it willingly and without fear.

¶ Chapter 70. Concerning **Theoxena**, page 98.
¶ Theoxena killed her children, herself, and her husband before she would allow them to enter into slavery <note>original: "eygenschafft" – the state of being owned as property; bondage.</note>.

¶ Chapter 71. Concerning **Berenice**, page 100.
¶ Berenice killed the murderers of her children with her own hand.

¶ Chapter 72. Concerning the wife of Orgiagon [**Chiomara**], page 101.
¶ The woman Chiomara <note>original: "drigiagontis" – likely a corruption of "Orgiagontis," the name of her husband Orgiagon.</note> cleansed her body and soul with the head of her violator <note>Chiomara was a Galatian noblewoman who was raped by a Roman centurion; she later had him killed and brought his severed head to her husband to prove her honor was restored.</note>.

¶ Chapter 73. Concerning **Tertia Aemilia**, page 102.
¶ Tertia Aemilia was patient regarding the adultery of her husband.

¶ Chapter 74. Concerning **Claudia Quinta**, page 104.
¶ Claudia Quinta went out with all the noblest Roman women as the most slandered, and returned home as the most famous/reputable <note>Claudia Quinta was a Roman matron accused of unchastity who allegedly proved her innocence by miraculously pulling a grounded ship carrying the statue of the goddess Cybele.</note>.

¶ Chapter 75. Concerning **Hypsicratea**, page 105.
¶ Hypsicratea was killed by her husband <note>Note: Historical and Boccaccian accounts usually focus on Hypsicratea's extreme devotion to King Mithridates VI, following him into exile; the German summary here implies a tragic end at his hands.</note> to whom she had previously shown great loyalty.

¶ Chapter 76. Concerning **Sempronia**, page 106.
¶ Sempronia, the most learned, most eloquent, and most unchaste, was killed.

¶ Chapter 77. Concerning the women of the **Cimbri**, who are the **Kurwalhen** <note>original: "kurwalhen" – an archaic German term for Celtic or Romance-speaking peoples; the translator is equating the ancient Cimbri with a group familiar to his 15th-century audience.</note>, page 107.
¶ The Cimbrian women stabbed their children to death and hanged themselves before they would allow themselves to be taken into slavery.

¶ Chapter 78. Concerning **Julia**, page 108.
¶ Julia died of shock at the sight of her husband's bloody clothes <note>Julia, the daughter of Julius Caesar, died in childbirth shortly after seeing the blood-stained toga of her husband, Pompey the Great.</note>.

¶ Chapter 79. Concerning **Porcia**, daughter of Cato, page 109.

¶ Chapter 80. Concerning **Hortensia**, page 110.
¶ Hortensia was the spokeswoman for the women before the three men <note>Hortensia was an orator who famously argued against the taxation of wealthy Roman women before the Second Triumvirate—Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus—in 42 BCE.</note>.

¶ Chapter 81. Concerning **Sulpicia**, page 111.
¶ Sulpicia followed her husband into exile <note>original: "ellende" – literally "other-land," meaning misery or exile. Sulpicia followed her husband, Lentulus Cruscellio, when he was proscribed and fled to Sicily.</note>.

¶ Chapter 82. Concerning **Mariamne**, page 111.
¶ Mariamne, the most beautiful, was killed without guilt by her husband **Herod**.

¶ Chapter 84. Concerning **Antonia**, page 117.

¶ Chapter 85. Concerning **Agrippina**, page 117.

¶ Chapter 86. Concerning **Paulina**, page 119.
¶ Paulina coupled with a youth, believing he was a god <note>This refers to the story of Paulina, a Roman matron who was deceived by a man named Mundus into thinking the Egyptian god Anubis wished to lie with her.</note>.

¶ Chapter 87. Concerning **Agrippina**, mother of Nero, page 120.
¶ Agrippina, the mother of Nero, killed her kinsman and husband and took **Claudius**. She brought her son to the empire; she committed many murders; she was killed by her son.

¶ Chapter 88. Concerning **Epicharis**, page 123.
¶ Epicharis, after much torture, hanged herself rather than give up the conspirators against Nero <note>original: "geschwornen" – literally "those who have sworn," referring to the Pisonian conspiracy to assassinate the Emperor Nero.</note>.

¶ Chapter 89. Concerning **Pompeia Paulina**, page 124.
¶ Pompeia Paulina wanted to die with her husband through bloodletting when Nero had him killed <note>original: "aderlassen" – the opening of veins. When Nero ordered the philosopher Seneca to commit suicide, his wife Paulina attempted to die with him by the same method.</note>.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the table of contents found on the previous pages to the formal dedication of the book. The author, Heinrich Steinhöwel, is addressing his patron, Archduchess Eleanor of Austria, explaining his motivations for translating Boccaccio’s work from Latin into German.</meta>

[Handwritten page number: 6]

->**To the most illustrious Princess and Lady, Lady Eleanor, Duchess of Austria, etc., his most gracious Lady, Heinrich Steinhöwel of Weil an der Würm, doctor of medicine, offers himself in all humble service.**<-

### Most illustrious Princess,

As I was not at home this past year for notable reasons <note>Steinhöwel may be referring to a period of travel or perhaps a retreat from public life due to the plague or political unrest, though he does not specify the "notable reasons" here.</note> and was almost entirely relieved of the cares of medical practice <note>original: "erczneyen" – refers to the practice of medicine. Steinhöwel was a prominent physician in Ulm.</note>, I thought not to spend my time in total idleness. And I took it upon myself, for the praise and honor of your Grace and all women, to translate into German <note>original: "zeteütſchende" – literally "to Germanize." This reflects the 15th-century movement to make Latin humanist texts accessible to those who could only read the vernacular.</note> the little book of **Giovanni Boccaccio** <note>The famous 14th-century Italian author of the *Decameron*.</note> regarding the **ingenious and illustrious women** who were placed into eternal memory by the ancient chroniclers because of their extraordinary undertakings.

Yet they are not all alike; for some are included because of their ingenious good works and histories, so that the female sex might wish to follow them; others are included because of their shameful misdeeds, so that their actions might be shunned.

Since I had previously considered dedicating this little book to your Grace—so that it would not lie idle with me but, with the help of a favorable escort, might travel more safely into the world and become more common—for good things, as **Aristotle** writes, become better the more common they are <note>A common medieval paraphrase of a principle found in Aristotle’s *Topics* or *Nicomachean Ethics*, suggesting that the wider a good is shared, the more its value increases.</note>. And I also found that it should be sent neither to princes nor lords, but—since it speaks only of women—to a discerning, God-fearing, and just female judge in all matters. I have recognized you, most illustrious Princess, through common renown, as such a person and as a lover of all good arts and artists.

Therefore, I wished to commend this, my diligent work concerning the **ninety-nine illustrious women**, to your Grace’s higher judgment, as a truly strict judge... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "richt," likely intended to be "richter" (judge) or "richtung" (direction/judgment).</note>

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Heinrich Steinhöwel’s translation of Boccaccio’s "Of Famous Women" to Archduchess Eleanor of Austria, explaining that he translated the work to avoid idleness and to provide moral examples for women.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Steinhöwel, Eleanor of Austria, Giovanni Boccaccio, translation, humanism, moral education, female role models, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Heinrich Steinhöwel to Archduchess Eleanor of Austria. Steinhöwel justifies his translation of Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" by explaining how readers should extract moral lessons even from the descriptions of shameful or "impure" women, using metaphors of roses among thorns and bees gathering honey.</meta>

to recognize and judge whether it be worthy to circulate in the world to exercise the minds of men, which is of great benefit. Humbly praying, by the holy marriage in which you are highly praised above other women, that you receive this my little book kindly and hear it before passing judgment. For I do not doubt that the virtuous, praiseworthy, and strong deeds of many women described therein will bring joy to your spirit. The impure deeds will cause wonder and serve as a warning to shun such things and cast them aside, in the same way that when one wishes to pluck roses, even though the thorns stand around them, they are nonetheless set aside and the roses are plucked.

The bees also do not suck from all the flowers to which they fly. Nor do they consume any flower entirely; rather, once they have sucked from them what is useful for honey, they leave the rest unhurt. In such a manner, **Saint Basil** <note>Saint Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD), a Church Father who wrote a famous address to young men on how to derive benefit from pagan Greek literature by being like a bee, taking the good and leaving the bad.</note> teaches us to read such books that are mixed with good and shameful deeds: to read them so that we pick the roses from the thorns and the honey from the flowers. That is to say, we should pick out what serves us toward virtuous deeds, leaving the rest to lie in contempt.

If I should ever write words that are coarser than your Grace’s purity is accustomed to speaking or hearing, I beg you to take it in the best light. For those same words could not be replaced with circumlocution <note>original: "vmbreden" – speaking around a subject or using euphemisms.</note> without changing the true meaning. Therefore, and especially so that the words are not counted as impure where evil deeds are not present, I have occasionally—with the permission of pure women—set down "harlot" <note>original: "hür" – Steinhöwel is apologizing for using blunt or "coarse" language, arguing that calling a vice by its true name is necessary for moral clarity.</note> so that the true meaning remains unchanged, as has been written regarding the women **Flora**, **Leena**, and several others. Their unchaste deeds were so impure that it would be improper to obscure them with flowery speech; rather, they must be spoken of despicably to shame the deed.

<summary>Steinhöwel explains his "bee and rose" method of reading, urging the Archduchess to overlook coarse language and shameful histories in favor of the moral lessons they provide.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Steinhöwel, Archduchess Eleanor, Boccaccio, Saint Basil, moral education, translation theory, Flora, Leena, virtue vs. vice</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Heinrich Steinhöwel’s dedication of his translation of Boccaccio’s "Famous Women" to Archduchess Eleanor of Austria. The previous page ended with a justification for using "coarse" language to describe the vices of certain women so that they might be properly identified and avoided.</meta>

[7]

be named, and also so that feminine purity might all the more quickly protect itself against the base deeds associated with such names. For just as feminine dignity is raised up in the spirit through virtuous deeds, so too is it weighed down by wrongdoing. And it would indeed be fitting, for the sake of human order and living well, that the virtuous deeds of women—those special gifts of nature or fortune <note>original: "geselles," likely referring to bestowed circumstances</note> which are worthy of memory—should be recorded for every woman for the benefit of those to come, so that they may follow the good and avoid the wicked. 

As I have written about the ninety-nine women, there is one who holds the hundredth place, who is rightly the crown of feminine honor in our time, and who encompasses all the virtuous deeds of women. Her praise for all her good gifts has resounded so far and wide in the world that she should rightly be placed in eternal memory. Why this has not yet happened up to this time, I believe, is only because those same deeds multiply and accumulate daily without ceasing, so noticeably that I think they are being saved up to be gathered together. 

Yet, to conclude this little book, it would be enough for me if I described these gifts: how she was born of noble birth, an illustrious princess <term>künigi: literally 'queen,' though here used as a title for the high-born Archduchess</term> from King James of Scotland <note>James I of Scotland (1394–1437)</note> and his consort, a Queen of England <note>Joan Beaufort (c. 1404–1445)</note>. She is armed with a Red Lion <note>The Royal Arms of Scotland features a red lion rampant</note>, signifying her fervent, courageous love for God and her husband, and marked with crowned constancy represented by the blue crown. In addition, fortune has given her a shared coat of arms, which is called "Old Austria": five flying larks <note>original: "lerchen." These are actually the five eagles of the medieval coat of arms of Lower Austria (the Lerchenwappen), which were often historically mistaken for larks</note> on a blue field, to signify how she always exercises herself through her five senses in high reason.

<summary>Steinhöwel concludes his dedication by praising his patron, Eleanor of Austria, daughter of the King of Scotland, framing her as the ultimate "hundredth" example of virtue to complete Boccaccio’s list of famous women.</summary>
<keywords>Eleanor of Austria, James I of Scotland, heraldry, Red Lion, Old Austria, Lerchenwappen, feminine virtue, dedication, Boccaccio</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Archduchess Eleanor of Austria. Having just mentioned her noble Scottish lineage and the "Red Lion" of her family arms, the author now transitions to praising her character and her husband, Duke Sigmund of Austria.</meta>

and as the lark flies up toward heaven, observe how she performs virtuous works in mercy and kindness; to which end, however, good fortune has helped her through the bestowal of a high-born and agreeable consort, Duke Sigmund of Austria, etc., my gracious lord. He is highly regarded above others with special gifts from Divine Omnipotence. For in strength of body, in practice and agility, few shall be found to be his equal. But in the divine gifts of his mind and reason, with laudable and mastered moderation; with the maintenance and increase of God's adornment and service in all of God's houses; with the engraving of the word of God, *pax vobis* <note>original: "pax vobis," Latin for "Peace be with you"</note>—that is, "peace be unto you"—which he, following God, constantly endeavors to uphold. And through the friendly, free governance of those bound to him, to whom a free course is granted out of the innate goodness of the high house of Austria, with the constant utterance of well-considered words, the truth, without any wavering, is praised above all letters and seals. 

But if he were to be praised throughout his land by his knighthood, nobility, and the common man, time would slip away from me before I could recount the dutiful submission toward his Grace and the friendly love toward his people. Yet if great wealth made a person more blessed—though this was doubted by the ancient sages <note>A common philosophical theme in the Renaissance, questioning whether material wealth contributes to true happiness or "the good life."</note>—then he might well be esteemed the most blessed on account of his silver mines and the perfection of all metals. Through these, Count Laurentz of Tyrol <note>A reference to the legendary King Laurin, the dwarf king of Tyrolean folklore who ruled over a subterranean kingdom of treasure.</note>, whom they call "Strong Laurin" for his great wealth and power which his people dug out of the mountains (for which reason they were also called "earth-men" <term>erdmänlin: literally "little earth-men," referring here to the dwarfs of legend who were master miners</term>), was famed in like measure. All of this is given to your Grace through the bestowal of God and

<summary>In this section of the dedication, Steinhöwel praises the virtues and physical prowess of Duke Sigmund of Austria, Eleanor’s husband, while highlighting the immense wealth of the Tyrolean silver mines by comparing Sigmund to the legendary dwarf king Laurin.</summary>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication to Archduchess Eleanor of Austria. The previous page praised her husband, Duke Sigmund of Tyrol, for his governance and his silver mines. Here, the author shifts focus entirely to Eleanor, comparing her virtues to the great women of classical antiquity and positioning her as the "crown" or final entry in his collection of famous women.</meta>

<note>A metal clip is visible at the top center of the page, likely used during the digitization process.</note>

8

...the innate goodness of her high consort. Her holy life is rendered humble in order to preserve its true essence.

But if she were to be praised for her natural gifts, one would find that God has so endowed her with physical beauty that she equals **Mariamne** <note>Mariamne (c. 54–29 BCE) was the wife of Herod the Great, famous in the ancient world for her extraordinary beauty.</note> in loveliness; **Minerva** <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and strategic warfare.</note> in wisdom; and the noble **Sulpicia** <note>Sulpicia was a Roman poet and an aristocratic woman held up as a model of conjugal devotion and virtue.</note> in honor. In the laudable management of a princely court, she matches Queen **Gaia Caecilia** <note>Also known as Tanaquil, a legendary Roman queen and a traditional model of domestic skill and wifely duty.</note>; in the subtlety of her handiwork, she equals **Arachne** <note>A mythical weaver who was so skilled she challenged the goddess Minerva to a contest.</note>; and in the constancy of faithful marriage, she follows **Julia**, **Pompeia**, **Paulina**, **Argia**, and **Artemisia** <note>This list cites famous wives from Roman and Greek history noted for their loyalty: Julia (daughter of Caesar), Pompeia (wife of Caesar), Paulina (wife of Seneca), Argia (wife of Polynices), and Artemisia (who built the Mausoleum for her husband).</note>. In the pure gathering of maidenly discipline, honor, and the modest chastity of **Lucretia** <note>Lucretia was a legendary Roman heroine whose commitment to her virtue made her a symbol of the ideal woman; she was famously discovered spinning wool with her maids while other noblewomen were feasting.</note>, who was found carding wool in the midst of her handmaidens.

What more shall I say? A diligent observer of the laudable works of these same women—of which I lack the space to complete my full list—would find that there is no praise written of these ladies in which she does not also share a great part. 

Therefore, I have waited to conclude my little book with the described life of such a virtuous woman of our own time. Since, however, I have been disappointed in my hope <note>The author may be implying that he had hoped to wait for even more great deeds to record, or perhaps refers to a delay in the publication process.</note> until now, I did not want to let this little book of mine lie idle any longer, but rather to send it to Your Princely Grace, as was my original intention: as a crown to the hundred illustrious women whose further praise I have anticipated. 

For I do not doubt that, although you cannot be personally present in many places, this little book will spread the fame of your virtuous works far and wide, and inspire other women to follow your example and the laudable deeds of others described herein. And I pray Your High-Born Grace to accept this with the same good intention with which I have offered it, in the hope of being placed under your Grace’s protection and favor for all time. May God preserve you long in blessedness.

<summary>The author concludes his dedication to Archduchess Eleanor by comparing her to various classical heroines of wisdom, beauty, and domestic virtue, ultimately presenting her as the final "crowning" example in his book of famous women.</summary>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page begins the main body of the work, following the dedication and dating provided on the previous leaf. It introduces the first chapter, focusing on Eve, and establishes the author's intent to chronicle the lives of noble and illustrious women.</meta>

->The 1st leaf<-
->¶ The viper loses its venom through the power of the child-bearing maiden. <note>original: "Vipera vim perdit vi pariente puella"; this Latin verse draws a parallel between the serpent (the viper) who deceived Eve and the Virgin Mary (the maiden), whose child-birth restores what was lost.</note><-
->¶ Of Eve, Chapter One.<-

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden. They stand on either side of the Tree of Knowledge, around which a serpent with a human head is coiled. The serpent hands an apple to Eve, who in turn hands one to Adam. They are surrounded by a low brick wall, signifying the enclosed nature of Paradise.</note>

**S**ince I intend to write of illustrious deeds through which women are enlightened and henceforth notably ennobled, it is appropriate that a beginning be made with the mother of the human race. For just as Eve is the noblest mother, she was also most remarkably ennobled by the fact that she was not—like other mortal humans—born into this frail vale of tears <note>original: "jamertal"; a common late-medieval metaphor for the hardships and sorrows of earthly life in contrast to the peace of heaven.</note> for toil; nor was she forged with that same hammer and anvil <note>A metaphorical reference to the natural, often painful process of human procreation and birth, which Eve bypassed through her divine creation.</note>; nor did she come into life weeping for sin, nor weak <note>original: "blöde"; in this context meaning physically or mentally frail.</note> like the others. Instead—in a manner that has never occurred again—after the best Craftsman of all things <note>The "best craftsman" refers to God the Creator.</note> had formed Adam from a clod of earth with His own hand...

<summary>This opening page introduces the history of noble women, beginning with Eve. The author highlights Eve’s unique and divine creation, contrasting her birth with the suffering and toil of the rest of humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Eve, Adam, Creation, Garden of Eden, noble women, vale of tears, human origins</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the creation narrative of Eve, picking up from the description of Adam’s initial state in Paradise. It details her formation from Adam's rib, her divine beauty, and her eventual role in the Fall.</meta>

->The 1st leaf<-

...led into delight, and having fallen into a sweet sleep through the skill known to Him <note>Referring to God, the "best Craftsman" mentioned on the previous page.</note> alone, He drew from his side a compliant woman for the man—joyful from the delight of the place and the sight of her Creator, an immortal lady over all things, and now the spouse of the waking man, by whom she was also named Eve. What greater or more illustrious thing could ever happen to anyone born than this? Beyond that, we may well consider that she was of wondrous beauty; for what has ever been made by the finger of God <note>original: "finger gottes"; a biblical metaphor for divine action or direct creation.</note> that has not surpassed all other things in beauty? 

And although such beauty is perishable by time, and often diminishes in the midst of blooming age from a small illness, nevertheless it is counted among women and their foremost gifts, through which many a woman has received much honor from the unconsidered judgment of mortal men. So it is not superfluous that beauty is now set down as a foremost ornament to the praise and illustrious works of women, and will be mentioned often hereafter. 

Thereafter, as she became a citizen of Paradise by right of her origin and residence, and was surrounded with a radiance <note>original: "ſchein"; referring to a state of grace or divine light.</note> unknown to us, and with her husband eagerly enjoyed the delights of that same place, then the envious enemy of her blessedness <note>The Serpent or Satan.</note> poured a mischievous counsel into her mind: that if she transgressed the single commandment set for them by God, they might achieve greater glory. And as she, out of female levity <note>original: "leichtfertikeÿt"; a term often used in medieval literature to imply a supposed natural fickleness or lack of serious judgment in women.</note>, believed him more than was beneficial for her, and foolishly intended to climb higher, she—before all other things—made her husband pliable to her opinion through flattering endearments. And when they, in contempt of the commandment, tasted the apples of the tree of the knowledge of good...

## The 2nd leaf

and evil, they deceived themselves and all their race, [casting them] from future rest and eternity into anxious labor and wretched death, and from a delightful fatherland <note>Referring to the Garden of Eden.</note> into the midst of brambles, clods, and crags. For when the radiant light with which they were surrounded departed, they were punished by their Creator and covered with garments <note>original: "kosten"; likely referring to the tunics of skin mentioned in Genesis 3:21.</note>, driven out of the place of delight, and arrived wretchedly upon the level fields. There, the woman became renowned through her handiwork, for she was the first to discover the art of spinning, as is said by some; and her husband tilled the soil. There she would often feel the pains of childbirth and was many times anxiously grieved by the death of her children and her children's children, and was set in misery for many years. And even if I say nothing of the heat and the frost and other hardships, she nevertheless arrived, weary from labor, at a great and mortal old age.

## The 2nd leaf

**S**emiramis, noble and most ancient, was a queen of Assyria. But from where she descended, antiquity has carried away <note>The specific details of her lineage have been lost to history.</note>. Therefore, it was left to the ancients to say in the manner of tales that she was a daughter of Neptune, whom they claim to be a son of Saturn; and they say, out of their erring faith <note>The author characterizes pagan mythology as "erring" or false beliefs from a Christian perspective.</note>, that he is a god of the sea. And even if this is not to be believed, it is certainly to be thought that she was born of noble lineage. She was wedded to King Ninus of Assyria and bore him an only son named Ninyas. But after Ninus had made all of Asia and finally the Bactrians <note>original: "bacharos"; the people of Bactria, an ancient region in Central Asia.</note> subject to him, he died from the shot of an arrow while she was still young and her son was but a child. She thought that she could not safely entrust such a large and powerful Oriental empire to the tender age of her son. And she was so high-spirited <note>original: "grossmütig"; in this context, it suggests she was bold, ambitious, or possessed a "great spirit" beyond what was expected of her gender.</note> that she dared to take up the rule, through skill and wit, over the lands that the bold man had made subject with weapons and conquered with strength. For through womanly cunning and exceedingly great devised cleverness, she deceived the army of her dead husband. It was also no wonder, for Semiramis was very similar to her son in the appearance of her form; also, her womanly voice, on account of her age, did not sound different from his youthful one. There was also no or only a small difference of the body, except that she was a little taller. Through such help, and also so that nothing else in the future might occur to make her trickery manifest, she covered her head with a wrap <note>Likely a turban or veil.</note>, with hidden hands and with hidden feet. But since such clothing was uncommon for the Assyrians, she commanded all the people to wear such clothing, so that the innovation would not cause the inhabitants to wonder. Thus

<summary>This page describes how Queen Semiramis took control of the Assyrian Empire after the death of her husband, King Ninus, by disguising herself as her young son to ensure political stability.</summary>

# The 3rd leaf

Ninus’s former consort, in the likeness of the son, and the woman in the form of the child, was received into kingly majesty with wonderful diligence. Under feigned manhood, she maintained knightly discipline and wisdom, performing such works as were highly esteemed even by the strongest of men. Since she spared herself no labor and took no fright at any perilous things, and through her unheard-of deeds had overcome the envy of all, she was not ashamed to reveal to everyone who she truly was and how she had feigned these things through womanly cunning. It was as if she wished to show that the necessity for ruling lies not in manhood, but in a manly spirit <note>The author uses the phrase "manlichs gemütes" to suggest that leadership is a quality of the soul or intellect rather than a biological trait.</note>; and the more everyone wondered at this, the more that lady’s majesty was further glorified.

And to set forth her history more broadly: she not only protected the realm won by her husband with arms through her adopted manly spirit, but she also added to it the land of the Moors <note>original: "moren land"; likely referring to Ethiopia or the regions of North Africa as understood in the 15th century.</note>, which she had previously thoroughly conquered through war. Thereafter, she turned her powerful weapons toward India, a place where none before her husband had ever reached. Afterwards, she rebuilt the ancient work of Nimrod: Babylon. That city was powerful in those times in the region of Shinar <note>original: "senaar"; the biblical name for the region in Mesopotamia where Babylon was located.</note>. She enclosed it with walls made of baked stones, fixed with sand, resin, and other bitumens <note>original: "gummi"; in this architectural context, it refers to the asphalt or mineral pitch used as mortar in ancient Mesopotamia.</note>, so high, thick, and exceedingly long that it was a thing to be marveled at. 

And so that we do not withhold one story from among her many others that is well worthy of remembrance: it is said of her in truth that once she had established her affairs in peace and was taking her rest, one day she was having her hair combed with womanly diligence by her maidens and having braids woven from it according to the custom of the country, when it happened that she

# The 4th leaf

# The 5th leaf

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a man and two women standing within a landscape. Above the woman in the center is the label "JUNO." To the left, the scene includes a building, the sun, and a star. To the right, a moon with a human face is visible alongside more stars and a structure resembling a castle.</note>

Juno, a daughter of Saturn and Ops <note>original: "Saturni vnd opis"</note>, according to the poems of the poets and by the tradition of the pagans, is exalted in dignity high above all other pagan women—so much so that over a vast length of time, her name could not be forgotten. 

Yet, we might better describe the great good fortune that befell her, since no actual deeds were performed by her that are worthy of remembrance. For she was born in one birth <note>i.e., as a twin</note> with Jupiter of Crete, whom the ancients, through their traditions, invented to be a god of the heavens. And she was sent in her cradle to the island of Samos, and there she was raised with great diligence until her marriageable years, and at last was wedded to her brother Jupiter. 

This is according to the evidence of a cast statue <note>original: "gegoſſen ſaul"; likely referring to a bronze monument or pillar</note> which was placed for a long time in the temple of Samos as a testimony of this event. For the Samians believed that no small honor had been granted to them and their descendants by the fact that Juno—who was esteemed by them as a queen of heaven and a goddess—had been wed among them. And that

# The 6th leaf

so that she would not easily be erased from human memory, they built a magnificent temple, wondrous above all others in the entire world, called the Temple of Juno after her name. Within it, they placed a statue carved from marble of a young woman, dressed in the clothing usually worn by those who were to be wed to husbands. 

This same Juno—after she was given as a wife to the great King Jupiter, and as his kingdom grew from day to day and his praise resounded far and wide throughout the lands—was enlightened in no small measure and elevated in praise. Through the poems of the poets and the irrational generosity of the ancients, she was called a Queen of Heaven. Thus, they gave to her—who was but a mortal queen—authority over the heavens and all riches, and especially over the happy granting of marriages and of women in childbirth, and many other things that are better suited for mockery than for belief. 

Because of such things, and through the counsel of the enemy of human salvation <note>A standard medieval reference to the Devil, who was thought to have lured the ancient world into the sin of idolatry.</note>, many temples were built in her honor, and many altars, priests, games, and festive days were established according to ancient custom. And though I remain silent about the others, she was nonetheless highly honored by the people of Samos, by the Greeks, also by the people of Achaia, and by those of Carthage. 

Finally, she was brought from that same city <note>referring to Carthage</note> to Rome and placed in the Capitol, in the chamber <note>original: "zell"; referring to the *cella*, the inner room of a temple housing the deity's statue</note> of Jupiter the Greatest <note>original: "Jupiter des grösten"; a translation of the Latin title *Jupiter Optimus Maximus*</note> as if beside her husband, and it was called the Temple of Juno the Queen. There she was highly honored for a long time by the mighty Romans of the entire world with many kinds of dignity—even after the time when God appeared as a man upon the earth <note>A reference to the birth of Christ, noting that pagan worship persisted into the Christian era.</note>.

# The 6th leaf

Ceres is also called Ops, Cybele, Thea, Proserpina, Vesta, the Kind Virgin <note>original: "alma virgo"</note>, the Great Mother <note>original: "magna mater"</note>, and the Good Goddess <note>original: "bona dea"</note> by various and diverse people. Juvenal also says: "and they appease the Good Goddess with a female sow" <note>original: "bonam tenere placant ab domine porce"; a reference to the Roman poet Juvenal and the sacrificial customs associated with the cult of the Bona Dea, or "Good Goddess."</note>.

## Concerning Ceres, the goddess of fruits and a queen of Sicily; the fifth chapter.

<note>A woodcut illustration shows various agricultural scenes: on the left, a man carries a heavy sack into a stone barn; in the center, oxen pull a cart while a figure harvests grain with a sickle; on the right, a crowned woman representing Ceres holds a scepter while a laborer works at her feet.</note>

Ceres, as some say, was the most illustrious queen of Sicily. She possessed such exceeding intellect and reason that she was the very first among her people to devise the art of agriculture and to tame oxen to the yoke. She also invented the plow and the plowshare <term>plowshare: the metal blade of a plow that cuts the soil</term> with which, through the aforementioned help of the oxen, she cut through and turned the earth, hiding the seed within it. 

And when the crop had reached its full growth for the harvest, she taught the people how to thresh, grind, and bake the grain. She showed these people—who previously had survived only on a diet of acorns and wild crab-apples—how to make proper food from the harvest. Because of these great merits, although she was a mortal woman, the people believed she was a goddess of grain sent down to them from heaven, and they elevated her with divine...

<summary>This page introduces Ceres as a historical queen of Sicily who achieved divinity in the eyes of her people by inventing agriculture, the plow, and bread-making. It highlights the transition of ancient society from foraging for acorns to organized farming.</summary>
<keywords>Ceres, Sicily, agriculture, invention of the plow, grain, bread-making, deification, Bona Dea</keywords>

# The 7th leaf

...and believed she was a daughter of Saturn and Cybele. Furthermore, they say how Orcus, the king of Molossia <note>original: "Orcus der küng von molosia"; Molossia was an ancient Greek state in Epirus. Here, the author uses a historical explanation for the myth of Pluto (Orcus) abducting Proserpina.</note>, during a time of turbulence at sea, abducted her daughter Proserpina, whom she had borne to her brother Jupiter. She searched for her for a long time thereafter, just as a long fable has been made about it. There was also another Ceres in the city of Eleusis, in the region near Athens, who was elevated through similar merits just like the Ceres mentioned before. It is said of her that Triptolemus <note>In Greek mythology, Triptolemus was the youth to whom Ceres (Demeter) taught the craft of agriculture so he could spread it to the world.</note> was her servant. 

And because they both possessed equal merits, they have been placed together in one chapter. Whether, however, their ingenious discoveries are to be praised or blamed, I do not know. For who would blame the fact that the wandering, wild people were drawn out of the forests and into the cities? Who would not also praise that those who previously lived without reason have been called to better habits? Who would blame that acorns were changed into grain, through which the body becomes more handsome, the limbs grow stronger, and a more fitting nourishment is given to man? Who would blame that a rough, ignorant time was transformed into orderly laws and life? 

Who does not praise that the sluggish mind and reason have been turned toward observation and practice? To whom would it not be pleasing that the coarse louts were drawn out of caves and brought to agriculture and rational labor? Through this, so many cities were expanded, so many newly built, so many kingdoms increased, and so many laudable customs invented and maintained—all because the art of grain was discovered. And since this art is good in itself, I believe—according to the judgment of many—that anyone who blamed it would be called insane. Conversely, however, who can praise that such a multitude of people, who had been scattered here and there in the woods, [fed upon] acorns and wild apples...

<summary>This page explores the dual identity of Ceres (Sicilian and Eleusinian) and defends the transition from a "wild" hunter-gatherer existence to an agricultural, urban civilization as a divine gift.</summary>

# The vij. leaf

...who were accustomed to the milk of wild animals, to small herbs, and to water; their spirits were free of all worry, content with the laws of nature, moderate, chaste, and ignorant of deceit—being enemies only to wild animals and birds—they were now called to a gentler and previously unknown food <note>This refers to grain and bread, the "unknown food" introduced by the goddess Ceres.</note>. From this, as we may well notice, all filth, sin, and shame have sprung forth, which previously lay hidden in the depths; for these vices, a safe path to emerge was opened. 

From this also arose the dividing of the earth with distinctions, ditches, and boundary stones; for before this, the great burden of agriculture had emerged, and the division of the earth was apportioned to humanity with great labor. But even more, the two names "mine" and "thine" <note>original: "mein vnnd dein"; this refers to the legal and philosophical concept of *Meum et Tuum*, the beginning of private property which many Renaissance thinkers believed ended the communal "Golden Age."</note> have arisen from this, which are enemies of both common and private utility, out of which many kinds of harm and [owner]ship...

# The vij. leaf

...who were accustomed to the milk of wild animals, to small herbs, and to water; their spirits were free of all worry, content with the laws of nature, moderate, chaste, and ignorant of deceit—being enemies only to wild animals and birds—they were now called to a gentler and previously unknown food <note>This refers to grain and bread, the "unknown food" introduced by the goddess Ceres, who in mythology taught humans agriculture.</note>. From this, as we may well notice, all filth, sin, and shame have sprung forth, which previously lay hidden in the depths; for these vices, a safe path to emerge was opened. 

From this also arose the dividing of the earth with distinctions, ditches, and boundary stones; for before this, the great burden of agriculture had emerged, and the division of the earth was apportioned to humanity with great labor. But even more, the two names "mine" and "thine" <note>original: "mein vnnd dein"; this refers to the legal and philosophical concept of *Meum et Tuum*. This reflects the Renaissance belief that the transition from communal living to private property marked the end of the "Golden Age" and the beginning of social strife.</note> have arisen from this, which are enemies of both common and private utility, out of which many kinds of harm and ownership <note>The text likely refers to the legal disputes and greed that follow the establishment of property rights.</note>...

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of civilization, noting that while agriculture brought refinement, it also introduced private property, greed, and social vices that were unknown to prehistoric humans living in a state of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Ceres, agriculture, state of nature, private property, Meum et Tuum, morality, history of civilization, Golden Age</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical reflection on the transition from the communal "Golden Age" (under Ceres) to a society defined by private property and moves into Chapter 6, which introduces Minerva (Pallas) and her contributions to human industry and wisdom.</meta>

...out of which many kinds of harm and ownership <note>original: "eygenschafft"; in this context, it refers to the legal concept of private property or "estates" that arose after the communal living of the prehistoric era.</note> have arisen, and from these have sprouted the heavy wars which have since caused so much blood to be shed.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the goddess Minerva, often shown in this edition with a helmet, shield, and spear, but also surrounded by tools of weaving and the olive tree, signifying her role as a patron of both defensive war and civilizing industry.</note>

# The vi. Chapter

-> **Concerning Minerva, who is also called Pallas** <-

Minerva, whom the Greeks called **Pallas**, was a maiden of such outstanding and manly spirit <note>original: "manheitlicher gemueth"; a term used in the 15th century to describe women of exceptional intellect, courage, or leadership qualities that transcended contemporary gender expectations.</note> that the ancients, marveling at her many new inventions, did not believe she was born of human parents. Instead, they believed she had fallen from the heavens or was born directly from the head of the god **Jupiter**. Some writers state that she first appeared during the lifetime of the patriarch Isaac, near the lake called **Triton** in Africa, which is why she is also known as **Tritonia**. 

She remained a maiden throughout her entire life. Through her keen and sharp intellect, she was the first to discover the use of **wool**, teaching people how to prepare it by cleaning it, softening it with oil, and <term>carding<gloss>The process of combing raw wool with metal teeth to align the fibers for spinning.</gloss></term> it. She further demonstrated how the wool could be spun using a distaff and spindle, and how it could finally be woven into finished cloth. 

<margin>She invented the use of armor.</margin>
Furthermore, she was the first to devise **iron armor** and helmets to protect the body in battle, which was a thing previously unknown to humanity. Because of her great wisdom, she is also credited with discovering the art of counting—or mathematics—and the cultivation of the **olive tree**, which provides both a source of food and the oil required for light.

<summary>This page concludes the account of how private property led to the tragedy of war, then introduces Minerva as the divine figure responsible for inventing weaving, armor, and mathematics.</summary>
<keywords>Minerva, Pallas, wool-working, inventions, Tritonia, origin of armor, private property, Early New High German literature</keywords>

The 9th leaf

...continued. Furthermore, she was the very first to conceive of **oil-pressing** for mankind: how one should crush the seeds and the kernels and thereafter press the oil out. Because of the great benefit that people received from this, she won the right to give the city of **Athens** its name, prevailing against **Neptune** <note>In Greek mythology, Athena and Poseidon competed for the patronage of the city. Poseidon offered a salt spring or a horse, but Athena offered the olive tree, which the citizens judged more useful.</note>. The ancients also maintain that she was the first to invent the journey by wagon <note>referring to chariots or carriages</note> and their use; she also showed how to fashion iron into weapons and how to cover the body with armor to protect it. She gave laws and teachings on how to arrange the ranks of battle and how one should advance in combat. 

They say further that she was the first to find and order all numbers and give them names, just as they are still used to this day. Even more, she fashioned pipes out of bird bones or out of the reeds of the marshes and ordered them according to their sounds <note>the invention of the flute</note>. Because of this, the common folk <note>original: "grob volck"; referring to the uneducated or rural populace</note> believed she had been cast down from heaven, for the neck and face of the one playing the pipe became swollen and distorted thereby <note>A reference to the myth where Minerva discarded the flute because she disliked how blowing into it puffed out her cheeks and made her face look "unshapely."</note>. 

Why should I say more of such strange stories? Because of them, erring antiquity and foolish humanity gave her the title **Goddess of Wisdom**. For this reason, the people of Athens were moved to take their name from her, as that same city was well-suited for learning, art, and wisdom. They entrusted themselves to her protection and built for her an exceedingly high tower and a very great temple consecrated in her name. They ordered an image to be placed therein, fashioned in the form described below: 

First, with squinting eyes <note>original: "krummen augen"; literally "crooked" or "sidelong" eyes. This suggests a gaze that sees more than what is directly in front of it, symbolizing the indirect or deep penetration of a wise mind.</note>, because one can seldom recognize the true end or intent of a wise man’s meaning. Next, a **helmet** upon her head, because the counsels of a wise man should be covered <note>kept secret or protected</note> and well-armed. Next, a **breastplate** upon her body, so that a wise man [is prepared] against all things...

# The 9th leaf

...of the wheel of fortune, whether they be good or bad, she should be armed. <note>The text suggests that wisdom protects a person against the unpredictable turns of fate or fortune.</note> Thereafter, she holds a long, flexible spear or projectile in her hand, so that one might notice how the wise man may send his projectiles of wisdom far and wide. In addition, she had before her for protection a crystalline shield, in which a dragon's head was fastened <note>While the text says "dragon's head," this likely refers to the Aegis featuring the head of the Gorgon Medusa, which paralyzed enemies with fear.</note>. This signifies that the wise man should be covered and protected with pure and clear counsel, and yet be guarded with such snakelike cunning that foolish people stand terrified before his face. They also set an owl upon her to guard her <note>original: "zü zehüt ein eylen"</note>, because the wise man should see as well by night as by day.

Finally, the praise and worthiness of this same lady became so wide and so broad—and the errors of the ancients were so favorable to her—that temples were built in her honor throughout nearly the entire world, and festive days and feasts were established. This went so far that in Rome, on the Capitol, she was placed in a separate chamber next to the best and greatest **Jupiter**, and was held equal to **Juno** among the foremost gods of the Romans. 

However, there are some great men who say that these aforementioned things did not all happen because of one single Minerva. I will gladly concede this, so that the praise of these illustrious women may be all the wider and greater in number <note>The author acknowledges the "Euhemerist" view—that myths often combine the lives of several historical women into one legendary figure.</note>.

# The 10th leaf
-> 18 <-

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the mythological narrative of the goddess Venus and the god Mars. **V**enus was a woman of Cyprus, as some believe. However, there is some doubt as to which ancestors she was born from; for some say she was born of Cyrus and Syria. Others say she was born of Cyrus and Dione, the Cyprian woman. Some, speaking in praise of her beauty, say she was born of **Jupiter** and the aforementioned Dione. Yet, regardless of from whom she was born, she is numbered among the illustrious women <note>The text belongs to a tradition of "Euhemerism," which argued that the gods of antiquity were actually historical people whose lives were later mythologized.</note> more because of her exceeding beauty than for any of her meritorious deeds. For she was so beautiful in face and form, and appeared with such graceful physical elegance and loveliness of body, that those who looked upon her were often deceived by false perceptions. For some claimed she was the true morning star, which is also named **Venus**. <note>The planet Venus is the brightest natural object in the night sky after the Moon, often appearing as a "Morning Star" just before sunrise.</note> Others said she was a heavenly lady sent down to earth from the lap of **Jupiter**. In short, the eyes of all who looked upon her were so clouded by a dark mist <note>The "mist" is a metaphorical way of describing how lust or infatuation blinds human reason.</note> that they esteemed this woman, though born of mortal men, to be a goddess, and said that...

This arose from those who could not withstand her wanton provocations. She was also highly esteemed and honored by other peoples of the land, especially by the Romans, who had a temple built for her in the name of the **Temple of Venus the Mother and the Turner of Hearts** <note>original: "templum veneris genitricis et verticorde." *Venus Genitrix* was honored as the ancestress of the Roman people, while *Venus Verticordia* was the "turner of hearts" from lust toward virtue—though the author implies her "turning" was more manipulative.</note>.
