Afterward, she was with **Adonis**, the son of Cinyras and Myrrha, the king of Cyprus. And it seems to me that this is believable, based on her usual activities; for besides the fact that this woman's nature was inclined toward impurity, the kingdom of Cyprus was entirely stained with unchastity and was very helpful to her works. For after the death of **Adonis**, she fell into such great, provocative unchastity that she made her great clarity, surpassing beauty, and bright eyes entirely dark and defiled <note>original: "vermailiget"</note> through many manifold acts of impurity. ### The 11th leaf

When it became known throughout the lands, her first husband, **Vulcan**, caught her with an armorer <note>original: "wapner," a soldier or armor-maker, here referring to the god Mars.</note>. From this, the fable was invented of how Mars—the armorer—was caught with her by Vulcan and bound to her with a golden chain and shown to the gods. Finally, thinking she could wipe away her excessive behavior and somewhat lessen her blushing shame, and believing she could carry out her works of impurity more respectably, she first devised that shameful and unseemly work: the public brothels <note>original: "frawen hewser," literally "women's houses."</note>. She compelled honorable, beautiful women to comply with all men therein through acts of unchastity. The long-standing custom of the people of **Cyprus** has given testimony to this, for it was long held among them that they placed their virgins by the roads and the ports of the sea so that foreign men would have dealings with them. By this, they intended to show pleasure to **Venus** and pay a sacrifice from their chastity, believing they would be gifted because of it.

This is truly inhuman and clearly to be perceived; yet this same evil custom spread through the land as far as **Italy**. For one reads of the Locrians <note>The Locrians were an ancient Greek tribe who settled in southern Italy; historical accounts by authors like Justin claim they vowed to prostitute their daughters to Venus during a war.</note> that they also maintained this same custom. It even reached into the new era of faith <note>original: "newen ee," referring to the Christian era or "New Covenant."</note>, as the life of **Saint Afra** <note>Saint Afra (died c. 304 AD) was an early Christian martyr in Augsburg who was traditionally said to have been a prostitute before her conversion.</note> and her companions indicates.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the myth of Io. In the foreground, Mercury stands over the body of Argus, the hundred-eyed guardian whom he has slain. **Isis**, who was previously called **Io**, is not only a queen, but was ultimately the holiest goddess of the **Egyptians**. Some say she was a daughter of **Inachus** <note>original: "ÿnacÿ"</note>, the first king in **Greece**, and a sister of **Phoroneus**; these reigned during the time of **Jacob**, who was a son of **Isaac** <note>Early modern historians often tried to align "pagan" myths with the biblical timeline to create a universal history.</note>. Others say she was a daughter of **Prometheus**, in the time when **Phorbas** ruled **Greece**, which was a very long time after the previously mentioned period. Some say she lived in the time of **Cecrops** <note>original: "citropis"</note>, the king of **Athens**. And some say beyond that, she lived in the time of **Lynceus** <note>original: "linthei"</note>, king of the **Greeks**. Each one puts forward his own particular reasons to confirm his opinion; nonetheless, she is counted by all of them among us illustrious women <note>The narrator here speaks from the perspective of the "famous women" being profiled, a stylistic choice common in translations of Boccaccio's *De Mulieribus Claris*.</note> as one most worthy of memory. And according to the opinion of the majority...

# The 12th leaf

Yet this same fable is not far from the truth; for as it is said, the same maiden was defiled by King **Jupiter** through adultery. And through fear of her father because of the committed misdeed, by **Jupiter’s** arrangement, she was placed on a large ship with several of her servants, on whose unfurled banner a cow was painted. And this ingenious woman of manly spirit <note>In the Renaissance and Middle Ages, "manly spirit" (manlichs gemüts) was a high compliment for a woman, suggesting she possessed courage and intellect traditionally attributed to men.</note>, desiring a kingdom, was led with a lucky wind toward **Egypt**. But since it is not precisely found by what merit she came to possess the kingdom, it is nonetheless believable that she found the people coarse, ignorant, and entirely unknowing of human nature, living more like beasts than rational beings. These same people she taught, with great labor and courtly cleverness, how to till the earth, sow seeds therein, harvest the grain, grind it, and bake [bread]. 

Furthermore, she gathered together into a community the wild people who were widely scattered in the forests, and gave them laws <note>original: "gesatzt"</note>, and taught them to live in an orderly and civil manner. And that which is to be esteemed as even greater and more remarkable in a woman...

# The 13th leaf
## ¶ Concerning Europa, the queen in Crete, that is Candia. The ninth chapter.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the abduction of Europa. A woman, identified as Europa, is being led by a man toward a waiting ship. Inside the ship, a man is labeled "Jupiter." At the bottom right of the illustration, the name "Europa" is written. Some say **Europa** was a daughter of Phoenix <note>original: "phenicis"</note>. But many more say she was a daughter of **Agenor**, the king of the Phoenicians. She was of such wonderful beauty that the king of Crete—that is, Candia—**Jupiter**, was moved to love her by the reports of her beauty, even though he had not yet seen her. And so that he might seduce her <note>original: "verfüren," meaning to lead astray or entice</note>, he ordered a voyage to be prepared to travel to her. On the banner of the ship, a white bull was displayed as a coat of arms. And one <note>This likely refers to a messenger or Jupiter himself in the guise of a mortal</note> went to her playfully to speak with her such flattering words that she, without any ill suspicion, walked with him from her father's herd, which she was tending, out of the mountains down to the shores of Phoenicia. As she listened to his deceptive words, she was snatched into the ship and quickly carried away to Crete. 

Observe here how unseemly it is for young women to wander here and there according to their own free will, and to offer their ears to all the flattering words of young men or old women. For it is often heard and read that the discipline, modesty, and purity of young women are thus defiled, so that the...

marks that could never be washed away but remained for all eternity. <note>The author is likely concluding a moral point from the previous page about how a loss of reputation or virginity is a permanent "stain" that cannot be cleansed.</note> From this history comes the fable of how **Mercury** drove the Phoenician cattle <note>original: "vich phenicium"</note> to the shore and took **Europa**, carrying her across the swimming sea to **Crete**. However, there is some disagreement among the ancient writers as to what time Europa was abducted. For some write that it happened in the times when **Danaus** reigned in Greece; others when **Acrisius** reigned. The last say it was in the times when **Pandion** reigned at **Athens**.

But I am of the opinion that this same story is told of the Europa who was the mother of **Minos**, who was also seduced <note>original: "geſchwechet," literally "weakened," a common euphemism for a loss of virginity or honor</note> by **Jupiter**, and afterwards wed to **Asterus**, who was a king in Crete, and bore to him Minos, Rhadamanthus, and Sarpedon. Yet some say these two <note>Referring to Rhadamanthus and Sarpedon</note> were Jupiter’s sons and believe Asterus and Jupiter were two names for one man. It does not serve us much to clarify this, other than that this woman was called "illustrious" <note>original: "durchleűchtend," a high-ranking title for nobility</note> since she was wed to such a god. Also because she was found by her people to be of noble lineage, significant merit, and great virtue—so much so that the third part of the entire world is called **Europe** after her name. And as a testimony of this, the greatest philosopher, **Pythagoras**, had a bronze pillar cast in her likeness and set up in the city of **Tarentum**, and gave it the name Europa.

...**Busiris**, who afterwards became a king in Upper Egypt. The great virtues and good deeds of this same **Libya** have been almost entirely obscured by antiquity; however, it is well worth noting that her deeds must have been exceedingly great, since the great kingdom of **Libya** is named after her.

->**Horace**<-

"From what source the custom was derived, which throughout all time arms the right hands of the **Amazonian** women with the battle-axe, I have deferred asking." <note>original Latin: "Quibus mos unde deductus per omne/ Tempus amazonia securi dextras obarmet. Querere distuli." A quote from the Roman poet Horace’s Odes (4.4), reflecting on the martial tradition of the Amazons.</note>

### Of Marsepia and Lampedo, the Queens in Amazonia. The 11th Chapter.

<note>A woodcut illustration shows two crowned women on horseback, Marsepia and Lampedo, accompanied by two soldiers. The labels "marsepia" and "lampedo" appear within the image to identify the two sisters.</note>

**Marsepia** (or **Marthesia**) and **Lampedo** were sisters and both were simultaneously queens of the **Amazons**. Because of their illustrious <note>original: "durchleüchtiger," meaning shining or noble</note> honor, which they pursued and won in battle, they called themselves the daughters of **Mars**, the god of war. And because this same history is somewhat strange, we wish to say more about it.

<summary>This page concludes the account of the Egyptian queen Libya and introduces the Amazonian sister-queens Marsepia and Lampedo, who claimed divine descent from Mars.</summary>
<keywords>Libya, Busiris, Horace, Marsepia, Lampedo, Amazons, Mars, daughters of Mars, geography, female rulers</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Amazons introduced on the previous page, shifting from the specific queens Marsepia and Lampedo to the legendary origin of the Amazon nation in Scythia.</meta>

->The 14th leaf<-

The kingdom of **Scythia** <note>A vast region in Eurasia, known in antiquity for its nomadic and warlike inhabitants.</note> at that time still consisted of uncultivated wilderness and wasteland. It was enclosed by mountains and the high sea, so that it was less troubled by the movement of people via rivers. Out of this land, due to the evil desires of the ruling elite, two young men of royal lineage, **Silistus** and **Scolopicus**, were driven out along with a significant portion of their people. They came to the waters of the **Thermodon** <note>A river in northern Turkey, famously associated with the Amazons in Greek mythology.</note> in **Cappadocia** and inhabited the land of **Themiscyra** <note>original: "tyriam," referring to the capital of the Amazons</note>, living by robbery and causing significant distress to the surrounding region.

Because of this, from day to day, many of the inhabitants of that same land were killed through treachery and by evil enemies, so that nearly all the male population had perished. When the women, who were placed in the state of widowhood <note>original: "witwēn stül," literally "widow's chairs," a metaphor for the legal and social status of a widow</note>, bore this heavily and fervently thought of avenging their husbands, they were moved to take up warlike weapons along with the few remaining men.

In their first rush of frenzy, they drove the enemies out of their territory. Following this, they waged significant war against their neighbors. Finally, they began to consider that if they were to marry foreign men again, they would first have to submit themselves to servitude <note>The text suggests they feared a return to patriarchal subservience.</note>. They believed they were capable enough to fight with their own warlike hands. Furthermore, because the surviving men seemed to have no kinder gods protecting them than those who had already been slain, the women all took counsel together—including those whose husbands had not been killed—and decided that they would kill all the remaining men.

Immediately after that, when this deed was accomplished, they turned their angry rage toward their enemies and pursued them so powerfully to avenge their husbands that the enemies desired nothing but peace from them. And when peace was made, they began to hope for the continuation of their lineage...

<summary>This page describes the origins of the Amazons, detailing how a group of Scythians settled in Cappadocia and how, after their men were decimated by war, the women seized power and resolved to live without men.</summary>
<keywords>Scythia, Silistus, Scolopicus, Thermodon, Cappadocia, Themiscyra, Amazons, widowhood, female warriors, vengeance</keywords>

->The 15th leaf<-

...to consort in common with the men living nearby. As soon as they became pregnant, they departed from them again toward their own dwellings. And whichever boy was born, they had him killed or sent him away; but the girls were raised with great diligence for the life of a warrior <note>original: "ritterschafft," referring to the status and skills of a knight or noble warrior</note>.

In their childhood, the mark of the right breast was burned out with fire or caustic agents <note>The text describes the practice of cauterizing the breast bud to prevent its growth.</note>, so that when they grew up, it would not hinder them in shooting their bows. They left the left breast untouched to provide nourishment for the children who were to be born. Because of this, they were called **Amazons** <note>This reflects a popular medieval and ancient etymology: *a-* (without) and *mazos* (breast), though modern linguists dispute this origin.</note>. 

They did not apply such diligence to raising their children as our women do; for spinning, sewing, and similar womanly works were despised by them. Instead, they practiced hunting, jumping, wrestling, running, target shooting, and the catching and taming of wild horses. Each was practiced in these things according to her age. This was their constant exercise, so that they might become skillful in performing manly deeds.

Through such skill, they not only held **Themiscyra** <note>original: "tyriam," the capital city of the Amazons in Anatolia</note>, which had formerly belonged to their ancestors, but they also won through hard combat a great part of the earth toward the setting of the sun <note>Meaning the West.</note> called **Europe**. This went so far that they devastated the land of **Swabia** <note>A historical region in southwestern Germany.</note>; for this reason, the inhabitants sought their dwellings upon the high mountains for greater safety. 

And after they had subjugated the land, they appointed a king named **Henetus** at **Augsburg**, located in the **Ries** <note>The Nördlinger Ries, a large circular depression in Swabia.</note>, which at that same time was called **Vindelicia** <note>The Roman name for the region between the Alps and the Danube.</note>, to rule the land of Swabia under their scepter. This same Henetus also conquered further into the land of the Mantuans, which was called **Henecia** after his name. This territory reached as far as where **Baden** and **Venice** now lie; and it was called **Venecia**, by changing the letter *h* into *v*, as most [scholars say...]

<summary>This page describes the Amazonian customs of raising only female children and cauterizing the right breast to facilitate archery. It also details their conquest of Europe, including Swabia and the mythical founding of Venice by their appointed king, Henetus.</summary>

->The 15th leaf<-

Servius, who wrote a commentary on Virgil <note>Servius was a 4th-century grammarian famous for his line-by-line commentary on Virgil's *Aeneid*.</note>, gives us to understand in the first book regarding the verse on Antenor <note>A reference to Virgil’s *Aeneid*, Book 1, where the history of various nations is touched upon.</note> that they also won much land in Asia and became fearsome to everyone. And so that their great power would not be without government, they appointed **Marpesia** and **Lampedo** as queens following the departure of their mothers. Under their reign, the power of these women was notably increased, just as was written above. 

These same two, having been so highly ennobled through their great knightly deeds <note>original: "ritterlich groß getät," referring to the valorous and noble actions expected of the knightly class in the Middle Ages.</note>, divided the lands between themselves. Therefore, while one remained upon the royal throne to rule the land, the other would exert herself with the remaining part of the army <note>original: "pres hores," a variant of *Heeres*, meaning military host or army.</note> to make other lands subject to them. And thus they increased their realms for a long time through their notable reputation and the conquest of lands. 

But at last, when Lampedo had marched out against the enemies with the army <note>original: "hörestkrafft," meaning military force.</note>, the barbarians fell upon Marpesia and slew her, along with a significant number of her maidens. However, how things went for Lampedo, I do not know, for I have never read anything regarding that.

->The 16th leaf<-

¶ Ovid in the 4th book of *Metamorphoses* <note>original: "iiij. ma.," an abbreviation for Ovid's *Metamorphoses*, Book 4.</note>
¶ Pyramus and Thisbe, one the most beautiful of youths, the other the fairest of girls. <note>original Latin: "Piramus et Tisbe, iuuenum pulcerrimus alter, altera, quas Oriens habuit, praelata puellis."</note>
¶ Concerning Thisbe, the maiden of Babylon. The 12th Chapter.

**T**hisbe, the maiden from Babylon, has remained in the eternal memory of mankind more because of the tragic outcome of her ill-fated love than because of any other deeds. Also, we have no record from our ancestors regarding what lineage or family she came from. However, she lived in the city of Babylon in a house that was located right next to another house, in which lived a boy of her own age named Pyramus. And because they were neighbors, it happened that they both shared much childhood companionship and daily interaction with one another. There grew in them such a childhood affection that, in their later years, this childhood inclination kindled into an exceedingly great and fervent love, fueled by the great beauty of both their bodies, with which they were gifted above all others. They would also occasionally show such love to one another through gestures as they approached their marriageable years <note>original: "manberen jaren," referring to the age of puberty or the age at which one is ready for marriage.</note>. 

But as Thisbe grew older, her father and relatives thought to keep her under better guard and more at home in view of a future marriage <note>original: "gemähelschafft," a term for marriage or betrothal.</note>. And as the lovers had to endure this for some time—albeit with heavy hearts—they began to seek diligently for a way they might still speak with one another. And they found, in a wall shared by both houses in a secret corner, a crack <note>original: "klunsen," meaning a fissure or narrow opening in a wall.</note> that had previously been unknown to anyone. They came to this spot often in secret to speak with one another. And habit eventually led them to widen the crack at that end

->The 16th leaf<-

...where it was most clearly seen how they suffered for one another: it began with the longing of their hearts, their trials, suffering, sighs, and sometimes even fervent tears, so that they might better show their feelings to one another. At times, they also desired the mutual love of the soul, seeking to embrace one another and touch their lips <note>original: "berürung der münde," literally "touching of mouths," meaning a kiss.</note> in the full loyalty of eternal friendship.

At last, as they were so fervently inflamed with passion, they resolved to seek a way to flee. They agreed to steal out of their houses the following night as soon as they possibly could and go into the forest that lay near the city, to King Ninus's fountain <note>King Ninus was the legendary founder of Nineveh and husband of Semiramis; in Ovid's *Metamorphoses*, the lovers agree to meet at his tomb.</note> located at the edge of the woods. They decided that whoever arrived there first should wait for the other.

But Thisbe—perhaps because she was the more fervent in her love—decided to leave her father’s house earlier and more joyfully. She went out boldly, wrapped in a cloak <term>mantel|A heavy outer garment or cloak, used here for warmth and disguise.</term>, during the first sleep of the night, and arrived unafraid in the forest by the light of the moon.

And as she waited by the fountain, lifting her head whenever she heard a sound in hopes of Pyramus's arrival, she saw a lion approaching the fountain. She was so terrified by this that she fled into the bushes, but in her haste, she did not think of the cloak lying by the fountain.

When the lion had sated its hunger on other animals and quenched its thirst, it began to toy with the cloak with its bloody <note>original: "schwaiſſigen," literally "sweaty," but used here in the archaic sense to mean blood-stained or gory, as was common in hunting and predatory descriptions of the period.</note> mouth and claws, shredding it until it was completely bloodied and torn. Then, the beast left it lying there and went away.

In the meantime, Pyramus arrived in the forest, having left his house later than she. When he saw Thisbe’s blood-stained, tattered cloak, he believed the lion had devoured her. For this reason, he began to mourn and lament his misfortune and misery from the depths of his soul, believing that he was the cause of his beloved maiden's suffering under the grim [beast]...

<summary>Thisbe arrives first at the secret meeting spot but flees when she encounters a lion, accidentally leaving her cloak behind. The lion shreds the blood-stained garment, leading Pyramus to arrive and mistakenly conclude that his lover has been killed.</summary>

->The 17th leaf<-

...death. And disdaining to live any longer, he set his drawn sword against his breast by the fountain and fell upon it to kill himself. And immediately, when Thisbe thought that the lion had finished drinking and was gone—so that she would not leave her lover in suspicion <note>The text suggests she did not want him to worry that she had stood him up or been unfaithful to their plan.</note> of her absence, or cause him grief by a long delay—she began to creep back toward the fountain. And as she arrived, she heard—as can well be imagined—Pyramus still struggling <note>original: "zabeln," meaning to twitch or flail, describing the final involuntary movements of a dying person.</note> upon the sword, and she was terrified, thinking the lion was still there, and nearly fled back again. But when she looked more closely, she saw by the moonlight that it was Pyramus lying there. And when she hurried to his affectionate embrace, she found that he had poured out all his blood through the wound and was now giving up his soul.

And though she was greatly terrified by this first sad sight, she finally began to devise useless help amidst exceedingly great weeping, trying to keep the soul within his body for a while with kisses and loving embraces. But when she could get no word from him, and noticed that he now disregarded her kisses—which only the day before had been so welcome <term>empfencklich|Meaning "receptive" or "welcomed with open arms."</term> to him—and seeing that her lover was thus brought to his death, she realized that he had dealt himself death because he believed she had been torn apart by the lion. And moved by the love for her youth and by his pain, she was compelled in bitter grief to die with him. She drew the sword by the hilt out of his wound and, with exceedingly great sighing and weeping, she called upon the name of Pyramus and begged him to open his eyes so that he might at least see his Thisbe dying with him, and wait for her departing soul so that they might be together wherever they might go. It is a wonder...

<meta>This page continues the tragic climax of Pyramus and Thisbe. Pyramus has already stabbed himself, believing Thisbe is dead. Thisbe has just found him and is attempting to comfort him in his final moments.</meta>

->The 17th leaf<-

To say that when the dying youth—whose reason was now fading due to the **faintness of his heart**—heard the name of his dear maiden, he could not deny her his final request. He opened his eyes, which were already heavy with death, and looked upon her who had pleaded with him. Immediately, she placed the sword, hilt-first, upon his breast and pointed the blade <note>original: "spiez," literally 'spear' or 'point,' referring here to the tip of the sword.</note> at her own heart and fell upon it. With the shedding of her blood, both their souls departed from their bodies together.

And thus, the **envious wheel of fortune** could not prevent the mixing of their unhappy blood, even though it had refused to grant them the embrace of their living bodies. Who would not have compassion for these young people? It would be a heart of stone that did not grant at least a little tear <note>original: "zäherlin," a diminutive form of 'tear,' suggesting a small but sincere expression of grief.</note> to this wretched outcome. They loved each other with a pure, childlike affection; therefore, they did not deserve such a deadly misfortune.

The love of youth is indeed a fault, yet it is not so heavy a burden for those who are single <note>original: "ledig," meaning unmarried or free of other commitments.</note>, for they may eventually come together in the state of marriage. Therefore, we must blame their misfortune, or perhaps their wretched friends <note>The author likely refers to their parents or kinsmen, whose feud forced the lovers into secrecy.</note> were the cause of this evil. For one must not throw a sudden bolt <term>rigel|A bar or bolt used to lock a door; here used metaphorically as a barrier to love.</term> between loving youths; instead, they should be morally guided <note>original: "sitlich gezämet," meaning to be 'tamed' or 'reined in' through manners and ethics rather than brute force.</note>, so that they are not driven into the evil of despair by a sudden restriction.

For **Venus's son, Cupid**, is exceedingly strong and powerful—a sickness and a common trial for all youth. Therefore, it is something to be tolerated to an extent, for the Ruler of all things has granted this inclination and given to youth a more fervent stimulation toward such works of nature, so that the human race might remain in existence and not perish, as would surely happen if such works were universally abolished.

<summary>Thisbe commits suicide upon seeing the dying Pyramus, and the author offers a moral reflection on the tragedy, blaming the parents' interference and the natural power of love (Cupid) for the outcome.</summary>

¶ Of Hypermestra, Queen in the Land of Greece,

**Hypermestra**, illustrious by birth and dignity, was the daughter of Danaus, the King in Greece, and the wife of Linus <note>In classical mythology, her husband is usually named Lynceus; 'Linus' is a variation common in medieval German adaptations.</note>. From ancient histories, it is gathered that once in Egypt there were two brothers who were granted significant dominion by their father, Belus Priscus <note>Belus 'the Elder,' a legendary King of Egypt.</note>. One was named Danaus and the other Aegyptus. And although the number of children born to both was equal, the fortune of those children was very different; for Danaus had fifty daughters, and Aegyptus had just as many sons. But when Danaus had received an answer from the gods <note>An oracle or divine prophecy.</note> that he would be killed by the hand of his brother's children, he was secretly [filled] with great...

...should be. It happened that when the children of both had grown up, Aegyptus <note>original: "egistus"</note> asked Danaus to give all his daughters as brides to his sons. Danaus gladly consented to this because of the grim evil he had pre-meditated. And when all the daughters were betrothed to all the sons of his brother, the wedding day was set and all the festivities were prepared. Then Danaus, with the utmost diligence, implored and entreated his daughters: if they wished to preserve his life and hope for good things from him, then each must kill her husband in the night—while they were bound and weighed down by sleep and wine—with a blade <term>iron; historically referring to a sword or dagger</term>. 

Thus, complying with their father’s will, each of them hid a sharp knife under her pillow <term>original: "haubtpfulgen," a head-bolster or cushion</term>. At their father’s bidding, they murdered all their husbands, who were now in a deep sleep after surviving the day's celebrations. Only Hypermestra did not soil her hands with the blood of her husband, Linus. For the maiden had now set her heart upon him, as is the custom of daughters <note>This likely refers to the natural transition of a woman's loyalty and affection from her father to her husband upon marriage.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the moralizing history of Danaus, though the folio numbering jumps from the previous page's 18th leaf to the 24th leaf in this transcription. The text shifts from narrative action to a stern rhetorical condemnation of Danaus’s attempt to thwart fate through murder.</meta>

<margin>27</margin>

## The 24th leaf.

...as if you might secure the **flying wheel of fortune** <note>The *Rota Fortunae*, a common medieval and Renaissance symbol of the instability of fate and worldly power.</note> with impure works, and think to eternalize your short, sick life—which is shameful even to speak of—through disgraceful, heavy sin and misdeeds. And yet, you see all others hurrying in a flying race toward death. Why do you provoke the judgment of God with such terrifying deeds, with such unspeakable, evil works? 

And leaving all others aside for now, I take as testimony of such malice **Danaus**, who—so that he might lengthen his trembling years <note>original: "zitrende jar"; a reference to the frailty of old age</note> a little—robbed himself of the great honor and power he had obtained from them through the great bloodshed of his brother's sons. He was placed in eternal shame and established as a slandered practitioner of vice.

The wicked, evil man intended to prioritize the few years of his old age over the blooming years of the youth of his brother’s children. Yet they would perhaps have been more useful to him if he had kept them in honor. But to lengthen one's own age with the blood of so many youths is against nature and inhuman to contemplate. 

To such an evil, which heaped even more vice upon him, he did not appoint warriors or his own servants; instead, he armed **his own daughters** for the misdeed. Thus, he did not only destroy his brother's children, but he also cast his daughters into eternal, shameful mourning with **defiled hands** <note>original: "vermeÿligten henden"; hands stained or polluted by the crime</note> because of the great murder—daughters whom he could have kept in goodness and honor. And since he desired to prolong his life through misdeed, he did not consider what a bad example, what audacity, what betrayal, and what a terrifying cause he has left for future wives <note>original: "weÿßen"; likely referring to the daughters as the "wives" of the murdered sons</note> to violate and [destroy]... <note>The text ends mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>This page offers a moral condemnation of Danaus, arguing that his attempt to extend his own life by forcing his daughters to murder his nephews was a violation of nature and divine justice.</summary>

the kingdom, but because of her great and manifold 
virtue, became a priestess of the goddess **Juno**; in 
eternal, illustrious honors, she was seen to excel.

## ¶ Concerning Niobe, the Queen of the Thebans; the 14th Chapter.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the tragedy of Niobe. A man labeled "Amphion" stands with a sword over several dead children, representing the slaughter of their offspring. To the right, a woman wearing a crown, labeled "Niobe," stands gesturing toward them in grief. Above them, a sun with a human face shines down, likely representing Apollo, who—along with Artemis—brought about the death of Niobe's children.</note>

Niobe was very well known among the common people as one of the most highly celebrated women. This was because she was the daughter of the very ancient and widely renowned King Tantalus of Phrygia, and a sister of Pelops. She was married to Amphion, the King of the Thebans, who was the most illustrious man of those times; this was because he was known to be a son of Jupiter and the most eloquent <note>original: "baßredendest"; literally "the best-speaking."</note> of men. It was also because, during his reign, he fathered seven sons and as many daughters by this same Niobe. But that which should have been useful to a wise man, became for the proud woman... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the story of Hypermnestra's virtuousness and introduces Niobe, a queen of Thebes whose high status and many children initially brought her fame but eventually led to her prideful downfall.</summary>
<keywords>Niobe, Amphion, Tantalus, Thebes, Hypermnestra, Ovid, Metamorphoses, Greek Mythology, Pride</keywords>

# The 15th leaf.

...turned to destruction. For through the lovely appearance of her children and the great nobility of her ancestors, she was so uplifted in pride and arrogance that she even dared to speak insulting words to the gods. 

And when the Thebans one day were diligently holding festivals and sacrifices for Latona—who was the mother of Apollo and Diana, both of whom had been held in worthy holiness from ancient times according to their error <note>The author uses "error" (German: *jrsal*) to describe the pagan religions of antiquity from a medieval Christian perspective.</note>—at the command of the maiden Manto, who was the daughter of the prophet Tiresias; then Niobe sprang forth impetuously like a madwoman. She was surrounded by her children and adorned in royal garments, crying out: "What is this madness of the Thebans, to offer such festivals and honors to Latona? She is a stranger, descended from the Titans, and has borne no more than two children, conceived in adultery. And yet you wish to place her above the daughter of Tantalus, who has lawfully borne fourteen children with her husband, as if she were more worthy of honor than I?" 

Shortly thereafter it happened that, before her very eyes, all her sons—in their beautiful, blooming youth—were taken away by a deadly affliction <note>In the classical myth, Apollo and Diana killed Niobe's children with arrows as punishment for her pride.</note>, all except for one. And Amphion, the father of the fourteen children, was so overcome by sorrow and pain at their deaths that he stabbed himself with a sword by his own hands. The Thebans believed all of this had been done as vengeance for the insulted gods. 

But Niobe, who remained as a widow, fell from great mourning and sorrow into such a hardened rigidity, silenced forever, that one would more rightly have judged her an immovable stone than a woman. For this reason, it was written by the poets that she was transformed into a stone pillar at Sipylus <note>Mount Sipylus in modern-day Turkey, where a rock formation resembling a weeping woman is still associated with the legend of Niobe.</note>, where her children were buried. It is

# The 21st leaf

...hard and hateful proud men—not only are they to be endured, but even to be honored. However, proud women are unfaithful, from whom the gods' displeasure is justly earned. For nature has generally created men with a more fervent heat <note>The author refers to the medieval medical theory of the four humors, where men were considered "hot and dry" (associated with action and anger) and women "cold and moist" (associated with passivity and gentleness).</note> and a loftier spirit than women, who are ordained by that same nature for a disposition of kindness and a gentle life, rather than for powerful rule. Therefore, it is no wonder if God's wrath and judgment are stricter upon proud women when they overstep the bounds of their frailty <note>original: *blödikeÿt*. In this context, it refers to the perceived social and physical "weakness" or "frailty" assigned to women in medieval thought, rather than a lack of intelligence.</note>, as happened to the foolish Niobe.

She was deceived by the flickering wheel of fortune and did not consider that the gift of her beautiful children was not from herself, but from nature, by which the courses of the heavens are guided—not according to the will of man, but according to its own providence. Therefore, she should have rightly offered honor and thanks to God for such gifts, rather than deeming herself worthy of divine honor, as if she possessed the gift of children from herself and not from God. And because she acted ignorantly in her burning pride, nature ordained that she should weep and lament her misfortune while alive, and that after her death, her name should remain hateful to mankind in future times.

**H**ypsipyle was a highly renowned woman, both for the kindness she showed to her father and for the tragic misery and wretched death of the child of the King of Nemea <note>This refers to Opheltes, the infant son of King Lycurgus of Nemea. At that time, a fierce rage had possessed the minds of the women there; they no longer wished to be subject to men in any way. Therefore, in contempt of the power and rule of the old King, they drew Hypsipyle into their company. With a united counsel, they reached the decision that on the following night, they would kill all the men with weapons. This was indeed carried out according to their plan by all the women, except for Hypsipyle alone. She considered a more virtuous path and reflected on how inhuman it would be to defile her hands with her father’s blood. And without doubt, the most virtuous deed to be praised far above others is the gentleness of children toward those who gave them life <note>original: *geberern*, a term for parents or progenitors</note>. For what is more fitting, what more just, what more laudable, and what more human than to repay those with honor and kindness from whom we, when young and helpless, received nourishment? There is no doubt of this.

Since all these things were considered by Hypsipyle and diligently offered to her father, she is not unjustly placed among the number of illustrious women <note>The author refers to the *De Mulieribus Claris* (Of Famous Women) by Boccaccio, which this book translates and adapts.</note>.

He was kindly received by the queen, not only into her lodging but also into her bedchamber as a lover. After his departure, she gave birth to and raised two sons by him.

In the ensuing chaos, **she was taken away from him by the army** <note>original: *gē...*; the text suggests she was seized by a passing force or pirates while fleeing the vengeful women</note> **and led away so that her life was saved** <note>original: *vnd hin gefüret darmit jr leben gefristet ward*</note>. # The 23rd leaf
### ¶ Concerning Medea, the Queen of Colchis, Chapter 16.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts Medea and Jason fleeing on horseback. Medea holds the severed head of her brother, Absyrtus. Labels within the woodcut identify "Medea," "Jason," and "Absyrtus," with the latter name written vertically next to the head. This gruesome scene represents the moment Medea slowed her father's pursuit by scattering the remains of her brother.</note>

**M**edea, one of the most wickedly cunning ancient sorceresses and a raging destroyer, was a daughter of Aeetes, the most illustrious king in Colchis <note>original: *Colchon*; an ancient kingdom on the coast of the Black Sea</note>, born to his wife Perse <note>original: *Persa*; in mythology, she is often called Idyia or Perseis</note>. She was sufficiently beautiful and far surpassed all others in the art of sorcery. Because she had masters in whatever she desired, the power of herbs was well known to her, more so than to all other people. Through this and her magical incantations <note>original: *zauber segen*</note> she could darken the sky, draw winds forth from their caves, create storms, and bring flowing waters to a standstill. She also knew how to brew potions to sway the mind <note>original: *gemüt zebewögē*; likely referring to love potions or mind-altering drafts</note> and could set everything ablaze with a passionate fire. And—what was much worse—her temperament was equal to her dark arts; for whatever she knew how to do, she dared to fulfill through her deeds. Whenever men were too weak to fight in wars, it was easy for her to create a warlike army through her many similar arts. At that time, there was a youth in Thessaly <note>a region in northern Greece</note> who was highly esteemed for the nobility and strength of his spirit.

<summary>This page introduces Medea of Colchis, describing her as a powerful and dangerous sorceress capable of controlling the elements and the human mind. It sets the stage for her encounter with Jason by detailing her royal lineage and her mastery over the occult.</summary>

<keywords>Medea, Jason, Colchis, sorcery, Aeetes, Perse, mythology, potions, incantations, Thessaly</keywords>

Through this, she had to accomplish her third flight and came back to Thessaly, where she also regained the renewed friendship of Jason.

The .24th. leaf.

Yet not long after, she was driven out of all Thessaly along with Jason by Aegialeus, who was a son of Pelias. For this reason, she returned home to Colchis and restored her old, poor father <note>King Aeetes, whom she had previously betrayed to help Jason</note> back to his kingdom. However, where she finally went or in what manner she ended her life is not in my memory, nor have I ever read or heard of it.

Lest I overlook this notable point, it should be remembered that the eyes should not be permitted to see everything. For through sight, we encounter outward appearances, by which we are moved to lustful inclination or hateful envy. Because of this, following our own whims, we praise beauty while unjustly condemning filth and poverty. And since the eyes are unlearned judges, they judge only by the appearance of what is seen and are often so greatly deceived that they prefer shameful things over the worthy, fictions over the truth, and anxieties over joys—praising that which should be scolded.

...one should press them <note>The eyes</note> toward the earth before one sees anything by which one might be turned toward evil. For this reason, nature has also given them a covering <note>The eyelids</note>, not only so that they are closed in sleep, but also so they might resist harmful sights. For if Medea had closed her eyes or turned them elsewhere when she looked upon Jason with such inner desire, her father’s power would have lasted longer, her brother's life <note>Absyrtus, whom Medea killed to delay her father's pursuit</note> would have remained longer, and her chaste virtue would have stayed whole and unbroken; all these things perished through the unchaste glance of the eyes.

->The 25th leaf<-

**A**rachne of Asia <note>original: "Ragnes von Asia"</note> was of the common people, the daughter of a cloth-dyer from Colophon <note>An ancient Greek city in Ionia, famous in antiquity for its production of expensive resins and dyes.</note>. Although she was not of high birth, she is nonetheless to be highly praised for her various merits. Some of the ancients say of her that she, through her high ingenuity, was the first to invent the weaving of linen and all its necessary tools. She also devised the knitting of bird-nets, fishing-nets, and the like. And as her mind devised what is called spinning <term>spinning: the process of twisting raw fibers—like wool or flax—into thread or yarn before it can be woven on a loom</term> to assist her weaving, she produced with those and other instruments such artistic work of all kinds of imagery, so arranged with colors as if a painter had applied them with a brush—which was indeed a laudable wisdom for a woman. For this reason, some wished to give her the prize above all others who lived in those times in such art. She was also highly praised and renowned throughout the whole world for such work, and because of this, she became so exalted in her own mind that she there... <note>The sentence continues on the following page, likely describing her hubris in challenging the goddess Minerva.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Arachne of Colophon, describing her humble origins as a dyer's daughter and her legendary status as the inventor of spinning, linen weaving, and netting. Her skill is compared to that of a master painter, earning her worldwide fame and growing pride.</summary>

->The 26th leaf<-

...placed a rope around her neck, yet she was rescued from it by her friend <note>original: "freünde." In classical mythology, this refers to the goddess Minerva, who prevented Arachne’s suicide by transforming her into a spider.</note>. However, she spent the rest of her life from then on in idleness, without any work. Now let a man say what benefit can come from pride: Arachne <note>original: "Aragnes"</note> intended to bring all the honor and dignity of the whole world solely upon herself, for which reason she was brought low. Had she kept God in her heart—He who is the creator and giver of all things—and offered praise, thanks, and honor for the good bestowed upon her, He would have been gentler and more merciful toward her, opening His lap and pouring even more arts into her. 

Therefore, this example is well worth noting for all those who are gifted by God and nature above others: that they do not carry themselves with pride above others, so that they are not brought low. For it is as easy for God, the giver of all things, to take back as it is to give. And I would wish that Arachne were the only example given to us; but alas, there are many "Arachnes" now on earth, whose own self-knowledge I would heartily wish for, for the common good.

¶ "If you, O Boreas, are mindful enough of the abducted Orithyia." <note>Latin: "Si satis es rapte boreas memor orithie." A quotation from Ovid's *Heroides*, referring to the North Wind, Boreas, who forcibly carried off the Athenian princess Orithyia.</note>

### ¶ Concerning Orithyia and Antiope, Queens of Amazonia. Chapter 18.

**O**rithyia was the daughter of Marpesia <note>original: "Marsepie"</note>, and after her mother's death, she reigned together with Antiope <note>original: "Anthiobe"</note>, who some believe was her sister. Yet Orithyia was highly praised above all others for her eternal chastity. She achieved so much in wars alongside her co-regent Antiope that she greatly expanded the kingdom of the Amazons <term>Amazons: in Greek mythology, a nation of formidable women warriors who lived on the edge of the known world</term> with great honor and power. She also increased her renown through her knightly deeds...

## The <unclear>28th</unclear> leaf

of her prophecy were found. The merit of her intellect, her prayer, and her devotion was so great in the sight of God that she—through divine grace and the influence of the Holy Spirit—prophesied so much and so clearly that her words might better be called a Gospel than a prophecy. She also prophesied to the Greeks, because of their diligent entreaties, in a beautiful poem <note>This likely refers to the "Sibylline Oracles," a collection of Greek oracular verses.</note> regarding all their future sorrows and finally the destruction of Troy, as loudly and clearly as if all subsequent history had been seen plainly beforehand.

In the same way, she encompassed the power of the Romans and their various future histories in a few verses, many years before the founding of the city. And what in my opinion is even greater is that she clearly revealed what the prophets had prophesied in hidden figures through the Holy Spirit. She revealed the mystery of the Word that became flesh <note>A reference to the Incarnation of Christ as described in John 1:14.</note>, the life of the only-begotten Son and his miracles; how he would be betrayed, mocked, captured, and shamefully killed; and how on the third day he would rise again in victory and triumph; his ascension; and finally the future judgment of all people. She prophesied all of this so clearly that it seemed not like a prophecy, but rather a description of things already passed.

Through such merit, I believe she was exceedingly dear to God and is highly venerable above all other pagan women. Some also write of her that she remained in eternal chastity, which is also easy to believe; for from a defiled heart, so much light of the prophecy of future things could not have easily shone forth. But at what time or where she died has been worn away by age <note>The author uses a phrase meaning the information has been lost or eroded over time.</note>.

### Of Medusa, Chapter 20

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the hero Perseus on a winged horse labeled "Perseus." Nearby, a man labeled "Neptune" (the god of the sea) embraces a woman labeled "Medusa." In classical mythology, Medusa's beauty—particularly her hair—attracted the sea god Neptune, leading to the tragic events that resulted in her transformation into a monster.</note>

**M**edusa was the daughter and heir of the wealthiest King Phorcys, who ruled the most prosperous kingdom situated in the Atlantic Sea. Some say these were the Hesperides islands <note>In Greek mythology, the Hesperides were the "Nymphs of the Evening" who tended a blissful garden in the far west of the world, near the Atlas Mountains.</note>. This same Medusa was of such wondrous beauty that she alone surpassed all others in her lovely grace. Indeed, she moved many people to seek her out just to gaze upon her beauty, and she was esteemed by many as lovely beyond the capacity of nature. 

Her head was adorned with gold-colored hair, and she possessed a face of singular beauty and a body of graceful stature. Among other things, the power of her eyes was so lovely and potent that all men who looked upon her kindly were transfixed by her <note>The original German *erglasten* literally means "to shine" or "to glow," suggesting the men were dazzled or "fired up" by her gaze, which rendered them motionless.</note>, standing as if they were immovable, having forgotten themselves and their senses.

<summary>This page introduces the legend of Medusa, describing her not as a monster, but as a beautiful heiress and daughter of King Phorcys whose beauty was so great it left men transfixed.</summary>
<keywords>Medusa, King Phorcys, Perseus, Neptune, Atlantic Sea, Hesperides, beauty, gold-colored hair</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Medusa, transitioning from her physical beauty and reputation to a historical "euhemerist" explanation of her myth—interpreting the supernatural elements of her story as metaphors for her wealth, her ships, and her eventual downfall at the hands of Perseus.</meta>

...ignorant. But it is found of her that she knew much more of the world than others; for this reason, she was called Gorgon <note>The author provides a historical rather than mythological origin for the name, suggesting it relates to her great knowledge.</note>. Through this, and with her great wisdom and reason, she not only preserved her father’s estate in good standing but also greatly increased it, so much so that with her treasury, she surpassed the wealth of all kings in the West. Thus, she was famed in the furthest lands for her wondrous beauty, her immense riches, and her far-reaching reason and wisdom.

However, the great praise of her was brought before the Greeks. Among them was Perseus, who was more elegant than all others and a flower of charming youth. From hearing such reports, he fell into so great a desire to see this most beautiful woman and to take her treasures that he set sail with a prepared fleet toward the West. He sailed in a ship that was marked with a flying horse <note>The author rationalizes the mythical winged horse Pegasus as a figurehead or heraldic symbol on Perseus's ship.</note> and arrived there with great speed. And with reckless boldness and a strong, armed hand, he attacked the queen, burdened his ships with her gold and many spoils, and traveled back home to his own people.

From this history, poets were given the occasion to invent fables, such as those we read: how Medusa, through sorcery, turned men who looked upon her into stone, and how Minerva, moved to anger against her, transformed her hair into snakes because she had entered her temple to lie with the holy Neptune <note>The original German uses "beyligen," which can mean "to lie with" or "consort." In classical myth, Medusa was punished by Minerva after being raped by Neptune in the goddess's temple.</note>. From this union, she bore a flying horse, upon which Perseus, flying with his people across the sea, came to her land and overcame her with sword and shield.

O you wretched gold! Where you lie hidden from your master, you are useless. But if you were brought, shining, to the light of day, you would give rise to so much envy and desire that you would cause a thousandfold sorrow.

<summary>This page provides a historical interpretation of the Medusa myth, characterizing her as a wealthy, wise queen whose "monstrous" traits were metaphors for her power and her eventual defeat by the Greek hero Perseus.</summary>

world. And since Eurytus had promised her to him as his wife <note>original: "tzege maheln"</note> through the advice of his son, he did not wish to keep his word to him. Because of this, he [Hercules] waged a great war against him, and took from him his land, his people, and also his life. And he led away with him his beloved Iole. But the death of her father lay ever more heavily on Iole’s mind than any love for the one by whom he had been killed; and she became eager for revenge. Yet she was able to hide this so well with such wonderful displays of feigned love, with such pleasure and cleverness, and with so much flattery—with so much unchaste enticement to love—that she completely inflamed Hercules and set him so ablaze with passion that he could no longer perceive her ill intentions. Instead, he believed that whatever she might desire from him, she should be granted it. Immediately, she acted as if she felt some horror at his rough clothing and the wildness of his nature, and she commanded him first that he lay down the club with which he had tamed the wild and fearsome beasts. After that, she ordered him to take off his clothing, which was a skin he had stripped from a lion in the Nemean forest <note>The Nemean lion was the first of Hercules's twelve labors; its hide was impenetrable to normal weapons.</note>, which was a visible sign of his strength. Following this, he laid down the little wreath made of white poplar branches <note>original: "alberczweig"</note>, which had been placed upon him as a sign of victory. She also left neither quiver nor arrows upon him, which he had used so much. Yet this did not seem enough to her; instead, she constantly considered how she might further approach the unarmed man in a playful manner. And with great premeditation, she boldly launched her assault, first adorning his hands with most costly rings of gold and precious stones. Afterward, she had his rough head—disheveled with tight curls <note>original: "mor löcken," likely referring to dark, "Moorish" curls</note>—brushed, arranged, and combed, and his hair anointed with sweet-smelling salves.

# The 30th Leaf

->The 30th leaf.<-

And she adorned his face and placed upon his head feminine ornaments—veils, caps, and brooches <note>original: "gebend/hauben vñ spengen"; these were typical head-dressings for noblewomen in the late medieval and early Renaissance periods.</note> belonging to young women—and she dressed his body in feminine clothes made of purple and other costly fabrics, which women were accustomed to wearing in their soft lives. And her calculated purpose <note>original: "fürbetrachte meinung"</note> was to see if she could transform such a strong hero from his manhood into a soft life and a feminine nature; through this, she sought to hunt for greater praise and honor, and to be better avenged for her father. For it would be better than if he had been killed by her with a sword, as might be expected by anyone. Yet she also believed that her resentment against him was thereby sufficiently satisfied. 

She brought the effeminate man to such levity that he sat among other women with feminine gestures and appearance, recounting his "work"—that is, the exceedingly great deeds of manhood he had performed. He even spun the wool from the distaff <note>A distaff is a tool used in spinning to hold the unspun fibers. In classical art, Hercules is often depicted with Omphale or Iole holding a distaff as a symbol of his domestic subjection.</note>, twisted the thread, and softened his fingers for feminine labor. These were the same strong fingers that, while he was still lying in his cradle, had been hardened for manhood when he crushed the two serpents <note>According to myth, the goddess Hera sent two snakes to kill the infant Hercules in his crib, but he strangled them with his bare hands.</note>. 

Whoever looks upon these things and understands them correctly will find how great the frailty <note>original: "blödigkeit"; in this context, it refers to moral or physical weakness/susceptibility rather than shyness.</note> of men is, and how endless the desires of women are, through which the strongest man was softened. Thus, Iole’s revenge for her father was completed through pleasure, more effectively than any man could have ever done with a sword or had done before, resulting in an eternal tarnishing <note>original: "vermeilgung"; a staining or diminishing of his glorious reputation.</note> of Hercules’s praise. Therefore, Iole earned a place in memory with everlasting praise. For she effectively triumphed over the conqueror of the world and of wild, ferocious beasts with her full power and strong hand, without any resistance. This

<summary>Iole completes her revenge by dressing Hercules in women’s clothing and forcing him to perform feminine domestic tasks like spinning wool. The text reflects on how feminine "desire" can conquer even the strongest man, suggesting Iole’s psychological victory was greater than a military one.</summary>

# The 30th Leaf

->The 30th leaf.<-

This heavy and impure sickness <note>The author refers here to "lovesickness" or lust, viewed in the Middle Ages as a physical and spiritual malady.</note> also commonly afflicts those young women who are raised in soft and excessively delicate surroundings. It likewise strikes idle, softly-reared youths. For Venus’s child, Cupid, is a sworn enemy of all work, of all strength of spirit, of all reason and wisdom; he seeks only to keep company with a soft life, with pleasure, joy, and amorous flattery. Therefore, that this same child could slip into the exceedingly hard heart of Hercules is a much greater wonder than all the labors he ever accomplished by his own hand <note>original: "p̈e" (likely an abbreviation for *proben* or *per se*); the author suggests Cupid's conquest of Hercules was more miraculous than Hercules' Twelve Labors.</note>. Therefore, this story should be no small example to those who wish to consider their own salvation; for every person may well be terrified by it, since we see these enemies standing before our eyes at all times. Because of this, we should strengthen our hearts and build strong breastworks <term>defensive earthworks or parapets</term> against it, and resist the very first assault. For wherever Cupid feels resistance, he loses his strength. First, tame the eyes so they do not look upon vanity; stop the ears like the adder before the charmer <note>A reference to Psalm 58, which describes the "deaf adder" that stops its ears to avoid the charmer’s song; a common medieval metaphor for resisting temptation.</note> so that shameful words are not heard; and overcome the laziness of the body with hard work. For this child presents himself to all the world as friendly and flattering; at first glance, he is pleasing and welcome to everyone. In his first entry, he is received with desire and beauty, and he gives counsel to adorn the body, to maintain good manners, and to have an honorable bearing. He suggests good conduct, speaking and singing pleasant words, proverbs, and songs, joy, pastimes, dainties <term>refined sweets or delicacies</term>, and the seeking out and devising of many such things. But when he sees a person confirmed and established in such folly, and the free spirit now subjugated, then he binds them with hard bonds and stirs up sighing and lamenting in the night, when one should rest...

<summary>This page provides a moral commentary on the myth of Hercules and Iole, warning that idle and "soft" upbringings lead to the "sickness" of lust (Cupid). It advises readers to resist the first signs of temptation through hard work and by guarding their senses.</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Venus, Hercules, morality, lovesickness, idleness, temptation, virtue, Early New High German literature</keywords>

And it creates various chastenings <note>Here, "chastenings" or *kestigung* refers to the self-inflicted distress and mental torment caused by obsession.</note> of the mind arising from suspicion and many a ruse; it leads one to be reckless, considering not whether a deed be good or evil, so long as it may fulfill its own will. And whoever should offer any kind of interference or resistance against it would be written by him <note>Referring to the personification of Desire or Cupid.</note> into the book of enemies. 

It is also such that for fervently inflamed people, no effort is too much and no labor burdens them. They never grow weary, neither from running here nor there, so long as they are in pursuit of that which they desire. And as often as they catch a glimpse of it, the burning fire of the heart becomes even more fervent. And wherever the stronghold of their will <note>The author uses a military metaphor, comparing the beloved or the beloved's resolve to a fortified city or "stat" (place/state).</note> refuses to surrender, then the tears come. And in that place, copper is sought <note>Likely a reference to the use of money or low-value coins to bribe servants or messengers.</note>, many gifts are promised, and many are also given. For those hearts which cannot be softened by spirit or by gifts are harder than marble stone. 

Through gifts, one becomes companionable; gifts subvert all justice, and the protectors and guards are easily deceived by them. Thus, the lovers—through a willing contempt for their guardians—come together in unseemly lusts. There, the redness of feminine shame <note>The "blush" of modesty.</note> is laid aside, and the counselor of sin is received. The garment of honor is stripped off, and a shameful entanglement of impurity is embraced—likened to the swine who wallow in the filth. There, moderation is cast away. **Ceres** <note>The Roman goddess of grain; here representing gluttony or excessive eating.</note> and **Bacchus** <note>The Roman god of wine; here representing drunkenness.</note>, the gods of pleasure, are called upon and honored along with the fervent **Venus** <note>The goddess of love; here representing lust.</note> both day and night, driven by stinking impurity. 

Through these acts, however, the burning rage of passion is not driven away, but is instead often increased unto madness. From this it follows that manly spirits often fall into feminine subservience, just as did the strongest **Hercules**. They forget their...

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the centaur Nessus carrying off Deianira across a river while Hercules stands on the bank aiming a bow at him. Labels within the image identify the characters: Nessus (labeled "nasus"), Deianira ("Dyanira"), and Hercules.</note>

# Concerning Deianira, the wife of Hercules: Chapter 22

**D**eianira was the daughter of Oeneus, the King of the Aetolians <note>original: *etholorū*</note>, and the sister of Meleager. She was endowed with such great beauty that a battle for her hand in marriage was promised and fought between Achelous <note>original: *atheolum*; in mythology, a river god who wrestled Hercules for Deianira</note> and Hercules, with the condition that she should be given as a wife to whichever of them emerged victorious. But when Hercules prevailed in the battle, Nessus the centaur was so moved to love for her by her beauty that he followed Hercules as he led her home from Calydon. 

And when they came to the river Evenus <note>original: *ebanum*</note>, they found it so swollen with floodwaters <note>original: *güßwasser*</note> that it was not easily crossed. Nessus, because he was a capable mount <note>original: *wolgeritten*; a play on the fact that as a centaur, Nessus is both rider and horse</note>, submissively offered his service to Hercules, saying he would gladly carry Deianira across the water on his back. When Hercules consented, intending to swim across after him, Nessus hurried across the water like one who had achieved his desire, and fled away with the woman his heart loved...

<summary>This page introduces Deianira, whose beauty causes a struggle between Hercules and Achelous, and recounts the beginning of her abduction by the centaur Nessus at the river Evenus.</summary>
<keywords>Deianira, Hercules, Nessus, Achelous, Aetolia, mythology, centaur, abduction</keywords>

# The 32nd Leaf

->The 32nd leaf<-

But Hercules realized that he could no longer catch him on foot. Therefore, he shot him with a flying arrow, tainted with poison <note>original: *mit gift entveinet*; referring to the venom of the Lernaean Hydra in which Hercules dipped his arrows</note>. When Nessus felt this and judged himself to be dying from the shot, he immediately gave Deianira his garment, which was stained by the blood from his wound, and said to her: "If you can manage to have Hercules put on these clothes, all his anger toward you will be taken away." Deianira believed these words and received the garment from him as a special gift. 

And she kept it hidden for some time. But when she noticed that Hercules loved Omphale or Iole <note>original: *onphalen oder yolem*; Iole was the daughter of Eurytus, and Hercules' obsession with her sparked Deianira's jealousy</note> so wretchedly, she sent him the garment secretly via his servant Lichas <note>original: *lica*</note>, in good will, to earn back his favor. But when he put it on, and from the warmth and moisture of his body the poisoned blood dissolved and became fluid, the poisonous vapors entered through his pores into him. From this, he became so driven to madness by the pain that he burned himself to death in a fire <note>referring to Hercules' self-immolation on Mount Oeta to end his suffering</note>. And so Deianira was placed in the widow’s chair, though she had intended only to bind her husband to her in love; and thus, Nessus was avenged.

¶ "He who has both parents as the cause of his crime is experienced in it." <note>original: *Expertus scelus est cuius uterqʒ parens*; a reference to the incestuous nature of Oedipus’s birth and marriage</note>
¶ Concerning Jocasta, Queen of the Thebans; the 19th Chapter.

**J**ocasta, the Queen of the Thebans, has remained in the memory of people more for her great misfortune than for any notable merit or strict rule. Her ancient lineage was from the illustrious founders of the city of Thebes, and she was married to Laius, the king of that place. By him, she conceived and bore a son. Yet she had to cast this same son out to be eaten by wild beasts by the command of her husband Laius, who had received such an answer from the gods <note>original: *antwort der göter*; referring to the prophecy from the Delphic Oracle</note> that he would be killed by his own son. Although the mother did this unwillingly, she nevertheless had him cast away out of obedience to her husband. And while she believed the beasts had torn him to pieces, he had instead been brought to the King of Corinth, who ordered him to be raised, nourished, and taught as his own child; and he was named Oedipus <note>original: *Edippus*</note>. As he grew, became practiced in knighthood, and grew strong, the king sent him out on a military expedition against the Phocians. By chance, he came to fight against his unrecognized father, Laius, and struck him dead. Afterward, he won the widow—his own mother—as his wife, with neither party knowing the other's identity. And she bore him two sons, Eteocles <note>original: *Ethioclem*</note> and Polynices <note>original: *Polinicem*</note>, and as many daughters, Ismene <note>original: *Ysmenam*</note> and Antigone <note>original: *Anthiogonam*</note>. And just as she now believed she had become entirely fortunate and blessed by the kingdom and her children, she one day desired an answer from the gods regarding her situation, and it was...

Although some say she could not endure such adversity so long, but rather as soon as she saw her son Oedipus cast out his own eyes, she also turned the sword upon herself, ending her life.

->The                                        34th                                        Leaf<-

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts King Tarquinius Priscus <note>original Latin: *tarquiniꝰ prisci*</note> seated on a throne to the left, gesturing toward the Sibyl Amalthea <note>original Latin: *Amalthea*</note> who stands on the right. This illustrates the legend in which the Sibyl offered nine books of prophecy to the king; when he refused the price, she burned them three at a time until he finally agreed to buy the remaining three for the original price of nine.</note>

**A**malthea the virgin—whom some call Deiphobe, the daughter of Glaucus—had her first origin in the ancient city of Cumae, located in Campania. She was also a Sibyl <note>a female prophet of the ancient world</note> and lived during the time of the destruction of Troy. She grew to such a great age that she lived until the time of Tarquinius Priscus, the Roman king, as many of the ancient wise men truly believe. And they say in addition that her chastity was so pure and clear that in all the many hundreds of years she lived, she was never defiled by any man. And although the poems of the poets say she was beloved by the god Apollo and was therefore gifted by him with long life and made holy, this is nonetheless to be understood naturally: that she was born under the best influences of the sun (for Apollo signifies the sun). Therefore, a good constitution <term>complexion; in medieval medicine, the "complexion" was the specific balance of the four humors that determined a person’s health and character</term> inclined toward great things was given to her. Into her, the true Sun <note>This is a common medieval Christian interpretation, suggesting that while the pagans saw "Apollo," the Sibyl was actually receiving divine light from the Christian God</note>—who enlightens all people of this world—poured a true light through which she recognized God and prophesied much; with His help, she desired to maintain eternal chastity. This same Sibyl had a particular dwelling near Babylon on the shore...

<summary>This page introduces the Sibyl Amalthea (the Cumaean Sibyl), detailing her origins in Campania, her legendary longevity spanning from the Fall of Troy to the reign of King Tarquinius Priscus, and her reputation for prophetic wisdom and lifelong chastity.</summary>

->The [unclear] 34th leaf<-

By Lake Avernus, where she revealed her divine prophecy and which keeps her name to this day. And although that same house of prayer <note>referring to the Sibyl’s cave or temple</note> has almost perished from great age and neglect, one can still perceive from the remaining ruins and the heap of boldly crafted stones in what great honor and majesty it once stood—so much so that those who see it to this day are seized with great wonder. Concerning the Sibyl, Virgil writes in the sixth book of the *Aeneid* <note>original: *von enea*</note> about where she leads him; I will leave that story in its own place <note>the author chooses not to recount the plot of Virgil’s epic here</note>. It is also written of her that she presented King <note>original: *keÿſer*</note> Tarquinius Priscus with nine books of her prophecies. When Tarquinius would not give what she demanded for them, she took three of the books and burned them before his face. The next day following, she asked him for the same price for the six remaining books as she had previously asked for the nine. And she said that if he did not give it to her, she would burn another three books before his face, and on the third day, the final ones. Thus Tarquinius gave her what she desired and took the books from her. In these books, those who came after found the entire history of the Romans clearly prophesied. Therefore, in future times, the Romans kept those same books with total diligence and in high honor. Whenever they desired to know their destiny <note>original: *beſcherung*</note>, they paid special attention to the sayings of those books, in the same manner as they would to an answer from the gods. It is found concerning her that she ended her final days in Sicily <note>original: *cecilia*</note>, where for many years afterward her grave was shown. From this you may observe that no human can become more illustrious through any matter than through diligent learning joined with divine grace. Both are denied to no one who makes themselves worthy of them; but whoever [wishes to]...

Whoever considers this finds how like a child the old, ignorant, and 
unskilled man is carried to his grave in a dull state <note>original: *tozother gestalt*, referring to a state of foolishness or stupor</note> because of his

was given to them by nature—that is, by God—to acquire 
such things, if only they wish to drive folly from themselves. 
Therefore, all men should rightly weep, mourn, and 
wither away in dejection, those from whom such a gift is 
withdrawn because of their stubborn ignorance. They 
should rightly judge themselves as immovable rocks among 
living people, as will happen when they must silently 
confess their own wrongdoing.

...of Arcadia, wedded; others say she was his son’s wife. This same woman appeared illustrious not only because of her royal lineage, but also because she was so skilled in the Greek tongue. She practiced such high wisdom and arts at all times that she was celebrated for her prophecies and held to be a prophetess above all others of that time. And whenever anyone desired her to speak of such matters, she gave her answer through beautifully composed speeches, which in Latin are called songs <note>original: *Carmina*; referring to verses, poems, or ritual songs, from which the name Carmenta is derived</note>. For this reason, her name Nicostrata was forgotten, and she was called Carmenta. 

She was the mother of Evander, the king of Arcadia. The ancients say that the god Mercury was his father; however, one should understand this correctly—not that you should think the gods performed impure acts with women, but rather that such children were born under the best influence of the planet Mercury <note>original: *mercurij*</note>. <margin>44</margin>

->The 36th leaf<-

...the city of Pallanteum <note>original: *pallantina*; the settlement founded by Evander on the Palatine Hill, named after his Arcadian home</note>, in which he lived with his mother for a long time. He had it built from the ground up, on the site where many years later the mighty city of Rome was established. However, when Carmenta found the inhabitants of that same land to be rough, coarse, and ignorant of how to live in a civilized, human way—living instead like beasts, very nearly like wild animals—she sought to improve them. Even though Saturn <note>referring to the myth of the god Saturn fleeing to Italy and teaching the people agriculture during the "Golden Age"</note>, who had come to them as a fugitive from the land of Greece long before, had given them some instruction in the cultivation of grain <note>original: *kozen bawes*; likely a form of *Kornbau*, or agriculture</note>, they still lacked any knowledge of Greek writing, or had very little familiarity with it. 

She recognized in her divine spirit, considering things far into the future, that it was only right that the great glory, history, power, and wonders of this city and region—which were ordained by God—should be recorded in a foreign language and script for the sake of future times. Through diligent exercise of her innermost reason and with all her might, she set about devising a unique language and script for the people, entirely distinct from all others. God was also supportive of her undertaking, for by His grace it was accomplished. 

She also created the letters through which she taught the people the Latin language; and at first, there were no more than sixteen letters. This was the same number that Cadmus <note>the legendary Phoenician prince who introduced the alphabet to the Greeks</note>, the founder of the city of Thebes, had discovered for the Greeks many years before. These same letters are still used by the Romans and by us to this very day, and they are called Latin letters. They were given to us by her, although some of the ancient wise men, for good reasons, have added more letters to them since then. 

And although this lady's prophecies were seen as miraculous by everyone, the invention of these letters and the language was held in such high and great esteem by all the people in Italy that the coarse folk did not believe Carmenta was a human being, but rather a goddess sent to them from heaven...

<summary>This page credits Carmenta with the civilizing of the early inhabitants of Italy and the invention of the Latin alphabet, which she originally based on sixteen letters. Her contribution was so significant that the local people eventually deified her.</summary>

<keywords>Carmenta, Evander, Pallanteum, Rome, Saturn, Latin alphabet, Cadmus, history of writing, Italian mythology</keywords>
