...sent to teach. Therefore, after her death—even though they had shown her divine honors during her lifetime—they had a temple built in her name at the very base of the Capitoline Hill, where she had long ago made her dwelling, for her everlasting memory. And the surrounding estates were named the Carmentalia after her name. Because the city of Rome, which by then had grown great, did not wish this to be forgotten, when the need arose for the Romans to place a gate at that same end of the city, they called it the Carmentalin Gate <note>original: *Carmentalem portam*</note> after her name, which it retained for many years. 

The land of Italy and afterwards its capital Rome once flourished with great gifts above all other cities in the world and shone, as one might say, with a heavenly light. For from Asia came the greatest wealth, royal furnishings, and nobility of blood. And although significant growth was contributed by the Greeks, the very beginning came there from Troy. The Egyptians provided the arts of Arithmetic and Geometry. Philosophy and Rhetoric—which is the art of orderly speech—and nearly all crafts were taken from the Greeks. But agriculture <note>original: *baw*; here referring to the "tillage" or cultivation of the earth</note>, which was known to few people at that time, was first brought by Saturn, who had been driven there as an exile from the land of Greece. The unholy <note>original: *unſälige*; likely reflecting the 15th-century Christian author's view of pagan worship as "unblessed" or "wretched"</note> worship and invocation of the gods first arose from the Etruscans and came from Numa Pompilius <note>The second king of Rome, traditionally credited with establishing Roman religious institutions</note>. Common laws were first given by the people of Athens, and afterwards the supreme authorities and emperors expanded and established them according to the requirements of the times. The highest priesthood and the spiritual life according to the right order originated from...

# The 38th Leaf

...and [she] could not be called back, nor had any infidelity been planned by her <note>This suggests that while she succumbed to the bribe, she did not intend to be a "faithless" person by nature.</note>. Therefore, she resisted his desire and fled from there over rocks, mountains, and valleys through the wilderness. And Cephalus followed her diligently with a hunting spear <note>original: "jäger spye"</note> to bring her back to him. And it happened by chance that Procris had hidden herself near a marsh in a thicket of wild reeds; and when Cephalus saw from a distance that the reeds were moving, he thought a wild animal had hidden itself there. In the hope of hitting it, he shot Procris to death; and so she perished.

What shall we say? I do not know if pitch <note>The author uses "peches" (pitch/tar), referencing the biblical proverb that "he who touches pitch shall be defiled," comparing the "stain" of greed to the physical stickiness of tar.</note> is more powerful on earth than gold. I also cannot imagine that a greater folly could be seen than when a person seeks after that which, once found and acquired, may only bring regret. Both of these things this frivolous woman has well proven; for she stained herself with an indelible mark for all eternity, and also sought the death which she found, though she had desired it little. 

But leaving aside the immeasurable desire for gold, to which all fools are drawn, I ask those who are hardened in such desire to tell me what profit, what ornament, what praise, or what glory they receive from it. Truly, according to my knowledge, such desire is nothing other than a shameful sickness of the soul, sprung from frivolity; and it is found only among those who can carelessly endure having all other people—whoever they may be—placed in higher standing than themselves. <note>The author argues that greed destroys one's social honor and "glory," making the greedy person lower in status than any honest person.</note>

<note>A metal clip is visible at the top right, holding the page. The left edge shows the binding of the book. There is some bleed-through of text from the reverse side of the page.</note>

<summary>Procris flees into the wild to escape her shame but is accidentally killed by Cephalus, who mistakes her for an animal in the reeds. The author concludes with a moral warning against the corrupting power of gold and the folly of greed.</summary>

<keywords>Procris, Cephalus, tragic death, hunting accident, greed, gold, moral philosophy, shame, infidelity</keywords>

-> Of Argia, wife of Polynices <-
-> the 27th chapter. <-

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a man and a woman standing over several fallen bodies. Above the man is the label "Polynices" and above the woman is the label "Argia." Argia the Greek was of the most ancient, noble, and royal lineage sprung from that same land, and she was the daughter of King Adrastus. She was a woman of desirable beauty to behold; and just as her appearance moved many to joy, so she also left to posterity an eternal, exceptionally clear, and true testimony of right matrimonial love <note>The author uses the term "gemahelschafft" to denote the bond and duties of marriage.</note>. And until our own time, her noble name has shone with prominent luster, remaining in great memory and honored. 

She was given in marriage to Polynices, the son of Oedipus, King of the Thebans, who had been driven out from his home. By him, she also bore a son named Thersander. # The 39th Leaf

[The text continues from the previous page, describing King Adrastus's state of mind] ...was very troubled. She <note>Argia</note> considered his anxieties and tribulations, wishing also to have compassion and to share in them. And she comforted her old father and strengthened him with tears and prayer to march against Eteocles, who at that same time, through his own power and against all right, ruled the kingdom of the Thebans <note>original: "küngreich thebanorū"</note> fearsomely alongside his brother. And so that these things might proceed as they were foreseen to happen through the answers of the gods <note>A reference to the oracles and prophecies surrounding the Seven Against Thebes.</note>, and not be diminished, she wished to be seen as generous beyond the common nature of women. 

And so, unasked, she gave to Eurydice <note>The text names her Eurydice, though in most classical accounts of this myth, the wife of the seer is named Eriphyle. She was bribed with the Necklace of Harmonia to convince her husband to join the war.</note>, the wife of the seer Amphiaraus, that precious jewel which had once been so unlucky for the women of Thebes. Through this gift, Amphiaraus, who had been lying in hiding, was revealed by his wife. And because of his prophecy, he was drawn toward Thebes with a great army <note>original: "höres kufft," likely a variation of "Heereskraft," meaning a large military force.</note>, yet with great misfortune. 

For after an immense slaughter, wherein other captains had perished and Adrastus was stripped of all help and turned to flight, the dead body of Polynices <note>original: "polinitis"</note> remained lying among other stinking corpses upon the field, unburied. And when his grieving wife <note>The text uses "haußfraw," literally "house-wife," but in this courtly context meaning his spouse or queen.</note> heard of this, she rose up from the royal hall and threw off her shining raiment and soft clothes. Laying aside womanly weakness, she went with few companions to the <term>walstatt</term> <note>A traditional Germanic term for a battlefield, specifically one strewn with the bodies of the slain.</note> of the conflict. She allowed neither enemies nor any kind of horrors to frighten her; and what is even greater, she was not deterred by the great command of King Creon. Creon, who had held the victory, had forbidden—upon pain of beheading—anyone to go onto the battlefield or to bury the fallen. Yet she did not let this lead her astray; rather, she went fervently and with such a sad heart at midnight to the place of the slain, stinking corpses, and turned them over...

-> The 40th Leaf <-

...then another, for so long until, with a **small light**, she recognized the **miserable face** of her most beloved husband. O, wonderful deed of this woman! The face, wounded by weapons, defiled by the filth of the earth and the dust, and covered in foul sweat—so much so that he was unrecognizable to almost everyone—could not hide itself from his most faithful spouse. The impurity of his soiled face could not drive away her affectionate kisses. Nor could the decree of Creon <note>Creon, the new King of Thebes, had decreed that the bodies of the fallen invaders should remain unburied as a punishment—an order Argia bravely defies.</note> prevent her from fulfilling her will. For after she desired to move the soul back into the body with her many passionate embraces and kisses upon his mouth, and showered all his limbs with tears so that she might omit no kindness toward him, she laid the dead corpse into the fire prepared for that purpose. And after his burning, she gathered the ashes into an urn <note>original: "krug," literally a jar or jug</note> and had him committed to the earth with royal honors according to their custom.

It has often happened that women have wept for their husbands' sickness, imprisonment, poverty, and various misfortunes, yet always in the hope of better fortune for which they waited, and an end to the misfortune. Although that is also to be praised, it cannot give such testimony of the highest love as the service Argia showed her husband. For she went onto the battlefield <term>walstat<note>A Middle High German term for a battlefield, derived from "valr" (the slain) and "statt" (place).</note></term> when she might well have mourned her husband in the safety of her house. She turned over the stinking corpses with her own hands, a task she could easily have commanded others to do. She had him laid to rest with royal honors by fire, even though, given the circumstances and the time, burying him in the earth would have been enough. She wept for him with wailing and cries, which she might well have passed over in silence; and yet there was no word of profit to her from him...

## Of Manto, the 28th Chapter

<note>A woodcut depicts a woman labeled "Mantho" kneeling in prayer or performing a prophetic ritual before a fire. Behind her, two demonic figures are visible, one of whom is riding a goat-like beast, symbolizing the supernatural or "dark" nature of her divination skills.</note>

**Manto** was a daughter of Tiresias <note>Tiresias was a famous blind prophet of Apollo in Thebes, known in mythology for his long life and for having lived as both a man and a woman.</note>, the greatest soothsayer of the city of Thebes. She was held in high regard during the times of King Oedipus and his sons. She was also of such perceptive mind and reason that she learned <term>pyromancy<note>original: "piromanciam," the art of divination by observing flames or fire.</note></term>—that is, the art of soothsaying from fire—so completely from her father that in her time no one was found [who surpassed her]. This art was discovered in years of old by the Chaldeans, as some believe, while others say it was found by King Nimrod <note>Nimrod, a biblical figure and great-grandson of Noah, was traditionally associated with the Tower of Babel and the origin of various ancient sciences.</note>.

...who, through the movement of the flames, from their color, from the crackling, and through many other signs—I know not by what devilish assistance—could more effectively foretell future things. Furthermore, the entrails of sheep and bulls, and of all other animals, were so well known to her that she, through these and other helps and arts—as is said of her—often compelled the **unclean spirits** to come to her to speak and give answers to her questions. 

And after the Greek kings, who lay before the city to conquer it, were slain, and King Creon had undertaken to rule the city with great force, she fled from the new king and came into that part of the world called **Asia** <note>In this context, Asia refers to Asia Minor, specifically the region of Ionia.</note>. There, she was also the first to establish the temple of the idol <term>Clarian Apollo<note>original: "Clarÿ appollinis," referring to a famous sanctuary and oracle of Apollo at Claros.</note></term>, which thereafter was most sumptuously built and honored. In that same place, she gave birth to **Mopsus**, the greatest soothsayer, whom we call prophets or seers when they foretell something from the divine heart of our faith <note>The author is likely contrasting Manto’s pagan divination with Christian prophecy, suggesting that while her skills were great, they lacked the "divine heart" of the author's own religion.</note>; yet one does not find from what father she bore him.

But some others say that after the Theban war, she wandered about for a long time with several others and finally came to **Italy**, where she was wedded to **Tiberinus** <note>The personification or god of the Tiber River.</note>. By him, she conceived and gave birth to a son, **Cythau** <note>Also known in other traditions as Ocnus.</note>, who was called **Bianor** by some. With him, she came out over the mountains into **Lombardy** and **Venetia**, toward **Benacum** <note>The Latin name for Lake Garda.</note>, the watery land by our lake; for she believed that same place to be suitable for her activities. And because she spent the remainder of her time there, she had a dwelling built on piles and supports in the middle of the marsh, where she was also buried after her death. And at that place, her son Cythau had **Mantua** built and named after her; in which city, to this day, marquises rule. 

Yet there are some who truly believe...

### ¶ Concerning the wives of the youths called Minyans, Chapter 29.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts three women in medieval attire labeled "Menie" (Minyans) in the foreground. **T**he names and the number of these same women have been worn away by age, so that they are no longer named by the scribes. This is indeed unjust, for through their deeds they have well deserved to be placed among the highly illustrious women. And although the envious **wheel of fortune** <note>A common medieval trope (Rota Fortunae) representing the unpredictable nature of fate.</note> has allowed their names to pass away into obscurity, they should nevertheless, according to our ability, be adorned and honored for their praiseworthy deeds, and be brought out of oblivion into the memory of posterity because of their merit. 

The <term>Minyan youths<note>original: "Menie," referring to the descendants of the Argonauts who settled in Lemnos and later Laconia.</note></term> were not of the least, but of the highest nobility, having been illustrious members of the company of **Jason** and the **Argonauts**. Having arrived, they abandoned their old dwellings and chose to establish their lives among the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans.</note>. They were not only received warmly and in a friendly manner, but were even made participants in all power and accepted among the rulers of the common good <note>original: "gemeinen nucz," referring to the state or public welfare.</note>. At that time, they were exceedingly wealthy and highly noble, not only by paternal blood but also because they had married into the most eminent families of the Lacedaemonians. Their nobility was doubly illuminated; for among other things, they had the most beautiful wives from the noblest families of Lacedaemonian citizens—though worldly adornment is usually considered of the least value. Furthermore, many of the common folk entered into their service. They failed to consider that all of this had happened to them through the grace of the public authorities, but instead believed it was due to their own merit. Therefore, they fell into a foolish desire to rule and imprudently attempted to seize all the power in the land.

For this misdeed, when it was discovered, the powerful authorities threw them into a harsh prison and sentenced them to death by the supreme court as declared enemies of the common good. It was decreed that this sentence should be carried out the very next night, according to the ancient custom of the Lacedaemonians. When their sorrowful wives heard of this, they went to work and devised an unheard-of means of help to show their condemned husbands, and they carried out in deed what they had planned. They put on wretched clothes, covered their weeping faces, and when the sun had set, they came to the dungeon, desiring to see their dying husbands. And because they were the most noble women of the city, this was easily granted to them by the guards. However, once they had gone inside to them, they drove away the...

Thus, the young men went out of the prison in the women’s clothing, weeping, with sorrowful gestures and their eyes cast down toward the earth. To this end, the darkness of the night was also helpful to them, as was the dignity <note>In this context, "dignity" (original: *würdikeyt*) refers to the social standing and perceived honor of the high-born women, which made the guards less likely to suspect them of trickery.</note> of the women, by which the guards were easily deceived, letting the condemned men out to their deaths <note>The guards allowed them to pass, thinking they were the wives leaving the execution site.</note> and keeping the women in their place. This deed was not revealed until the executors of the sentence came to kill those who had been condemned. Then, for the first time, they found the women lying in the prison in the men's stead.

This is, without doubt, the great loyalty of women and an exceedingly high, fervent love. And while I shall pass over in silence how the guards of the condemned were made into a mockery, how the prisoners were released, what the authorities decided regarding the matter, and what resulted from it, I wish only to say a little about the power of true love in holy matrimony.

Some maintain that according to the old law of nature, which cannot be changed, no greater envy <note>The author uses "envy" here in the sense of bitter jealousy or social discord.</note> can arise than from the impurity of the loins <note>original: "vnreynikeit der gemächt," referring to infidelity or sexual misconduct.</note>. Conversely, no greater love can be found than that of a friendly, harmonious marriage. For the fire of reason does not burn toward madness, but rather warms toward mutual pleasure and binds hearts together so firmly that they always, in the same manner, desire and do not desire the same things <note>This is a reference to the classical definition of friendship by Sallust: "to want and not to want the same thing, that is true friendship" (*idem velle atque idem nolle*).</note>. And when this lovely habit

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Minyan women of Sparta. Having successfully swapped clothes with their husbands in prison to help them escape execution, the narrative now shifts to reflect on their motives, their courage, and the legal consequences of their trickery.</meta>

[Handwritten in top right corner: 51]

-> The 43rd Leaf <-

When adversity struck, these women offered themselves without being asked to endure toil, labor, and sorrow to provide helpful counsel. This same companionable, sweet, and proven love between the Minyan <note>original: *meniacum*. The Minyans were a group of people in Greek mythology who had settled in Lacedaemon (Sparta).</note> spouses took hold of their spirits, causing them to devise such clever and high cunning while their husbands were in distress—a strength of mind they might not otherwise have possessed. Through the clever arrangement of clothes, by moving at the exact right time, and with total foresight, they sought to blind the eyes of the guards.

For this reason, they set aside all pleasure and joy and applied their high reason, resolving in the depths of their hearts to leave nothing untried for the safety of their beloveds. Thus, a spirit of goodness rose within them, by which they defiantly attempted to liberate their husbands. Through the complete faithfulness of their marriage, those men were to be set free and granted life anew—men who had been condemned by the highest court, who lay in the harsh dungeon, who had been abandoned, and who had already been handed over to the hands of the executioners.

Most significant of all was that the supreme authority was thereby shamed, all established laws were disregarded, and the will of the entire community was obstructed and nullified by their actions. In order to release their husbands, the women placed themselves in mortal danger, submitting to the power of those they had mocked and deceived.

I cannot marvel enough at the great loyalty and absolute love evident here; for had their love been meager or cold, they could have easily remained at home with their reputations <note>original: *gelÿmpff*, meaning propriety or social standing</note> intact and without punishment, having never attempted such a perilous feat. But to conclude much in few words: I may well call these women "manly-bold" and "true men," and conversely call the young men "women," for it was in that feminine guise that the husbands escaped and were saved.

### Concerning Penthesilea, Queen of the Amazons, the 30th Chapter.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts an armored woman on horseback leading other armored riders into battle. A label above her reads: Penthesila.</note>

Penthesilea the maiden was a queen of the <term>Amazons</term> <note>A legendary nation of all-female warriors in Greek mythology, known for their skill in archery and riding.</note>, and she reigned after Orithyia and Antiope. From what lineage she was born, however, I have not read. She despised her own exceeding beauty and set aside her soft, womanly disposition. Instead, she donned armor according to the custom of her ancestors and placed a helmet upon her gold-colored hair. With a quiver at her side, she practiced not feminine ways, but rather rigorous, manly <term>ritterschafft</term> <note>knightly deeds or the lifestyle of a warrior</note> with the war chariot and on horseback. She proved herself more wonderful than other queens in both physical strength and the arts of war. She must also have been highly ingenious, for one reads of her that she was the first to invent the throwing-axe, which before her time was unknown to the world...

<summary>This page introduces Penthesilea, the Amazon Queen, describing how she rejected traditional feminine beauty to become a warrior and inventor of the battle-axe.</summary>

Horace (65–8 BCE) was a famous Roman lyric poet; however, the story of Penthesilea’s love for Hector is more commonly associated with later medieval expansions of the Trojan myth rather than Horace’s original works.</note> that this same Penthesilea—as some claim—when she heard of the unprecedented strength of Hector of Troy, began to love him with a fervor never before seen. She desired so longingly that she might conceive an heir for her kingdom from him, that she marched well-equipped with a great army to Troy to assist him against the Greeks. 

And although the Greeks were widely and highly renowned for their strength and arts of war, she was nevertheless diligent in fighting the enemy fearlessly, so that she might please Hector with her power and skill in war, as well as with her beauty. She often scattered them from their horses onto the earth with her lance, often wounded them mortally with the sword, and often pursued the fleeing ones with her <term>missile weapons</term> <note>original: "geschütze." In this context, it refers to ranged weapons like bows and arrows or javelins, for which the Amazons were legendary.</note>.

### Concerning Polyxena, the daughter of King Priam
### The 31st Chapter

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the sacrifice of Polyxena. A maiden kneels upon a stone tomb while a man behind her—labeled **Neoptolemus**—seizes her hair and prepares to strike with a sword. The tomb bears the name **Achilles**, and the kneeling woman is labeled **Polyxena**.</note>

Polyxena the maiden was the daughter of King Priam of Troy and Hecuba. She was of such surpassing and blooming beauty that she ignited the heart of Achilles with the flames of Cupid <note>original: "flammen cupidinis." In Roman mythology, Cupid was the god of desire and erotic love.</note> so fervently that, through the treacherous instruction of her mother Hecuba, she arranged a secret meeting for him to come to her alone by night in the Temple of Apollo Thymbraeus <note>original: "tempel appollinis tÿmbrei." A sanctuary of Apollo located in the plain of Troy, where according to legend, Achilles was ambushed.</note>. It was there that he was mortally shot by Paris. 

Because of this death, when the power of the Trojans was diminished and the city of Ilium <note>original: "stat ilium." Another name for Troy.</note> was destroyed, Neoptolemus <note>The son of Achilles, also known as Pyrrhus.</note> led her to his father’s grave to take revenge for the death of Achilles. And when she saw the grim youth drawing his sword, and all the surrounding people weeping for her, the noble maiden—with such a strong spirit and such an un-trembling face—offered herself...

...her innocent neck to death. Thus, the strength of her spirit gave people no less reason to marvel than the devotion of the son toward his father <note>referring to Neoptolemus's sacrifice of Polyxena to honor his father Achilles' ghost</note>. It is undoubtedly a great thing and well worthy of remembrance that such a tender age, of the female sex, and a royal daughter raised in such comfort, could so master and restrain her spirit amidst the shifting of fortune—especially under the sword of a victorious enemy, a sight which often turns the manly hearts of strong men into despondency and trembling. It is right to believe that such quality came from a highly noble nature, which she displayed through her contempt for death; it was as if she were a bold woman by nature, had she not been persecuted by ill fortune and raised so soon <note>likely meaning she was forced to grow up or face death prematurely</note>.

Hecuba was also spoken of <note>original: "Meredicta," likely a scribal error for "maledicta" (cursed) or "memorata" (mentioned)</note> by Ovid in the 13th book of the *Metamorphoses*:
"And she terrified those fields with her strange barking."

# Concerning Hecuba, the Queen of the Trojans
### The 32nd Chapter

<note>A large decorative initial 'H' marks the beginning of the chapter on Hecuba.</note>

**H**ecuba, the most illustrious Queen of the Trojans, was a great symbol and a lesson in the transience of power and the transformation into misery and tribulation. Cisseus, the King in Thrace, was her father, and she was given as a wife to the most illustrious King Priam of Troy, by whom she bore nineteen children, sons and daughters. Among them, the highest ornament of the Trojan dominion was Hector, whose brilliance in rigorous knighthood was so bright that he not only enlightened himself but also graced all his friends and the fatherland with eternal glory. She was also not only illustrious for the blessedness of the kingdom...

# The 45th Leaf

and through her many noble children, but also because of the misfortune of fate that rules all this world, she became known far and wide to everyone. For she, in exceeding sorrow, wept over the death of her dearest son Hector and the youth Troilus—who displayed a greater manhood than was typical for his age—both of whom were slain by the hand of Achilles, and upon whom the strong foundation of the kingdom had stood. She also saw Paris, who was miserably treated by Pyrrhus; Deiphobus, whose ears, mouth, and nose were cut off and who was killed in that manner; the burning of the entire city of Ilium <note>original: "ÿlium," another name for Troy</note>; the death of her son Polites, who was killed in the lap of his father Priam; the stabbing of her husband Priam at the altar, to which he had fled for sanctuary <note>original: "freiheÿt", meaning liberty or the right of asylum in a sacred place</note>; the imprisonment of her daughter Cassandra and her son Hector’s wife, Andromache, as well as her own capture by the enemies; the beheading of her daughter Polyxena upon the grave of Achilles; and the death of Astyanax, her grandson <note>original: "jres suns kind," literally "her son's child." Astyanax was the son of Hector.</note>, who was dragged out of a hiding place and crushed with a stone. All this the miserable woman saw for herself.

And finally, she found her youngest son Polydorus, killed through the treachery of Polymestor and buried on the shore of Thrace, for which she mourned in pitiful despair. Because of such immense, unspeakable sorrow and tribulation, some say she lost her mind and ran through the fields of Thrace, raging and howling like a dog until her end; and after her death, she was buried on the shore called Cynosema <note>original: "Cinosemia," derived from the Greek for "The Dog's Tomb"</note> by the Hellespont sea <note>original: "möre helesponticū," the Dardanelles</note>. Others say she was led away by the enemies along with the other captive women. And so that she should be spared no misery or sorrow, she finally saw—after Agamemnon was killed—her beloved daughter Cassandra strangled at the command of Queen Clytemnestra.

## ¶ Of Cassandra, the daughter of Priam, Chapter 34

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the capture of Cassandra. Labels within the image identify "Cassandra" written vertically on the left and "Troy" above the city. On the right, a woman holds a scroll containing the Latin words "superum ilium cadet," which means "high Troy shall fall."</note>

Cassandra was a daughter of Priam, the king in Troy. Of her, the ancients say that she possessed the spirit of prophecy within her. Whether this came from her diligent study, through the grace of God, or the deceit of the devil, is uncertain to us. Yet it is said of her that long before it came to pass, she prophesied—with a loud voice and clear understanding—the audacity of Paris in kidnapping Helen, her arrival in Troy, the long siege of the city, and finally its destruction and the king’s downfall. 

And because of such prophecies, she was harshly beaten by her father and her brothers, and her words were not heeded. From this, the poets took the fable that she was courted by Apollo <note>the Greek and Roman god of prophecy, music, and the sun</note>, and she promised to submit to his will, provided that he [endowed] her with the art of...

<summary>This page introduces Cassandra, daughter of King Priam, discussing her disputed gift of prophecy and her ignored warnings regarding the fall of Troy.</summary>

# The 47th Leaf

endowed with the gift of prophecy. And when this was granted to her by him <note>referring to Apollo, the god mentioned on the previous page</note>, she did not wish to keep the promise she had made. Since he could not take the gift back from her, he added a condition: that she might indeed prophesy, but no one would believe her predictions, treating them as if a fool had spoken them.

She was betrothed to a noble youth named Coroebus <note>original: "Cozthus chorebus"</note>, who received her kindly. But in sorrow and mourning, he was taken from her during the war outside the city. And finally, after the loss of her father and other friends, she became a captive, allotted to Agamemnon as his share of the spoils of war. 

And as she was being led by him toward the city of Mycenae, she prophesied to him on the way how Clytemnestra <note>Agamemnon's wife</note> had prepared herself to kill him. But Agamemnon did not believe her prophecy. After many kinds of worry, fear, and the distress of the sea, they arrived at Mycenae; there, Agamemnon was killed by his wife, and afterward Cassandra was strangled.

### ¶ Concerning Clytemnestra, Queen
### of Mycenae, the 34th Chapter.

**C**lytemnestra was the daughter of Tyndareus, the King of Oebalia <note>original: "Cebalia," an ancient name for the region of Laconia or Sparta</note>, born of Leda and a sister of Castor, Pollux, and Helen. She was married to Agamemnon, the King of Mycenae. Although she was illustrious because of her high birth, she was nonetheless defamed far and wide for the wickedly committed misdeeds she boldly undertook. 

For at the time when her husband Agamemnon ruled the Greek people as a captain before Troy, and after she had already borne him many children, she fell into unseemly desire and wicked love for the idle and cowardly youth Aegisthus. He was a son of Thyestes, born of Pelopia, and because of his laziness, he had withdrawn from the life of knighthood and joined the priestly office. And through the help and counsel of Nauplius the elder <note>Nauplius sought revenge against the Greek leaders because they had unjustly executed his son, Palamedes, during the Trojan War</note>, who was once the father of Palamedes, they became unseemly companions to one another. 

From this evil it arose—partly out of fear of the King's return and punishment for her misdeeds, and through the advice of her lover, and perhaps because of the resentment she felt because he had brought Cassandra with him to Mycenae—that this bold and voracious <note>original: "freßdig," meaning greedy or insatiable in her passions</note> woman, daringly and with a hardened spirit, began to rage terribly against her husband. 

And when he returned victorious in triumph from Troy, having suffered much of the sea's great turbulence, he was received by her with a false heart and staged friendship, under the guise of a wife longing for her husband's return. And when they had eaten, and he was sated with good wine, she arranged with her lover Aegisthus—as some say, who lay secretly hidden in the hall—to kill him. Others say that as he sat at the table, he was dressed in the garment that he had won through victorious warfare. Because he the Greek

### Virgil, Aeneid, Book 2.
It is not the hated face of the Spartan woman, the daughter of Tyndareus, nor Paris who is to be blamed; rather, it is the cruelty of the gods that has overturned these riches <note>original: "Non tibi tindaridis facies inuisa lacene / Culpatus ve paris. verū inclementia diuū / Has euertit opes &c." ### Chapter 35: Concerning Helen

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a man and two women in a small boat upon the water. Labels from left to right identify the locations and figures: Troy, Paris, Helen, and Lacedaemon (Sparta).</note>

Helen—as many of the ancients believe—was very well known to the entire world because of her enslavement to unchastity <note>original: "vnkeüsche," referring to her legendary adultery and abduction</note> and because of the great, long-lasting war raised on her account. She was the daughter of Tyndareus, the King of Oebalia <note>original: "Cybalie," an ancient name for Laconia or Sparta</note>, born of the most beautiful woman Leda, and wedded to Menelaus, the King of the Lacedaemonians <term>Lacedaemonians: the people of the region surrounding Sparta</term>. The beauty of this same Helen—as all the ancient Greek masters and the Latin authors after them say—was so graceful and exceedingly radiant that she might easily be preferred over the beauty of all other women. Indeed, Homer wearied his own spirit—even though he possessed an angelic <note>original: "englische," here used to mean sublime, heavenly, or extraordinarily gifted</note> intellect—not to mention other writers, before he could fully describe her beauty in his poems as he had heard of it. And all the best...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Helen of Troy's legendary beauty. ->.48.<-

...diligence, effort, and labor to create a likeness of the highest beauty, so that she might also become known to those who came after. Among the masters, the best and most famous was commissioned by the people of Croton <note>The Crotoniates were residents of Croton, a Greek city in southern Italy famous for its schools of philosophy and medicine.</note> for a large sum of money; he was named Zeuxis of Heraclea. He applied all his strength, sense, art, and reason to shape her with his brush. 

But since this same painter had no other model than the poems of Homer describing her beauty and the general praise of all the people—from which he could not sufficiently form her figure in his mind according to his own judgment—he decided to gather her beauty from other well-formed women and youths <note>original: "frawen vnd knaben." While traditional versions of this story mention five virgins, the text here includes youths, perhaps reflecting a broader Renaissance ideal of anatomical perfection.</note>. He did this so that he might better approach and envision her divine beauty through the form of the face and the finest limbs, thereby creating a portrait that would show her likeness in future times. 

And five of the most beautifully formed in posture and limb that could be found were presented to him, from all of whom he assembled one figure, taking the best and most beautiful from each. Yet even then, with all his art and strength, he could not sufficiently assemble a portrait that could equal her, according to the general praise of her beauty. 

Nor do I wonder at this; for who could depict with a brush and colors, or with an engraving tool <note>original: "grabmesser," a tool used for woodcuts or metal engraving</note>, those joyful eyes, that lovely face, her pleasing smile <note>original: "schmollen," which in this context likely refers to a charming pout or a slight, attractive facial expression rather than modern sulking</note>, and the many graceful gestures that change according to words and deeds? Such things are the works of nature alone, and not of the master artist. Therefore, he made what he could; and what was made by him, he left to posterity as a heavenly image. From this, the ingenious masters have a...

Jupiter was transformed into a swan, and Leda was turned into an eagle <note>In standard Greek mythology, Jupiter (Zeus) becomes a swan to seduce Leda, who remains human. This German text's claim that Leda became an eagle is a rare variant or a specific interpretive error in the medieval tradition.</note> and laid two eggs. By this, they wish to show that the beauty of this same Helen cannot be depicted by any man with brushes and colors, since the highest god Jupiter himself formed her with all beauty and without any flaw.

Because of this wonderful beauty, Theseus was moved to travel from Athens into Laconia. And at the time of the greatest joy of all the royal knighthood, he boldly and by force abducted the young, tender maiden and carried her home. And although he could take nothing more from her than kisses, her chastity was nonetheless somewhat marked <note>original: "mit maſen v ermercket." This is a corruption of Aethra, the mother of Theseus, who was left to guard Helen.</note>. Although some say that Proteus, the king of Egypt, brought her back to her land. He gained hope from her flirtatious manners, her sparkling eyes, and her passionate gestures of love, with which she completely fixed the burning flames of desire in his heart.

Fortune also showed its favor to these things; for it happened by chance that Menelaus had to travel to Crete—which we now call Candia <note>Candia was the name used for Crete during the Middle Ages and the Venetian period.</note>—on great business. During that time, Hector was slain by Achilles, and Paris was killed by the bold youth Pyrrhus. <note>original: "freydigen jüngling pirrho." Pyrrhus, also known as Neoptolemus, was the son of Achilles who arrived late in the war to fulfill a prophecy that Troy could not fall without him.</note> And it is thought that Helen found little [satisfaction] in having taken Paris, and after his death she took Deiphobus, <note>Deiphobus was another prince of Troy and a son of King Priam; his marriage to Helen after Paris's death is a common element in the classical tradition.</note> the younger brother of Paris. Finally, when the Greeks intended to accomplish through treachery what they could not finish with the long siege, she who was the cause of all the evil [Helen] eagerly set about to carry out whatever she could devise against the Trojans, so that she might be restored to the favor of her first husband. And so it happened.

For when the Greeks, through cunning, feigned their departure from the siege of the city, the Trojans—now terrified and exhausted beyond measure by their labors—were somewhat overcome by sleep after the new joys and festive living of the feast. Helen had great torches lit on high, as if she intended to hold dances of joy, signaling to the Greeks to return, as she had previously agreed with them. <note>In this version of the myth, Helen acts as a collaborator with the Greeks, using fire signals to guide the fleet back from their hiding place at Tenedos.</note> When they returned, they found the city settled in a half-sleep; the gates were opened, and the entrance offered itself without resistance.

And after the city was set on fire and Deiphobus was miserably slain—in the twentieth year <note>This "twentieth year" refers to the total time since Helen was first taken, including the years spent gathering the Greek forces and the ten-year siege itself.</note> since she had been taken from Menelaus—she was returned to him. Some others say that Helen was not led away by Paris of her own will, but by force; therefore, it was quite fitting that her husband received her back into favor. But as he desired to sail back to Greece with her, he was forced by storms and the fury of the sea to travel to Egypt, where he was well received by King Polybus, and

### Of Circe, the daughter of the Sun. Chapter 36.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the sorceress Circe on the right, facing four men on the left. Three of the men have been transformed, bearing the heads of a boar, a wolf or dog, and a ram. The man in the center retains his human head and is labeled "Ulysses" <note>original: "vliyes"</note>. The woman is labeled "Circe" <note>original: "circes"</note>.</note>

**C**irce, on account of her sorcery, is to this day a widely renowned woman. According to the tales of the poets, she was the daughter of the Sun <note>Helios</note> and Perse, the poetess <note>The text uses the word "tichterin" (poetess), though in classical mythology, Perse (or Perseis) was an Oceanid nymph.</note>, whose father was Oceanus; her brother was Aeëtes, the king in Colchis. However, in truth, she was called the daughter of the Sun because she was illuminated by an exceptional and elegant beauty, and because the power of herbs was known to her above all others. Furthermore, she was exceedingly wise in all her deeds and conduct. All these things—beauty and wisdom—are gifts the Sun bestows upon those children who are born under its most favorable influence, as the astrologers say.

->The 51st leaf<-

...saying how she left the island of Colchis and came into Italy, I do not recall reading. But all ancient histories testify that her dwelling was on the mountain of the Volsci <note>The Volsci were an ancient Italic tribe.</note>, which to this day has kept the name Mount Circe <note>Mount Circeo on the Italian coast.</note>. And although one finds only fictional tales about this highly renowned woman, these same stories can be interpreted according to the truth as much as we are able, so that the readers may be instructed regarding their error.

It is said of her that all sailors who, by chance from a storm or by their own will and purpose, came to that same mountain—which once was an island but is now a coast of the sea—were transformed by her with sorcery through spells and potions into wild, cruel animals, each according to his nature.

Among these were also Ulysses' companions. But Ulysses was protected by the counsel of Mercury. He also subdued her with his sword and the threat of death, so that she had to restore his companions to their human form once more. He also remained for a whole year in her company, and she bore by him a son named Telegonus <note>The transcription reads "Theologonum," likely a scribal error for Telegonus, the son of Ulysses and Circe.</note>; then he departed from her, gifted with great counsel and wisdom.

The woman was powerful in wealth, in reason, words, and deeds, settled not far from the city of Gaeta in Campania. And many were moved by her beauty and her flattering, lovely manners and gestures to see her. She could so present and conduct herself with them that everyone had hope of winning her special favor and love; there was no one for whom it was not easy to fulfill her commands according to her will. Yet her mind was always set on keeping the purity of her body unblemished; therefore, she intended to send her lovers away from her with excuses <note>original: "geloten," meaning dismissals or pretexts.</note> so that she would not be overcome, and commanded...

## The 52nd leaf.

...[she commanded] some to trade in merchandise, because of which they were said to be transformed into dogs; for all merchants learn to chatter and talk much, and to bark like dogs until they have sold their goods. For this reason, Mercury, the god of merchants, is also painted with a dog’s head <note>The author likely refers to the syncretism of the Greek Hermes/Mercury with the Egyptian Anubis (Hermanubis), who has the head of a jackal or dog.</note>. She commanded others to practice chivalry for her sake, and these showed themselves to be more combative and stronger than their own nature had given them; therefore, they were said to be transformed into lions. The others she commanded to dwell with her; these were Ulysses’ companions, whom she allowed to live beautifully according to every pleasure because of the desire she had for Ulysses. Therefore, they were said to be transformed into swine, for they continuously pursued excessive lust like swine. And she gave many other similar commands to her lovers, who, according to the nature of her commands, might be judged to have been transformed into these or other animals.

¶ O you reader and listener of this story, observe how many "Circes" there are on earth who even today, with their unchaste gestures and unseemly finery, snatch men for their evil love and pleasure, and yet wish to be called pious in the process. This, however, is not entirely pure, for unchaste gestures bring about evil works.

¶ O, how many men and youths are daily transformed into lions, dogs, swine, apes, and cuckoos <note>original: "gauch" — a fool or simpleton, often associated with the cuckoo bird.</note>, who rightly should stop their ears so that they might not hear the flattering song of the Sirens <note>The author blends the story of the Sirens with the Circe myth to illustrate the danger of temptation.</note> if they wish to remain untransformed. This Ulysses did through the wisdom given to him by Mercury, with which his companions were also delivered from her, according to the testimony of history. It is also said of this same woman that she was the wife of Picus, King of the Latins, who was born of Saturn, and that she taught him to tell of future things from the song of birds. **C**amilla, the highly renowned maiden of worthy memory, was the daughter of Metabus, the oldest king of the Volscians, and of Casmilla, his wife, who ended her life at the birth of the same child. And in her memory, he took the letter "s" from her name, so that she [the daughter]

# The 53rd leaf

would be named Camilla. For this same maiden, from the day of her birth, fortune was harsh and ill-intentioned; for not long after her mother's death, Metabus was suddenly driven out of his kingdom by an uprising among his most powerful lords and citizens. In his flight, he set out taking nothing with him but his little daughter, whom he loved more than all his possessions; for her sake, he lamented his misfortune all the less, provided he could lead her with him into exile. 

But as the poor man escaped on foot, carrying his only daughter in his arms, he came to the flowing water called Amasenus <note>original: "damascenus," a likely misspelling of the river Amasenus in Italy, where this myth takes place</note>, which at that time had swollen so much from heavy rain that he could not swim across it with his daughter. And by divine inspiration—as God did not wish such a future, world-famous, noble maiden to perish by such an ignominious death in the water—he devised a plan: he wrapped her in bark and bast <note>inner tree fiber used for cordage</note> and bound her to a spear <note>original: "stange" (pole/staff); according to Virgil’s *Aeneid*, Metabus tied her to his heavy oaken spear</note> that he happened to be carrying with him. He then vowed the child to the goddess Diana <note>the goddess of the hunt and chastity</note> if she would keep her alive, and he threw the spear with the child with all the strength of his body as far as he could into the water, and quickly swam after her. 

And when, through God's help, he reached her again unharmed, he was somewhat comforted <note>original: "erstrewet," meaning his sorrow was dispersed or he was cheered</note> in his misery. He went with her into a wild forest, living in a cave, where with great effort he nourished her with the milk of wild animals. When this same Camilla had grown older, she covered her body with the skins of wild animals that she herself had shot with a bow. In this she practiced daily, as well as in throwing from a sling and pursuing and catching wild animals; and above all things, she cherished purity <note>original: "reynigkeit," referring to her lifelong vow of virginity in service to Diana</note>.

# The 53rd leaf [Handwritten: 62]

...expressing what they thereby learned was to be done in their father's house; what was required in the temples, at dances, and other festivities where they are judged and seen by all. They were taught not to offer their ears to listen to the words of frivolous people, and to restrain their mouths with silence. They were not to let their eyes wander lewdly, but to maintain good manners and always be diligent in honorable conduct. They were to always despise idleness, gluttony <note>original: "schleckmeüln," literally "licking mouths," referring to a fondness for delicacies or sweets</note>, excessive mirth, too many dances, and the company of young men. They were to remember that the fulfillment of every pleasure serves little to the preservation of chastity. Thus, being adorned with praiseworthy chastity through reason, they might be joyfully placed into the holy state of matrimony <note>original: "gemahelschafft"</note> by the advice of their friends.

# The 54th leaf

Penelope, daughter of King Icarius and wife of the most valiant <note>original: "strengsten," which in this context implies military prowess and hardiness rather than just strictness</note> knight Ulysses. Because of her unblemished grace and complete chastity, she has been a holy example for honorable women for all eternity. This same lady’s steadfastness was long and sorely tested by unwelcome suitors <note>original: "vngesell," literally "un-companions" or "bad company," referring to the suitors who pressured her to remarry</note>, yet without any success for those who pleaded. In her youth, because of her wondrous beauty, she was especially beloved by her father and was married to Ulysses. At the time when the Greeks traveled to Troy to conquer Ilium <note>another name for the city of Troy</note>, she bore him a son named Telemachus. Immediately thereafter, Ulysses was called to the war and, in part, compelled to go. For this reason, he left Penelope and the small child to the care of his father Laertes and his mother Anticlea. 

During the years the Greeks lay before the city, she endured a state of widowhood for over ten years with much distress. But after the city of Ilium in Troy was destroyed, and the princes and lords desired to return home across the sea, a common rumor arose that several ships of the high lords had been driven against the rocks by the storm and there were shattered. Some were cast far into other lands; some were sunk entirely, both lives and goods; some—though very few—returned to land. But of the voyage of Ulysses and his companions, no one knew anything to say of where they had gone. 

After long waiting and doubting where he might have gone, he was presumed dead. Because of this, his mother Anticlea was cast into such mourning, grief, and despair that she hanged herself with a rope to put an end to such pain. But Penelope, although she bore her husband’s absence heavily and sorrowfully in her heart, she was tormented even more...

# Leaf 55

...out of the worry that he might have died a wretched death. And when she, after much weeping and calling out, realized that all was in vain and done without hope, she set her mind to remain between her old father-in-law, Laertes, and her son, Telemachus, to protect her widow’s status <note>original: "witwen stül," literally "widow’s chair," referring to her social position and household authority as a widow</note> for all eternity, remaining chaste and pure without any wavering. 

Yet, as time passed, her laudable nature, her immense beauty, her proven character, her high nobility, and her manifold virtues moved many noble youths from Ithaca, Cephalonia, and Aetolia to desire her. By these men, she was constantly troubled, provoked, and petitioned; and the less hope there was for Ulysses’ life, the greater their pleading for her became. This reached such a point that the old Laertes could no longer bear to watch the rowdiness of these suitors and moved away to the countryside <note>original: "dorf," meaning a village or rural estate</note> to avoid them. 

Immediately, the suitors occupied the hall of Ulysses and did not cease from their constant courting and wooing—each according to his means—so that he might have her as his wife. But as the honorable lady feared that some violence might be done to her holy intent, and since she could find no way to delay or refuse them as she desired, she conceived a clever ruse <note>original: "lüst," which in this context means a cunning plan or trick rather than modern "lust"</note> through divine inspiration. By this, she might put off the rowdy youths longer. 

She desired to tell the countless suitors a deadline: that she wished to wait for her husband only as long as it took her to fully complete the weaving <note>original: "wepp," referring to the shroud she was making for her father-in-law, Laertes</note> she had at hand, according to royal custom. The noble youths easily granted her this. However, everything that she had worked on with diligent industry all day, she—as a ruse to delay the youths—unraveled again at night. In this manner, so much time passed that the suitors consumed much of Ulysses’ wealth in his own hall with their constant feasting and high living, and she still...

->Leaf 56<-

...has remained in the memory of mankind because of the wars and battles of Aeneas against Turnus on her account, rather than because of any other praiseworthy deeds performed by her. This same Lavinia, because of the preeminent grace of her beauty, and also because she was the expected heir to her father’s kingdom, was ardently desired by Turnus, the fierce <note>The original term "fraissigē" implies a combination of being terrifying, fierce, and formidable.</note> king of the Rutulians, to be given to him as a lawful wife. To this end, Amata, the maiden's mother, gave him good hope and favor because he was the son of her sister <note>The text says "tochter sun," which literally means "daughter's son," but in classical mythology, Turnus is the son of Venilia, the sister of Amata; the author likely refers to him as a close kinsman.</note> from another man. 

But Latinus, through the practice of knowing future things for certain, had learned from the response of the gods that his daughter should be betrothed to a foreign prince; for this reason, he delayed giving her away for many days. And as soon as Aeneas, a fugitive from Troy, had come into Italy and requested friendship and peace from Latinus—which was granted to him—and because of his highly noble descent, and also because Latinus had recognized this through the aforementioned prophecy, he gave him his only daughter as a lawful wife. Because of this, a great war arose between Aeneas and Turnus. 

And when, through hard fighting with much bloodshed and the slaying of the most noble, the Trojans prevailed and were completely victorious, Lavinia was given to Aeneas with a festive wedding. Because of this, Amata was cast into such mourning and sorrow that she ended her life with a rope <note>Amata committed suicide by hanging, despairing over the defeat of Turnus and the marriage of her daughter to the "foreign" Aeneas.</note>. Yet some say she was given to him before the battles. But however that may be, it is known that Lavinia was made pregnant by him, and before she gave birth to the child, Aeneas drowned in the river called Numicus <note>original: "numicus"; a small river in ancient Latium where, according to legend, Aeneas vanished during a battle.</note>. And Ascanius, the son of Aeneas <note>Ascanius was the son of Aeneas and his first wife, Creusa, who died at Troy.</note>, took charge of the kingdom; but Lavinia feared him, because he was her stepson, and therefore she fled...

# The 57th Leaf

**D**ido, who was previously named Elissa, was a queen and the founder of the city of Carthage. I must further extend her praise a little with my writing, so that I may somewhat erase the stains unjustly brushed onto the honor of her widowhood <note>The text uses "wittwenstůl" (widow's seat), referring to her reputation and status as a widow, which the author feels has been unfairly slandered by earlier writers.</note> by some. To speak of the beginning: the Phoenicians are a highly sensible and ingenious people who, in ancient times, traveled across the sea from furthest Egypt to Syria, where they built many magnificent cities. Among them, the chief ruler was King Agenor, who was not only illustrious in his own time but remains far-famed even until our own; from his lineage, Dido took her origin. 

And when her father Belus had conquered the island of Cyprus, he ended his days and entrusted the young maiden and her brother Pygmalion—who was the elder—to the lords and citizens of the land. These people immediately thereafter placed him upon his father’s royal throne to rule the kingdom. They gave his sister Elissa as a wife to the highest-ranking man after the King in Tyre, named Acerbas, or Sychaeus, or Sicharius <note>Ancient sources vary on the name: "Acerbas" is used by Justin, while Virgil calls him "Sychaeus."</note>, who was the administrator of the high office of the priesthood of Hercules. They loved each other holily in their marriage. 

Pygmalion was, above all other mortal men, so possessed by greed that he never believed he could be satisfied with gold. Now, Acerbas was exceedingly rich in gold. But when he recognized his brother-in-law's greed, he kept it as hidden as he could; yet, he could not suppress the common rumors of his wealth. Because of this, Pygmalion sought ways to obtain the gold and had him secretly murdered while he was unawares. When Elissa became aware of this, she bore the death of her husband so unbearably that it was nearly the end of her; she was...

# The 58th Leaf

[...having] died to him. <note>The sentence fragment refers to Dido mourning her husband Acerbas, who had been murdered by her brother.</note> But when she had spent much time in tears and had often called upon her dearest husband Acerbas in vain, and had at all times heavily cursed her brother, while still longing for her husband, she was warned in her sleep—as she had also often wisely considered while awake—to flee from her brother, so that she would not also be killed because of his great greed. Therefore, she set aside feminine weakness and strengthened her heart with a manly spirit; for this reason, her name Elissa was abandoned, and she was called **Dido**, which in her language signifies as much as a "manly woman." <note>The author refers to a traditional, though now debated, etymology where "Dido" is linked to a Phoenician word for "heroine" or "wanderer."</note>

She first considered how she might win over the princes of the cities who had been burdened and harmed by her brother in various ways, so that they would agree to her undertaking. When this was accomplished, Dido took all the treasures of her husband, and as much as she could snatch away from her brother, and had them secretly carried into the ships that he had prepared to send out for official business. <note>original: "zü gericht," referring to ships prepared for state use or legal trade expeditions.</note> And with well-considered cleverness, she had many sacks and small chests filled with sand and made very heavy to feign that her treasures were inside; these were carried openly into the ships. 

And so she sailed away from there with all the people who were in agreement with her, though the greater part of them did not know what her purpose was. But as soon as they had reached the high sea, she ordered those same weighted sacks and chests to be thrown into the water, which greatly astonished many who were not informed of her secret. And upon this, she spoke to her fellow travelers thus:

"Now I will gladly suffer the death I have often desired, since I have been granted the opportunity to sink the treasure into the sea, for which my dearest husband was killed by the greedy Pygmalion. But I have pity and compassion for..."

# The 58th Leaf

...you; for I know well that if we turn back to my greedy <note>original: "gefāgen," likely *geizigen* (greedy).</note> brother, he will not leave any of you unpunished by a grim death once he notices that the treasure has been sunk. To prevent this, I have strengthened my resolve never to return to my brother, and I offer myself, to the best of my ability, to be honest and helpful to all who wish to be with me, wherever we may go, and never to abandon you.

When the sailors heard this—although they left their ancestral homes unwillingly—out of fear of the death they expected from Pygmalion, they easily consented to travel into exile with her. They turned their ships and, through the lady's persuasion, came to the island of Cyprus. There they found young maidens sitting on the shore according to their custom, to provide lovely joy and pastime for guests in honor of the goddess Venus <note>original: "veneri." In ancient Cypriot tradition, young women practiced sacred prostitution at the temple of Aphrodite/Venus before marriage.</note> and as a special sacrifice, so that they might keep the human race in existence. 

Dido took all of them with her, and also the high priest with all his household, from whom she heard many future things that would arise from her flight. Thus sailing, they left Candia <note>The medieval name for the island of Crete.</note> behind them and Sicily to the right hand, and came to the shores of Africa, where they intended to find rest from their great labor and safety for their shipping; and they landed there. 

Immediately there was a great gathering of the nearby people out of a desire to see the foreign guests; some brought food, and others brought merchandise, as is customary. Through this, both parties gained mutual goodwill and favor; and through friendly conversation, the travelers realized it was the residents' good will that the guests should remain with them. Furthermore, the people of Utica <note>An ancient Phoenician city in North Africa, near the site where Carthage would be built.</note> advised this, who themselves were once from...

# The 59th Leaf

They <note>Dido’s advisors and the citizens of Carthage.</note> knew the holy and unshakeable resolve regarding the chastity of their lady. Conversely, they feared the king <note>King Iarbas of Libya, who desired to marry Dido.</note> that if his will were not followed, they would be completely destroyed by him. And when the queen asked the cause of their sorrow, they dared not tell the truth and devised a ruse by which they might achieve their purpose.

And they said the king’s desire was that they should choose one of two options: that they should send some of their best men to him, who would teach him and his people according to their established way of life—because he wished to set aside his fierce and unpraiseworthy character—or else they would have to choose the destruction of the city through war. Now they were troubled because they did not know whom they should appoint for this task, since no one among them wanted to leave his home and go to teach that fierce tyrant. 

The noble blood <note>A poetic reference to Queen Dido herself.</note> noticed what deceit lay hidden in their words and turned boldly toward them, speaking thus: "O you manly citizens, what levity is in your spirit! O, what sluggishness of body! Do you not know that we are born for our father and our fatherland, and that no citizen is worthy of the name unless he dares, for the common good <note>original: "gemeinen nucz." A central concept in medieval and Renaissance political thought, prioritizing the community over the individual.</note>, not only to suffer hardship but even death, should it happen so? Therefore, go joyfully and subject yourselves to these small worries, so that you may prevent the destruction of the land." 

By this speech, they thought they had sufficiently trapped the queen; for if it were her advice to suffer concern for the sake of the common good, then it would be a very small thing for her to also take a husband to preserve it. And so they revealed the king’s true demand to her. But when she began to realize how she had entangled herself through her own advice, she dared <note>The sentence continues on the next page, describing her reaction to the realization that her own logic about self-sacrifice for the state was being used to force her into a marriage that broke her vow of chastity.</note> such treachery—

<summary>Dido's advisors, fearing King Iarbas, trick the Queen into arguing that a citizen must sacrifice themselves for the state, only to reveal that the King demands Dido herself in marriage.</summary>

# The 60th Leaf

...not resist her people <note>The citizens of Carthage who were pressuring her to marry King Iarbas to avoid war.</note> and sighed deeply in her spirit and thought how she might strengthen her resolve of chastity to her husband <note>Dido’s first husband, Sychaeus, whom she had sworn to remain faithful to after his death.</note> and found what she believed to be most fitting for that purpose. A set amount of time to prepare or mourn.</note>, then she would enter into marriage again. This request was granted to her.

In the meantime, Aeneas, a fugitive from destroyed Troy, arrived by chance, wandering to her shores, and he was well received by her. But to keep eternal purity for her husband—as was her steadfast resolve, choosing death rather than breaking it—she had a pile of dry wood carried to the highest point of her dwelling. She did this according to her custom, to make it appear as if she intended to celebrate the anniversary rites <note>original: "jarzeit." In a medieval context, this refers to a religious memorial service or "year's mind" held for the deceased.</note> for her husband Sychaeus. For this, she also prepared all the sacrifices and sacrificial victims <note>original: "hostien." From the Latin *hostia*, meaning a victim or sacrifice offered to a deity.</note> with ordained adornment...

->The 60th Leaf<-

...you have shed. They shall look upon it pitiably, and especially those for whom it was easy not only to go to a second husband, but even to a third or even more. What will they say, I ask you, at the time of the reckoning of all mankind <note>A reference to the Last Judgment, where the author suggests Christian women will be judged against the moral standards of virtuous pagans.</note> when they see the pagan women standing before them—to whom Christ was unknown—who wished to keep their purity for worldly praise <note>original: "zeitlich lob." Reputation or honor in this mortal life, as opposed to eternal reward.</note> with such a steadfast mind and such a strong heart that they would rather go into death by their own hand than into another marriage partnership? Yet one of our women, who are quick to seek clever excuses for their conduct, might say: "It was necessary for me to take another husband. My father, mother, and brothers had died. I was entirely forsaken by everyone. I had nothing from anyone, nor any help. Furthermore, this person and that person promised me so much wealth..."

The author anticipates and dismisses the common social and economic excuses women use to justify taking a second or third husband.</summary>

->The 61st Leaf<-

...against the earth. Block your ears and stand firm against the storm-gusts of flattering suitors, and turn your cloak against the wind <note>A metaphor for protecting oneself against persuasion or social pressure.</note>, so you shall be preserved. Perhaps another woman comes and says: "Ah, I had great wealth—many landed estates, beautiful houses, royal furnishings, and gold and silver in abundance. I did not want to let those pass away without an heir. Therefore, I took the second husband so that I might leave heirs behind me."

O foolish desire! Did Dido not have an entire kingdom without bodily heirs? Did she not have royal wealth? Yet she did not wish to become a mother, for she thought in her supreme wisdom that nothing more foolish could happen than to break oneself down and build up another <note>The author argues that sacrificing one’s soul or vow for the sake of an heir’s worldly status is a poor trade.</note>. How could she then have pledged the purity of her heart just so she might provide a master for her heirs? Because of this, if you have many goods, do not lose them; instead, give them to the poor children of God, so that you may build yourself a hall in eternity <note>original: "einen sal... jn der ewigkeit." A reference to the "treasure in heaven" (Matthew 6:20) that cannot be stolen or destroyed.</note> which no enemy can destroy. By doing so, you may powerfully protect and keep your purity unassailed.

Then comes a third woman and says: "I was forced by my kin <note>original: "freūnden." In Early New High German, "friends" often referred to family or relatives.</note>. My father would not have it otherwise; my mother commanded it; it was the common advice of all my relatives. I would never have taken another man otherwise." Such words might be offered to fools! If one’s own desire and lust <note>original: "gaile." Carnal or worldly desire.</note> had not been involved, she could have well rejected all the talk of father, mother, and all kin with a single "No." Dido would rather die than live defiled, yet you would rather live in shame <note>original: "schamrot." Literally "blushing red with shame."</note> than say "No."

A fourth woman might come, who thinks herself cleverer, and say: "I was young, and it is well known how young blood seethes, which I could not resist." So speaks...

->The 61st Leaf<-

...the twelve apostle Paul <note>The author refers to 1 Corinthians 7:9: "It is better to marry than to burn [with passion]."</note> says: "It is better to be married again than to burn." So, I have followed his advice. And although that might be said as an excuse—if I did not know the chastity of our own women—did not Dido also, in the blooming youth of her warm blood, strengthen her mind to remain constant in purity? It is a great evil that you prefer to use the holy advice of Paul to justify your unchastity, especially since you have never truly regarded the actual intent for which that advice was given. 

It would be more praiseworthy for us to consider how we might restore the lost strength of the body through temperate living and good food, and to diligently consider that when the blood occasionally becomes overheated with desire, we should tame and cool that raging, tempting blood through labor and the reduction of food <note>This refers to the medieval practice of asceticism, where fasting and physical work were used to suppress sexual urges.</note>. The heathen lady Dido was able, for the sake of temporal honor, to withstand and conquer her raging blood. Why, then, should a Christian woman not be capable of the same, in order to earn eternal honor and glory? 

But alas! While we seek ways to hide this misdeed from God—to whom all things are manifest—we strip ourselves of temporal honor and eternal joy. We force ourselves to fall into a depth from which no one can climb back out. Therefore, all those who look upon the dead body of Dido and consider the cause of her death should rightly be ashamed that they are surpassed in praiseworthy chastity by a heathen woman. 

Nor should any widow think that she has done enough for her husband simply by weeping for him and wearing a black dress and a <term>wide headdress</term><note>original: "breiten sturtz." This refers to a specific style of mourning veil or structured headdress worn by widows in the late medieval and early modern periods.</note>. Her shared love should endure until death, if she wishes to remain promised to her husband in true friendship and love. She should not think of entering another marriage—as many do, more to fulfill their wicked bodily lust than for the holiness of marriage. And

<summary>The author critiques widows who use Saint Paul’s advice on marriage as an excuse for remarriage, arguing that they should instead use fasting and labor to control their desires. He holds up the pagan Dido as a moral superior to Christian women who prioritize physical lust over spiritual constancy.</summary>

Therefore, I ask the readers to forgive me for what I have written in excess, and I will return to my intended subject. The city <note>original: "purg" refers here to the city-state of Carthage.</note> mourned their dead Queen Dido; they honored her not only with human honors but also with decorations and sacrifices that, according to their custom, were offered to the gods with all their might. And not only at that time, but such honor was dedicated to her annually for as long as Carthage stood. She was also continuously honored by them as a goddess at other times.

## Of Nicaula, the 41st Chapter.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a woman, labeled "Nicaula" (the name used here for the Queen of Sheba), and a man holding a scroll labeled "Solomon." The man is seated upon a throne, representing the biblical meeting and exchange of wisdom between the two monarchs.</note>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the history of Dido to the life of Nicaula, better known as the Queen of Sheba. The previous page concluded the account of Dido's deification in Carthage and introduced the biblical context of the Queen's visit to King Solomon.</meta>

->The 62nd Leaf<-

Nicaula was born in the furthest land of the Moors <note>original: "morenlād," referring to Ethiopia or sub-Saharan Africa</note>; and because she attained such great wisdom, skill, and high dignity from such a wild, savage, and inhuman land—in terms of the customs and nature of its people—she is justly celebrated above others throughout the world. She lived during the times when the Pharaohs in Egypt were passing away, and she was also descended from their lineage. It is also said of her that she was a queen in Ethiopia (that is, the land of the Moors), in Egypt, and in Arabia. Furthermore, she possessed great wealth on the island of Meroë, where the river Nile flows. Because of this, she had so much wealth in gold and silver that she was estimated to surpass all the world.

Yet, despite such great wealth, she was never idle, nor was she found in feminine weakness <note>original: "waichmütigkeit," literally "soft-mindedness," here implying a lack of intellectual or moral resolve</note>; instead, she was always diligent and tireless in the practice of learning wisdom and the arts. She was so remarkable that she is praised in the Holy Scripture <note>original: "göttlichē geſchrifft"</note> and is the one whom people call Sheba. When she heard, through the wide fame of the world, of the greatest wisdom of King Solomon, she was filled with such great wonder that she set out from nearly the end of the world. Leaving behind her kingdom, the land of Egypt, and the Red Sea, she traveled through the desert in Arabia with a royal host and at great expense to come to Jerusalem to see him. 

The wealthiest King Solomon was so moved to wonder by this woman’s great power and magnificence that she was received by him with great dignity according to royal honors. And after she had been well answered and instructed by him regarding several questions, she openly confessed his wisdom to far surpass the skill, sense, and wisdom of all men, and that it undoubtedly came not from human learning, but from the divine...

<summary>This page introduces Nicaula, the Queen of Sheba, detailing her origins in Ethiopia, her immense wealth, and her intellectual diligence. It describes her journey to Jerusalem to test the famed wisdom of King Solomon.</summary>
<keywords>Nicaula, Queen of Sheba, King Solomon, Ethiopia, Meroë, Nile, wisdom, Jerusalem, Pharaohs</keywords>

## On Pamphile, Chapter 42

**P**amphile <note>original: "Amphiles" in the decorative initial, "Pamphile" in the heading. She was a woman of Kos credited by Pliny the Elder with the invention of silk weaving, though the German text here refers to "baumwollen" (cotton).</note> was a Greek woman, though antiquity has swept away the knowledge of which region she came from. Although she is not highly praised for her noble lineage, she nevertheless increased the common good, for which she should rightly receive praise. For no discovery is so small that it is not a sign of a subtle mind, for which a person deserves honor. Since this Pamphile was the first to discover how to shell cotton <note>term: <term>cotton</term> (original: "baumwollen," literally "tree-wool")</note> from the buds, then to card, comb, and spin it, and to make cloth from it—all of which was unknown to the world before her discovery—she is rightly placed among other ingenious women and honored with fitting praise according to her merit.

From [Ovid's] *Fasti*: <note>original: "Qui. fastorum"</note>

Whom Numitor follows, the brother of harsh Amulius.

->The 64th Leaf<-

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the story of Rhea Silvia and the birth of Romulus and Remus. The scene shows the figures associated with the founding of Rome.
