**R**hea Ilia was once highly renowned among all other women in Italy for her noble blood. For she took her origin from the Silvians <note>The Silvian dynasty was a line of kings in Alba Longa, descending from Aeneas's son, Ascanius (also called Iulus or Silvius in various accounts).</note>, who reigned one after another after the death of Aeneas, the first prince of Troy. The last of these were two brothers: Amulius the younger, and Numitor the elder, whose daughter she was. While she was still young, it happened that Amulius fervently desired to rule alone. Against all right and through his own power, he drove out his brother. Yet, so that he would not be seen to be raging too furiously against his brother, he forced him into a quiet life <note>original: "zwãg in dz blüt," likely meaning he forced him into a private, non-political existence</note>, allowing him to live idly in the countryside.

But he ordered Numitor's son, Lausus, to be miserably killed, so that he would be without worry that Lausus might later drive him out and return the kingdom to his father. He allowed the young maiden, Lausus’s sister <note>Rhea Ilia</note>, to remain alive, but only so that he could ensure no offspring would be born from her, from whom a threat <note>original: "trãg"</note> might come to him in the future. [He] gave her to the goddess Vesta <note>Vesta was the Roman goddess of the hearth. Her priestesses, the Vestal Virgins, were required to remain celibate for thirty years upon pain of death.</note> and placed her with other virgins in her cloister, wherein she was required to vow eternal chastity. But as she grew older, she was overcome by the desires of Lady Venus <note>original: "fraw venus," referring to the goddess of love and sexual desire</note>, though in what way is not known. However, that she had come to a manly embrace was proven by her large body <note>An early modern way of describing her visible pregnancy.</note>. For she gave birth in a single labor to Remus and Romulus, the beginners, founders, and fathers of the mighty city of Rome. For this offense, even though she was a royal lady, the children were cast out into the wilderness of wild beasts, and according to the ancient laws, the mother was buried alive. And although her body was buried, her name is highly honored in eternal memory through the great deeds of her children—a memory which the tyrant <note>Amulius, her uncle.</note> would have gladly erased.

When I consider this Rhea and see how many "holy clothes" cover the works of Venus, I cannot restrain myself from mocking her folly. There are some greedy people who, in order to save some money <note>Specifically, to avoid paying a dowry or to consolidate inheritance for a single heir.</note>, force their children into cloisters under the guise of devotion. These parents draw the younger children in their childhood days so strictly that the children later enter the monastic life through flatteries and the hope of an easier life, not knowing what they do. They then claim they have sacrificed a child to Almighty God, so that through the child's prayers, much grace shall be earned from God for both the dead and the living. With this and similar excuses, they wish to cover their evil intentions. O senseless heads! Do they not know what idle women contemplate while in the service of Venus <note>A reference to sexual urges and romantic fantasies.</note>? Or how eager they are to practice their "knighthood" <note>original: "ritterschafft," a metaphorical term for sexual activity or the "combats" of love</note>, so much so that they often prefer the harsh life of the common knighthood of Venus over the monastic way of life? And as often as they hear of marriage, when they [see] worldly...

[When they] behold [fine] clothes, golden jewelry, small brooches, belts, rings, or the like; or when they hear pipes [playing], or learn of weddings, courtly gatherings, or dances, they sigh deeply from their hearts and lament their misfortune, that they should be deprived of such joys. 

Then they curse the cowl and the scapular <term>scapular: a monastic garment consisting of a long piece of cloth worn over the shoulders, symbolizing the "yoke" of religious life</term>, along with the time, their fathers, their mothers, and all those who gave help or counsel toward their entry [into the monastery]. 

And their constant thought is how they might find an honorable way <note>original: "gelimpff," meaning with decency or without losing their reputation</note> to leave the cloisters again, or how they might bring their lovers <note>original: "bulen," a term for paramours or illicit lovers</note> to them in secret. Because open marriage is closed to them, they nevertheless secretly offer the tribute of their "knightly service" to Lady Venus <note>The author uses the mythological figure of Venus, goddess of love, to imply that the nuns are engaging in secret sexual affairs.</note>. 

Such is the choral singing, the devotion, and the evening prayer of these young nuns—not of all of them, lest I overreach, but of a great many. Through such "praiseworthy prayer," their fathers, mothers, and others who assisted them [in forcing the children into the cloister] will climb into heaven just as easily as a cow fits into a mouse-hole <note>A biting German proverb used to illustrate the impossibility of these parents reaching heaven through the coerced piety of their children.</note>.

[They should remain in] their father’s house from childhood, to be raised honorably with good manners, and to reach an age where they can clearly understand in their own minds what they are doing. And even if such [sincere women] were found only very rarely, it would still be better to have a small number of such maidens in the cloisters than a great number of those by whom the holy places are blasphemed and dishonored.

### About Gaya Cyrilla, the 44th Chapter

**G**aya Cyrilla was the beloved wife of the Roman king, Tarquinius Priscus. She was so industrious and clever that, although she was the wife of a Roman king, she was never found wandering idly in her royal halls. Instead, she devoted herself to the labor of wool-working—which in those times was considered an honorable and proper duty for Roman noblewomen. She showed herself so diligent in this work that her name is honored to this day for her domestic industriousness.

She was also so highly esteemed during her lifetime, and held in such honor and dignity by all the Roman people and loved by everyone, that they established a public decree: whenever a new bride was led into her husband’s house for the first time, she should be asked at the threshold what her name was. She was to answer immediately that her name was "Gaya," as a reminder that she should follow the example and life of the same Gaya <note>This refers to the Roman wedding formula: "Ubi tu Gaius, ego Gaia," meaning "Where you are Gaius, I am Gaya." The author interprets this as an homage to Gaya Cyrilla's domestic virtue.</note>. Although this custom is little valued by the ignorant, to the wise it is a great sign of the high wisdom and reason of this praiseworthy lady, for which she is rightly not forgotten.

<summary>The text concludes a critique of forced monasticism, advocating for informed consent in religious vows, and introduces Gaya Cyrilla as a legendary model of Roman domestic industry.</summary>
<keywords>Gaya Cyrilla, Tarquinius Priscus, monastic vows, domestic virtue, wool-working, Roman marriage customs, Titus Livius</keywords>

### About Sappho the Poetess, the 45th Chapter

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a woman labeled "Sappho" seated in a room playing a lute. To her left sit a bed and a chest; to her right, a book rests on a lectern while a harp lies on the floor. In the background, a man and a woman are seen embracing in a doorway.</note>

Sappho the maiden was born in the city of Mytilene <note>The capital city of the island of Lesbos.</note>. We find no further information regarding her birth. However, when we examine her artistry and the books she created—though the greater part of them has been lost to the passage of time—we may well conclude from them that she was possessed of a high intellect and was born of noble blood. Indeed, no lowly peasant lineage could ever have produced such works. She was so gifted that she was not content merely to read letters and join them together in speech; instead, out of a fervent desire for learning, she led... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "fü", likely the beginning of "fürte," meaning "led" or "continued."</note>

->The 66th Leaf<-

...[her study] was valued so highly that, because of such great art and intellect, an honorary statue was erected to praise her, named "Sappho’s Column" <note>original: "Saphos saul". This likely refers to a commemorative pillar or statue mentioned in classical sources as a tribute to her genius.</note> after her own name. She was numbered among the greatest poets. Such an honor cannot be equaled by an imperial or papal crown, nor a bishop’s miter, nor the palms of victory. However, if we are to believe the ancient writings: just as she learned well and blessedly, she was also captured by the ill-fated love of a young man <note>A reference to Phaon, the ferryman of Lesbos, whom legend says Sappho loved to the point of despair.</note>. She was so bound by this passion that she could not bear his absence with any measure of patience. Because of this, she also composed new, masterful, and mournful poems in which she lamented her despair and misery. She lived during the times of Ezekiel and Daniel, which is 4,630 years from the beginning of the world.

**T**ullia—who, because of her grievous misdeeds, gave cause for the Roman state <note>original: "römisch reych". While literally "Roman Empire," the text refers to the Roman Kingdom's transition into a Republic.</note> to be transformed from a state of servitude into freedom—was a daughter of Servius Tullius, the sixth king after Romulus. She was born to the daughter of the fifth king, Tarquinius Priscus. She was of high spirit and sharp intellect, and would be worthy of praise had she not turned those qualities toward murderous evil. 

For at the time when her father, Servius Tullius, was chosen as king through the general favor of the Roman people and the arrangement of King Tarquinius and his wife Tanaquil—as had been foretold to them previously by the burning head of the child Servius while he still lay in his cradle <note>According to legend, the infant Servius Tullius's head caught fire while he slept, which Queen Tanaquil interpreted as a sign of his future greatness.</note>, which is why the King later gave him his daughter in marriage—there were two brothers, Aruns and Lucius, born of the royal house of the Tarquinii. These two, and Lucius in particular, bore the reign of Servius with great resentment, believing that the kingdom should belong to them by hereditary right. 

They considered how to prevent him from ruling and deliberated whether it would be better to kill Servius or the King <note>Tarquinius Priscus, the reigning king and father-in-law to Servius.</note>. They decided it would be better for their purpose to execute the King; for if Servius died, the King would simply find another son-in-law to whom he would bequeath the kingdom, thus alienating it from them. They appointed two henchmen, who were well-practiced in wielding axes <note>original: "bephel". Refers to a hatchet or poleaxe, the traditional weapon used in the assassination of Tarquinius Priscus.</note>, to go before the king under the guise of a legal dispute, pretending to seek justice. And as the king turned toward one to hear his argument, the other struck his axe into the king’s head and left it sticking there. Queen Tanaquil immediately ordered everyone to leave who did not belong there and locked the royal hall, considering with great diligence how the...

### The 67th Leaf

The wounds of her dear husband were tended to in such a way as if there were great hope for his life. The murderers were captured; because of this, Aruns and Lucius feared their evil deeds would be revealed and avenged, and they fled from Rome to the city of Pometia to avoid retribution. 

The Queen also considered other aid, should the hope for his recovery fail. She summoned Servius and spoke to him, taking his right hand: 

"You see my husband is very near death. I beg you that you do not leave the death of your father-in-law <note>original: "schwehers"</note> unavenged, nor let your mother-in-law <note>original: "schwiger"</note> become a mockery to her enemies. For the kingdom is yours, if you are a man—not theirs who have accomplished this murderous evil with foreign hands. Therefore, rise up and follow the gods who marked you as king with the celestial fire <note>original: "hellischen feüer." In this context, it refers to a bright or heavenly light rather than "hellish." According to legend, a divine flame appeared over the head of the infant Servius Tullius, signaling his future royalty.</note> surrounding your head in the cradle. Consider who you are now, not the lowly birth from whence you came." 

Meanwhile, a great cry arose from the common people <note>original: "pöfel" — the common populace or "rabble."</note> with a violent desire to see the King in front of the royal palace, so that they could hardly be kept back from the gates. Thus Tanaquil thrust her head out of a window from a high hall and spoke to them: 

"You should be of good cheer! For the King was greatly startled by the unexpected, sudden blow, but the weapon has not wounded him deeply. He has even now come back to his senses. Once we have cleaned the wounds, all signs are favorable. We hope that you shall see him soon." 

With this, their violent commotion was stilled. And in the meantime, she commanded the people to be subject to Servius at the bidding of the King. Thus Servius reigned for some time in the guise of the King's administrator <note>original: "verwersers" — meaning a regent, steward, or one who acts on behalf of another.</note>, though the King was actually dead. But his death was entirely unknown to the common man. And

# The 67th Leaf.

When his death was made public, Servius had so strengthened himself with friends and public favor that, at the command of the people and with the consent of the fathers <note>The "fathers" refers to the Roman Senate (Patres).</note>, he was proclaimed king. Now that he ruled with power, he did not think to avenge the death of his father-in-law <note>original: "schwehers"</note>, but rather believed it better to overcome the enemy with goodness than with anger. And he took the two fugitive brothers <note>These are the sons of the previous king, Tarquinius Priscus.</note> into his friendship and gave them his two daughters, both named Tullia, yet of unequal mind and spirit, just as the two brothers were. 

And the elder, cunning sister Tullia was given to Aruns the mild; and to Lucius, the sharp-witted man of evil schemes, was given as a wife the gentler sister, the younger Tullia. Nevertheless, Lucius constantly considered through various desires how he might come to the kingdom, but his wife was good-natured. On the other hand, Aruns was good-natured, but his wife, the elder Tullia, was excessively bold, complaining about her husband's soft-heartedness—that he had no courage—whereas Lucius was a real man and born of royal blood. 

And she hated her sister because fortune had given her such a man, while she [the younger sister] was so weak-hearted and unhelpful to him. But since evil is compatible with evil, like began to draw toward like, as usually happens; and the beginning of all this trouble came from the women. And when the bold Tullia had learned all the secrets of her brother-in-law Lucius, she withheld no words from him regarding her husband and his wife. 

She said she had a stay-at-home <note>original: "postuezler" — likely referring to someone inactive, a "bench-sitter," or someone lacking ambition.</note> who was not worth being called a man, being of such soft spirit. Likewise, he [Lucius] had a soft-hearted, ignorant wife; and she said it would be better for him to be a monk <note>original: "münich" — an anachronism used by the translator to describe a life of quiet retreat, which would be seen as shameful for a Roman of Lucius's standing.</note> than to waste his days with a woman who was so unlike him in spirit. ### The 78th Leaf.

...she would have had the kingdom in her house, which at this time remained with her father, if only she had had him as a husband <note>original: "zeman," an archaic term for husband or spouse</note>. Not long after, through the mutual agreement of them both, it came to pass that Aruns and the younger Tullia were carried as corpses to their graves, one shortly after the other. And both evil-inclined spirits, now placed in the state of widowhood, were wedded to one another with the king's knowledge, but not with his will.

From then on, envy toward the king began to grow hourly, and Tullia rested neither day nor night, constantly inciting her husband to seize the kingdom, and she said to him: "You know what I have long lamented: that I did not have a man of strong spirit, just as your wife was of a different mind than you. For this reason, they have both died. But consider that this did not happen in vain; my complaint was that I had a husband who did not deem himself worthy of the kingdom. 

Now I have you, a high-minded and manly one, to whom the kingdom is due, if you will only consider that it belongs to you—for you are the closest heir of the Tarquin lineage <note>original: "tarquĩorũ," referring to the Latin *Tarquiniorum*</note>. Therefore, you should not be idle, if you are indeed the man I believe myself to be wedded to, and would rather possess the kingdom now than merely live in the hope of future rule. 

But if your spirit were changed, then everything would become far worse, should you choose to be soft-hearted after the evil already committed. Therefore, be manly, considering that the kingdom is ordained for you by the gods and belongs to you by inheritance from the house of the Tarquins, so that I may call you my king." With these and many other words of reproach, she constantly incited the young man, so much so that he secretly plotted to bring the people to his side in various ways, [proclaiming] his royal descent and...

### The 68th Leaf

Recounting hereditary right. Now enticing the leaders, now bribing the youth, now promising much for the future with gifts, he waited until the appropriate time arrived to carry out his evil intention. And one day, well-provided with many of his followers, he entered the hall and sat boldly upon the royal throne and commanded all the fathers <note>The "fathers" refers to the *Patres*, the Roman Senators.</note> and the leaders of the city to come quickly to the palace. Some, who knew of the plot beforehand, were quick to arrive as they were expecting it. Others, who did not know of these things, came out of fear. Some came to see the strange undertaking. 

Indeed, let everyone take note that this was done against King Servius. When the people were assembled, Lucius Tarquinius began to complain about Servius with a long speech, making various accusations to turn the people against him. When the king became aware of this, he ran immediately into the hall, shouting: "Tarquinius, what thing is this? With what audacity do you dare, while I live, to summon the fathers or occupy my royal throne?" 

Tarquinius answered defiantly that he was occupying his father's royal hereditary throne, from which he—a born slave <note>original: "geborner aigen man." Tarquinius refers to the legend that Servius Tullius was the son of a captive woman, and therefore a slave by birth, making him unfit for the throne in the eyes of the conspirators.</note>—had been violently and unlawfully pushed through the influence of a woman; and in which he [Servius] had long enough mocked the royal blood through a compromise. 

Then a cry went up from both parties, in such a manner that it seemed the stronger one would rule. And the final moment of necessity came for each side to show its manhood. But Tarquinius was the more powerful, and jumping from the royal throne, he seized Servius, lifting him high, and threw him all the way down from the top steps of the stairs. And immediately he turned his power toward subduing the senate. King Servius fled with his servants, so that he might reach his house in his weakened state, but on the way, he was stabbed to death by the servants of Tarquinius.

<summary>Lucius Tarquinius publicly usurps the throne, physically assaults King Servius Tullius by throwing him down the palace stairs, and sends assassins to finish him off as he flees.</summary>

### Leaf 69

...ordered by his wicked daughter Tullia. Immediately, the same sinful Tullia drove in a chariot <note>original: "frewden wagen." While literally a "joy-wagon" or ceremonial carriage, its use here highlights the gruesome irony of her celebration during her father's murder.</note> to the palace. She called for her husband and was the first to address him as king, just as he had commanded her. However, as she was returning to her house and had come to the street called the Cyprian Way <note>original: "vicũ Cipriũ" (Vicus Cyprius). A residential street in ancient Rome.</note>, the driver pulled back the horses and, in great terror, showed the lady her murdered father, Servius, lying there. 

And to establish an eternal memory of such a base and inhuman sin, that same street was named the Wicked Street <note>original: "sceleratus vicus," meaning the Street of Crime or the Accursed Street.</note>, which is to say, the "sinful road." Because she had previously committed evil against her own husband and sister <note>Tullia had allegedly murdered her previous husband and her sister so that she could marry Lucius Tarquinius, who had similarly cleared his path to her.</note>, she felt no regret and avoided no misdeed. It is said of her that she commanded the driver to drive the wagon directly over the dead body of her father. It happened then that the wagon—and even her own clothes—were stained and defiled by the blood of her murdered father. 

Yet more murderous evil followed, born of an envious heart and ingratitude for the kindness they had received. Neither of them wished for their own father and father-in-law to be laid in the earth with well-deserved royal honors. Instead, they commanded that he be left lying shamefully unburied, and they spoke mockingly: "Romulus, the founder of the city of Rome, had no grave <note>According to Roman legend, Romulus did not die but was taken up to heaven in a storm to become the god Quirinus; thus, there was no body to bury.</note>, and he was of much better and divine birth. Therefore, this man, born of a servant girl <note>Servius Tullius was legendarily born to Ocrisia, a captured noblewoman who served in the palace of King Tarquinius Priscus.</note>, may also remain unburied." 

Thus they began to rule through their own power and with such arrogance, without any consideration for the advice of wise counselors. Instead, they ruled through innate evil cunning, so that he was called and named by everyone Lucius Tarquinius *Superbus*, which is to say, "The Proud" or "The Haughty." Yet after many evils were committed by them...

<summary>This page describes the aftermath of King Servius Tullius's murder, focusing on the infamy of his daughter Tullia, who drove her chariot over his corpse, and the rise of the tyrant Tarquinius the Proud.</summary>

### Leaf 69

...and because of the deadly evil committed by her son, Sextus Tarquinius, against Lucretia, they fled into exile and died miserably. Oh, deadly evil against nature! How could such a wicked plot against an innocent creature <note>original: "vihe," literally "beast," here used to emphasize the victim's perceived innocence or the inhumanity of the act.</note> enter a woman's heart—to say nothing of a father, who had always loved his children with great kindness? 

But no sin exists alone without being attached to another; and usually, where one evil is carried out according to one's will, it brings constant provocation toward even greater evil. Therefore, after the wicked murder of her husband, Aruns, and her sister, she became even more monstrous <note>original: "freißsamer," implying she became more terrifying, reckless, or hardened in her cruelty.</note> regarding her father’s death. 

For this reason, one must avoid the beginning of evil, because the slough of sin is sticky, clayey, and swampy; once a person falls into it, it is very hard to get back out again. So that this evil may be seen as even greater, I will now set against this betrayal the great faithfulness and steadfastness of the pure Lucretia, who was harmed in her body by that haughty family, yet remained undefiled in her spirit.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a somber scene centered on a bedchamber. Lucretia is shown lying in bed, while several men stand around her in various states of grief and resolve. Three figures are specifically labeled: **Collatinus** (Lucretia's husband), **Lucretia** herself, and **Brutus** (the kinsman who would lead the uprising against the Tarquins).</note>

<summary>This page reflects on the cumulative nature of sin, using Tullia's lack of remorse for her father's death as a warning, and introduces Lucretia as a virtuous counter-example.</summary>

### The 70th leaf.

When Lucretia pierced her chaste breast with the steel,
And as a torrent of blood flowed forth, she said:
"Let these witnesses go forth to prove I did not favor the tyrant:
My blood before the spirits of the dead, my soul before the gods."
<note>original Latin: "Cum foderet ferro castum lucrecia pectus / Sanguinis et torrens egrederetur ait / Procedant testes me non fauisse tyranno / Sanguis aput manes spiritus ante deos"</note>

### On Lucretia. The 47th chapter.

**L**ucretia, a leader of Roman honor among women and a holy, high ornament of all purity, was the daughter of the noble Roman named Lucretius Spurius Tricipitinus, and the wife of Collatinus. It is a matter of doubt whether she should be praised more for her physical beauty or for her character above all other honorable Roman women. 

And at the time when Tarquinius the Proud <note>original: "hochfretig," meaning arrogant or haughty. This refers to Tarquinius Superbus, the last King of Rome.</note> lay before the city of Ardea to conquer it, she—belonging to our town of Collatia <note>original: "vnsers von dẽ weiler collatium," referring to the town from which her husband took his name.</note>—moved out of the city and into her husband’s house. And now, as the siege was prolonged, they became...

<summary>This page introduces the story of Lucretia, framing her as the ultimate example of Roman female virtue and purity. It sets the scene during the siege of Ardea by Tarquinius the Proud, leading up to the events that would result in her famous tragedy.</summary>

<keywords>Lucretia, Roman history, virtue, Collatinus, Tarquinius Superbus, Collatia, Ardea, chastity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of Lucretia from Chapter 47. Lucretia was found working diligently while others were idling.</meta>

### The 70th leaf.

...as her working of wool proved <note>original: "wollen bewÿstend." Lucretia was famously found spinning wool with her servants, a mark of a virtuous Roman matron, while other noblewomen were feasting.</note>. For this reason, she was esteemed by everyone, and rightly so, as the most worthy of honor. When **Collatinus** saw how he was honored through his highly praised wife, he thought to honor her in return and invited all the royal youths into his house. And there, after they had been kindly received and were well and honorably satisfied with sufficient food, **Sextus**, the son of **Tarquinius the Proud** <note>original: "Tarquinij des hochfertigen keisers." Though Tarquinius was a King, the text uses "keiser" (emperor), a common title for Roman rulers in medieval and early modern German texts.</note>, began to cast his unchaste eyes greedily upon the honorable beauty of the chaste woman. He was so ignited by an overwhelming, foul passion <note>original: "überbrennend vnflätig feür," literally "over-burning filthy fire."</note> that he set his mind on possessing her body and defiling her lovely beauty. And he resolved that if this could not be done with her consent, it must then be accomplished by force.

A few days later, as this madness again seized him fervently, he set out secretly at night and rode from the army camp <note>original: "hōr," likely referring to the siege camp at Ardea.</note> toward **Collatia**, to the house of **Lucretia**. She let him in and received him kindly, without any suspicion of evil, because he was her husband’s closest kinsman <note>original: "vetter," meaning cousin or relative.</note>. 

But when **Sextus** noticed that the entire household was quiet and now heavy with sleep, he entered **Lucretia’s** bedroom with a drawn sword and spoke thus: "Above all else, **Lucretia**, I tell you this: if you let even one cry escape your lips, you shall be stabbed to death. Furthermore, know that I am fervently inflamed with passion for you; therefore, I desire that you willingly fulfill my pleasure. If you will not do so kindly, you shall be forced to it by the sword." 

But when he could not achieve his goal through threats—for she did not fear death if she could but preserve her womanly honor—he devised a damnable and wicked scheme to stain her purity. And he said:

<summary>Sextus Tarquinius, driven by a lustful obsession with Lucretia’s beauty and virtue, sneaks into her home at night. He threatens her with a sword, but finding that she values her honor more than her life, he begins to devise an even more malicious plan to coerce her.</summary>

<keywords>Sextus Tarquinius, Lucretia, Collatinus, virtue, lust, threat, Collatia, Roman history, chastity</keywords>

### The 71st leaf.

“...if you refuse to do my will, I will kill you and a servant beside you, and I will say: ‘I found you both together in sin.’ Then, out of duty to my friend <note>original: "schuldigē freūndtschaft," referring to his relationship with Lucretia's husband, Collatinus.</note>, I will claim I punished you both for your evil and adultery.” At these words, the chaste woman was deeply troubled and thought in her sorrowful heart: “If I am killed in this way, there would be no one to clear me of such a charge. Therefore, it is better to surrender my body to the adulterer, so that I may later prove my innocence through my own self-inflicted punishment.” When he had satisfied his filthy lust, he departed from there with joy, as if he had—in his own estimation—won a great battle. But **Lucretia** was heavily burdened and sorrowful over such a sinful evil. 

And as soon as day broke, she sent for her father **Tricipitinus** <note>Spurius Lucretius Tricipitinus, a distinguished Roman who later became a consul.</note>, and for **Brutus**, her husband **Collatinus’s** closest friend, who always held them in high regard. She also called for her other friends and her husband. When they arrived, she recounted to them all, weeping and in sorrow, what **Sextus** had done to her the night before. But when her friends, each and all together, began to comfort the weeping woman, she drew forth her sharp knife, which she had hidden within her garments. She spoke: “Though I have been cleared of the sin, I am not released from the punishment. Nor shall any woman ever take me as an example to live in shame after such an evil deed as this.” With these words, she plunged the knife into her innocent heart and, falling upon her face before her father and husband, she ended her life. 

O, the tragic beauty of this woman! O, the pure clarity of your spirit! Who can praise you enough, or exalt you as highly as you deserve? No one, except he who can truly contemplate what...

# The 74th leaf

### ¶ Of Leena the harlot, Chapter 47.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a woman labeled "Leena" being tortured on a rack. Two men stand to the left, observing the scene, and an executioner operates the mechanical rack on the right to stretch her body.</note>

->**Leena**<-

**Leena** was a Greek woman. And although she was of ill-repute <note>original: "verschämet," literally "shamed" or "disgraceful," referring here to her profession as a courtesan.</note>, yet with the permission of pious women, queens, princesses, and others, I place her also in the number of those who have performed certain notable deeds above others. For many remarkable stories are often accomplished by both evil and good men. Therefore, although she practiced shameful works, her manly deeds <note>original: "manlicher getāte." In this era, "manly" was a common term for "courageous" or "virtuous," regardless of gender.</note> should nevertheless not be forgotten. 

For at the time when Amyntas <note>Amyntas III, King of Macedon.</note> reigned in Macedonia, the noble youths Harmodius and Aristogeiton <note>original: "Armonius vnd ariston." These were the famous "Tyrannicides" of Athens, though the text erroneously places them in Macedonia.</note> rose up to deliver the land from the oppression of the tyrant <term>tyrant: an absolute ruler who governs without legal restraint; the original German "wüttrich" emphasizes a bloodthirsty or raging ruler.</term> Hippias <note>original: "hÿspar." The text refers to the Athenian tyrant Hippias or his brother Hipparchus.</note>. 

And after they had killed him, an inquiry and questioning was conducted by his successors to see if it could be discovered who had done this thing. Because of this, many people were captured, and among others, Leena herself; for because many of the youths frequented <note>original: "wandel... hetten," meaning to have social intercourse or move within a space.</note> her house for pleasure, she was suspected <note>original: "verarckwonet."</note> of having knowledge of such plots.

<summary>This page introduces Leena, a Greek courtesan, and justifies her inclusion in a history of notable women despite her profession. It sets the scene for her bravery during the aftermath of the assassination of a tyrant by Harmodius and Aristogeiton.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the story of Leena, the Greek courtesan, who was introduced on the previous page as an associate of the Athenian rebels. # The 74th leaf

And when she heard <note>The text shifts to the scene of her interrogation.</note> that she was to be tormented with manifold tortures so that she would surrender those whom she knew were guilty of the deed—although she intended, beyond words, to die rather than betray these youths—she was nevertheless moved by a fear that she might, through the agony of the pain, be made weak-willed enough to confess. Also considering how honorable and holy the name of true friendship was, and after strong denial, even as the torture increased and the frailty of her body grew, she fell into a stronger, more manly spirit <note>original: "manlichers gemüt." In the 15th century, "manliness" was often used as a synonym for "virtue" or "courage," regardless of the person's gender.</note>. And for that reason, so that she might make her purpose firm, she bit off her own tongue and spat it out, so that by this single, highly-famed deed, she took away all power of speech from herself. By this she knew that everyone would remain unbetrayed by her. 

Who would say that Leena, who was of such a strong spirit, would have been placed in such a shameful life except through the great misfortune of evil luck? Without doubt, whoever says that women can only keep silent about what they do not know has not truly recognized this woman. And truly, this woman fell into frivolity <note>original: "leichtfertikeit," here referring to her life as a courtesan.</note> not out of her own nature—for that was good—but out of idleness and sloth, as still happens to many who are not raised by their mothers to appropriate work.

**A**thaliah possessed a cruel spirit; within the lineage of David, she made herself notorious <note>original: "kunder," meaning more well-known or infamous.</note> even to the Syrians and Egyptians. For although her ancestors were already foul with the stench of many kinds of sinful bloodshed, she nevertheless cast a dark shadow over her royal crown, whereby she spread the name of Athaliah far and wide through her evil deeds. She was the daughter of Ahab, the King of Israel, and of Queen Jezebel, that most wicked of women. She was married to Joram, the son of King Jehoshaphat of Jerusalem. 

And afterwards, when his father Jehoshaphat died, as well as his elder brother Ahaziah <note>original: "Ozias." Medieval chronicles often vary from modern biblical spellings; here the text refers to the succession within the house of David. In the biblical account, Ahaziah was Joram's son, but some traditions or transcriptions conflate these names.</note>, who ruled after his father, her husband Joram was crowned king in Jerusalem; and he also desired that his <term>consort<gloss>A queen who is the wife of a reigning king; she shares his rank but does not necessarily hold sovereign power in her own right.</gloss></term> should rule with him. To that same royal throne, great dignity was added with visible honor and wealth through the death of her father-in-law Ahab and his own rule. [Ahab's body] lay among the dogs, shedding his blood through a thousand wounds. And her mother Jezebel, in royal finery, was thrown down from a high tower and so trampled and crushed by the riding and driving of horses and chariots that not a single trace of her wicked corpse remained; instead, nothing but dung and filth could be seen. And finally, when these things were fulfilled—so that she and her murderous bloodshed did not remain unpunished—her grandson <note>original: "suns sun," literally "son's son."</note> Joash, whom she did not know was still alive, was raised up as king through the help of the prince Jehoiada <note>original: "Joadam." In medieval German texts, biblical names often followed Latin or idiosyncratic spellings.</note>. She was dragged from the royal throne and, amid the shouts of all the people crying out against her, she was killed after she had reigned for seven years. Thus was the evildoer sent to hell by the same path she had sent many innocents. 

These are the works of God's justice. And though the punishment for sins may be delayed for a time, it later strikes all the harder, especially upon those who, after long waiting, do not wish to turn toward improvement. And if we do not consider this, or if we refuse to believe it—if we pay no heed to punishment but instead choose to burden ourselves with even greater guilt—then, when we least expect it, we shall be swallowed by the raging waves of the bottomless sea, where neither moaning nor crying out for our sins will be of any help. 

Shameful and disgraceful is all desire to rule without a lawful beginning; for without a legal right, there are two ways to enter into power, both of which are wicked and burdensome: through treachery or by force. To achieve treachery, one must use wicked cunning, enmity, perjury, and...

...Christians <note>original: "risten"; in this period, chroniclers often used "Christians" as a general term for the faithful or the righteous, even when discussing Old Testament history</note> killed or sent into exile; the wealthy ruined; old friends driven away. You strive for such "honors," O heart; consider whether it would not be better for you to be content with what is yours, which you might possess peacefully, honorably, and godly, without interference or fear for your life. For just as great things are won through bloodshed, so must they be maintained and protected with great worry and anxiety; and it often happens that wealth gained through the wicked is lost again because of them. And perhaps life itself perishes along with that wealth, by the hands of those who helped to win it through evil means.

<note>original: "Aspiceres pontem auderet quia vellere cocles / Et fluuium vinclis innaret cloelia ruptis." This Latin verse celebrates the bravery of Horatius Cocles and the heroine Cloelia during the siege of Rome.</note>

¶ **Chapter 50: Concerning Cloelia**

**C**loelia lived at the time when Tarquinius the Proud, the emperor <note>original: "keyser"; while Tarquinius was a king (Rex), the author uses the medieval term for a supreme ruler</note> of Rome, was driven out. This occurred because of the great evil his son Sextus had committed against the noble Lucretia <note>referring to the rape of Lucretia, the event that traditionally sparked the Roman revolution</note>. Because of this, great wars arose between the Romans and him. Because of their alliance, Porsenna, the King of Clusium of the Etruscans <note>original: "Etruscorum"</note>, came to Tarquinius’ aid. However, his crossing was prevented by the bravery of Horatius Cocles <note>original: "Oracij cloetis"</note>, so that he could not cross the Sublician Bridge <note>original: "brucken sublicij"</note>. They were also driven away by the boldness of Mucius Scaevola <note>a Roman youth famous for burning his own hand to demonstrate his resolve to King Porsenna</note>. 

Because of this, Porsenna reached a settlement with the Romans: to confirm the peace, the Romans were to give him many noble maidens of Rome as hostages. Among these, Cloelia was also chosen. Afterward, it happened one day that Cloelia considered deeply that it was not honorable for the public good to leave such heavy pledges—so many young women—in the hands of a foreign king. She stirred her maidenly spirit and raised it to a manly courage <note>original: "manheyt"; in the medieval worldview, extreme bravery in a woman was often described as her adopting "manly" virtues</note>, and she planned to [escape] by night from the guards with several of her companions...

<summary>This page introduces the story of the Roman heroine Cloelia, setting the scene during the war between the early Roman Republic and the exiled King Tarquinius the Proud. It highlights the bravery of Roman figures like Horatius Cocles and Mucius Scaevola before focusing on Cloelia’s role as a hostage.</summary>
<keywords>Cloelia, Tarquinius the Proud, Lucretia, Porsenna, Horatius Cocles, Mucius Scaevola, Roman Republic, hostages, womanhood, bravery</keywords>

¶ **Chapter 51: Concerning Hippo**

<note>A woodcut illustration shows a group of men in a small boat on the water; a woman, Hippo, is in the water beside the boat. This depicts her leap into the sea to escape her captors and preserve her chastity.</note>

->**Hippo**<-

**H**ippo was a Greek woman, but from what ancestors she came has not come down to us. However, from the one strong deed that we may read of her, we can imagine how great and manifold her many praiseworthy virtues must have been.

->### The 77th Leaf<-

...though a single swallow does not make a summer <note>A common proverb suggesting that one example is not enough to establish a rule, yet her individual deed is still worthy of its own merit.</note>, she should rightly not be forgotten. Once, when Hippo was captured by sea-robbers and carried away, she noticed how the robbers plotted to violate her honor, for she was exceedingly beautiful. But when her delicate chastity could find no way to escape from them, she jumped into the raging sea to prevent their shameful violence. And with the loss of her life, she preserved her chastity.

Who could not praise the stern resolve of this woman with great acclaim? She, who for the sake of perhaps a few remaining years of her life, wished to preserve her purity and placed herself into the eternal memory of praiseworthy deeds, so that her praise would never be erased from the books of remembrance. Her body, after being tossed about by the sea for a time, came to the land called Erythrae. There, she was at first buried simply by the inhabitants <note>original: "ȳmeonern," likely referring to the local people of Erythrae</note> as just another drowned corpse. 

However, when the news came of the reason for her death, they had a costly tomb built for her with great dignity upon the shore for the long-lasting memory of her chaste spirit. This was done so that all might recognize that the bright light of virtue cannot be dimmed by any kind of adversity.

This scene represents the Roman matron Veturia interceding with her son, the general Coriolanus, to spare the city of Rome from his attacking army.</note>

# The 78th Leaf
### Titus Livius, Decade 1, Book 2, Leaf 10.
## Chapter 52: Concerning Veturia

**V**eturia, the noble Roman matron, through her great deeds, has made the years of her old age green again <note>A metaphorical way of saying her actions brought her renewed life and lasting fame.</note>. She had a son named Gnaeus Marcius, who was stern and strong in both body and spirit. Because the fortified stronghold of the Volscians called Corioli—which the Romans were then besieging—was captured through his bravery, great wisdom, and strong hand, she gave him the surname "Coriolanus." This was done with such praise and honor that he was celebrated above all others for his deeds of knighthood <note>term: knighthood (ritterschafft). While the setting is ancient Rome, the author uses a medieval term to describe the military excellence of the noble class.</note>.

And after a time, when the Romans suffered a great shortage of food, much grain was brought there from Sicily through the help of the eldest Romans, whom they call the "Fathers of the Country" <note>original: "vätter des lanndes." This refers to the Roman Senate, known as the *Patres*.</note>. However, Coriolanus forbade with harsh speech that such grain should be distributed to the common people <note>original: "der gemeyn," referring to the plebeians.</note> until they had returned to the nobility the honor and dignity they had previously taken from them. And without doubt, because the populace was hungry, they would have laid violent hands upon him had their leaders not set a day for him to appear before the court to explain the reasons for his prohibition.

But because of his great resentment toward the common people, he did not appear on the appointed day; for this reason, he was condemned to exile <note>original: "ellend." In Early New High German, this term specifically referred to being in a foreign land or being banished.</note>. He then withdrew to the Volscians, who only a short time before had been his enemies. He was received well and kindly by them, for bravery is still held in great dignity and value everywhere. Through him and their chieftain, Attius Tullius, the Volscians were moved to march against the Romans in war. And this same Coriolanus was appointed chieftain of the people. They marched in great strength up to a trench at the city of Rome, and brought the Romans to such a state that their leaders...

<summary>This page introduces the noble Roman matron Veturia and the early military success of her son, Coriolanus, who is later exiled for blocking grain relief to the common people during a famine.</summary>

# The 78th Leaf

The council ordered an embassy to be sent to him whom they had condemned to exile <note>original: "ellend." While this word now means misery, in Early New High German it specifically referred to being "out of the land" or in exile.</note>, to ask for peace from him according to his will and conditions. But Marcius sent them back home with a scornful answer; for this reason, they were sent to him a second time, but they were again received poorly and sent away with indignity. 

For the third time, the clergy went—the high priest with the others—wearing their miters <note>original: "inseln," referring to the ceremonial headwear or fillets worn by Roman priests, styled here after medieval ecclesiastical miters.</note> and other spiritual garments, humbly desiring peace. But all their effort was in vain, and because of this, the entire city was cast into despair. 

The noble women of the city came daily with many kinds of lamentations to Veturia, Coriolanus’s mother, and to his wife named Volumnia, weeping, recounting their distress and seeking help. Through this, they prevailed upon the two women so that they both went out with a great multitude of the noble women into the enemy camp <note>original: "veind leger."</note> with tears and prayers to obtain peace and to appease her son; for indeed, the city could not be protected by the sword. 

As soon as Coriolanus heard of his mother’s arrival, although he was fiercely moved against the Romans, he nevertheless restrained his spirit at the approach of his mother. He stood up from his chair and went out of his tent to receive his mother. Veturia took his wife on one side and his sons <note>original: "sůn." This may refer to the couple's children or be a plural usage.</note> on the other. 

And before she reached him, she cast aside her gentleness and was moved to anger against her son; everything she had planned to ask of him humbly as she set out, she transformed into harsh words of rebuke when she arrived in the camp. And as she saw him walking toward her, she took on a strong spirit in her frail body and spoke: "Stand still, you ill-advised youth! I wish to know, before you come to embrace me, whether you approach me as..."

# The 79th Leaf

...to receive a captured enemy; I believe that is your intention. Woe is me, that the joys for which I desired you to live—that you were sent into exile <note>original: "ellend." In Early New High German, this term literally means "other-land" or "exile," but carries the heavy weight of the modern word "misery."</note>—and that you are seen by me as an enemy of the common good. I ask you: do you recognize upon what chair you sit, thus armed? Do you recognize what land you have before your eyes? Recognize it, if you do not know: it is the city in which you were conceived, in which you were born, in which you were nourished and raised with great labor. With what spirit, or by what impulse, could you bring violent war upon your fatherland? You have not considered what proper honor was shown to you by your mother upon your arrival; the sweetness of your wife did not remind you of your duty, and you have entirely despised kindness toward your children. And you would not receive the petition of the leaders of your fatherland. 

Your hard heart could yield to no petition so that you would let go of the anger which you—though the guilty one <note>Veturia is arguing that despite Coriolanus's grievances against Rome, his current rebellion makes him the more guilty party.</note>—have taken into yourself. When you looked upon this city, did you not think: "There is my father's house; there are my gods <note>original: "gött." This refers to the Roman *Lares* and *Penates*, the household deities central to Roman family life.</note> whom I should honor; there is my wife; there are my children; there is my unhappy mother through many kinds of misfortune." 

The best Romans, the eldest fathers, and the high priesthood have not been able to soften your stony heart, so that you would do through prayer that which you are rightfully bound to do, and should have done unbidden. I must lament my misery <note>original: "ellend."</note>, for I recognize that through my giving birth, I have been an adversary to myself and to the whole land. For whereas I thought I had given birth to a son and a good citizen, I have instead borne such an unhappy, stone-hearted enemy, who now allows himself in no way to be moved or overcome. Much better...

<summary>Veturia delivers a stinging rebuke to her son Coriolanus, reminding him of his duty to his birthplace, his family, and his gods while lamenting that she ever gave birth to such a stone-hearted enemy of Rome.</summary>

# The 80th Leaf

...which previously was common only to women in the Orient <note>Likely referring to the Near East or Persian regions, where the author believes women had more legal standing.</note>. And furthermore, that women might inherit, which previously had generally been withheld from them. Whether such granted favors <note>original: "pẽcz," likely a contraction of the Latin "beneficia," meaning legal favors or privileges.</note> were pleasing to the men, I shall leave unwritten <note>original: "laß ich in der feder bleiben," literally "I leave it in the pen."</note>; for because of such finery and adornment of the women, I have seen many a downfall of men into poverty. I could speak of much greater and further trouble, but I will remain silent for the sake of keeping the women's favor. Yet I could cast many scolding words at Veturia regarding the great pride that arose among women from this law, had the city of Rome not been saved and kept upright through her. However, I cannot praise the remaining freedom of women and the increase of the same from day to day, for less would have been enough.

¶ See Megullia <note>Megullia Dotata was a Roman woman known for her large dowry, symbolizing shifting economic power.</note>, the 54th chapter; you will find it on the 75th leaf.

### ¶ Of Thamyris the painter, the 54th chapter.

**T**hamyris <note>Also known in classical sources as Timarete.</note> was the best female painter in her time. She lived in the ninetieth Olympiad <note>A four-year period in the Greek calendar, placing her around 420 BCE.</note>, a daughter of Micon the painter of Athens. She abandoned all womanly work and devoted herself to her father's art, in which she became so practiced that during the reign <note>original: "zeptten," referring to the scepters or rule.</note> of King Archelaus in Macedonia, she held the highest crown and glory of the arts. This was achieved through a panel on which she had painted Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and the moon.</note>, which for a long time was kept as a great treasure for a long and laudable memory of such art by women. It is indeed worthy of memory when weighed against the distaff and spindles <note>The tools of spinning and weaving, traditional symbols of a woman's domestic sphere.</note> of other women.

# The 80th Leaf

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts two figures in a domestic but regal setting. On the right, Queen Artemisia stands, lifting a chalice to her lips. **Artemisia** <note>Artemisia II of Caria (d. 350 BCE), sister and wife of King Mausolus, famous for her grief and for building the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus.</note> was a queen in Caria, of a high and strong spirit, possessed of a holy and exceeding love for her husband, and of such firm chastity in her state of widowhood that her memory is justly held up as an eternal example for the widows of posterity. She had as her husband the noblest king in Caria, **Mausolus**, whom she loved so dearly during his life that she could not forget him after his passing. Of this, his wondrous tomb <note>The Mausoleum of Halicarnassus, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World.</note> bears witness. For when her beloved husband had closed his final day, she had the dead body buried with the highest royal honors. And after the burning of the body, according to their custom, she did not allow the ashes to be buried in a golden urn as other royal ashes are, but rather had them gathered with the utmost diligence and carefully kept. For she believed no other vessel was fit to hold them worthily but her own breast, wherein the flames of her old love should never be extinguished, even after his death. And for the eternal memory of the life now past, she always mixed into every drink a...

# The 81st Leaf

...a little of his ashes, so much that nothing of them remained unconsumed; spending all the remaining time in weeping and mourning, until her natural vitality <term>vitality<gloss>original: "seychaitkeyt" (moisture/humidity). In medieval medicine, it was believed that excessive grief "dried out" the body's essential vital fluids.</gloss></term> was consumed, and she went joyfully into death to her husband as she had intended.

Yet she accomplished great works in her widowhood <note>original: "witwen stül," literally "widow's chair," referring to her status or her reign during her period of mourning</note>; for it was an old custom to erect costly tombs for kings after their death, through which one might recognize the love of the spouse during their life. Therefore, Artemisia devised a wonderful, costly structure, upon which no expense was spared. In this, she would not be satisfied with just one, nor with a poor craftsman, but she sent for four of the most highly famous masters who could be found in all the lands of Greece, named **Scopas**, **Bryaxis**, **Timotheus**, and **Leochares**, whose likes were not to be found in the world. To them she commanded that a grand **Mausoleum** <note>Named after King Mausolus, this structure became so famous that its name became the root for the English word "mausoleum."</note> be built for her husband according to their highest arts, and carved from marble with such workmanship that her dear husband's memory might be made eternal through such a miracle of art.

And it is not unjust, seeing that it surpasses all buildings in the entire world in cost and artistry and is counted among the **Seven Wonders of the World**, that it should also be recorded by us in writing specifically; for through public renown, the work shall flourish again and the honorable praise of this illustrious lady shall be refined.

Thus the master craftsmen, by the command of the queen, shaped a four-cornered tomb at **Halicarnassus**, the capital city of the land of Caria. And on the side facing south <note>original: "mittag," literally "midday," the direction of the sun at its peak</note>, they made it sixty-three feet long; the other sides were shorter. The height reached a point of one hundred and seventy feet, and it was with thirty-six...

-># The 81st Leaf<-

...surrounded by small walls and columns. The part facing the East <note>original: "orient"</note> with all its decoration was assigned to **Scopas**. The North <note>original: "mitternacht," literally "midnight," a common archaic term for the North</note> was given to **Bryaxis**, the West <note>original: "occient"</note> to **Leochares**, and the fourth part to **Timotheus**. All four of them displayed such great skill and diligence in carving narratives <note>original: "hystozien," referring to relief sculptures depicting historical or mythological scenes</note>, engraving pillars, and other related tasks, that the figures might be esteemed by everyone as truly lifelike images, thanks to the utmost earnestness and industry of the masters; for each one wished to prove his art above the others and be praised for this wondrous work.

Yet Artemisia reached the end of her days before the work was completed. Nonetheless, the masters did not abandon the work, but rather—for the eternal memory of their ingenious arts and for the benefit of posterity—they finished it completely. It was finished with such wonderful craftsmanship that the entire work appeared as if it were carved from a single stone. Within it, many kinds of vaults and hollows were carved, designed so that no person could speak so quietly with another—in whichever vault they might be—that it would not be heard in all the others.

This high and widely famed work was thus completed in honor of King Mausolus, from whose name it was called the **Mausoleum**. From this, it follows that all royal tombs are still called <term>mausolea<gloss>original: "mauseola," the Latin plural of mausoleum</gloss></term> in Latin. From these things, the loyalty and love of Artemisia are rightly highly praised and clearly recognized; yet even higher and clearer is the constancy of her widowhood and her unceasing shedding of tears. No less remarkable is her pure body, in which the ashes of her husband—which she had consumed in the drink—rested as if in a precious grave.

This same Artemisia is to be elevated in praise not only for these things, but also for her strong virtue, <term>manly spirit<gloss>original: "manliches gemütes." In this historical context, "manly" was used to describe a woman who possessed exceptional courage and strength of character typically associated with warriors.</gloss></term> and boldness. For in knightly deeds she was well practiced and highly capable, and her graceful name...

-># The 82nd Leaf<-

...from the sea, stable and secret, unseen by the enemies. And as soon as she received the signal from the citizens upon the walls that the Rhodians <note>original: "thodiſer," referring to the people of Rhodes</note> had entered the city and had left their ships—showing their power in the city as if it were already won—she let her people fall upon them from behind, and also within the city with all their might. The hidden citizens of the city revealed themselves to be brave in battle, and the enemies were attacked on all sides and hard-pressed. There was no way for them to flee, and they were all slain. 

And all the ships of the Rhodians remained there, fully equipped. Immediately, Artemisia thought with a noble mind to accomplish manly deeds; she manned those same ships with her own armed men, well-prepared, and had them decorated with laurel branches and other signs of victory, and sailed swiftly toward Rhodes. The guards of the gates and the city watched expectantly <note>original: "erſtreckt," meaning they stretched or reached out to see better</note> when they saw their ships coming with signs of victory. They opened all the gates of the sea and of the city with such joyful haste that they never noticed they were receiving enemies instead of friends, until with a strong hand she conquered the unsuspecting citizens and held the city. 

There she had the lords of the city put to death, and as an eternal sign of victory, she commanded that victory columns be erected in the center of the marketplace. Two bronze columns were made there, which stood for many years afterward: on one was a joyful, victorious image of Artemisia; on the other, a sorrowful image with a downcast face representing Rhodes, with meaningful inscriptions of this remarkable history. 

Thus she made the city tributary to her and turned her way back to her home. Furthermore, when Xerxes, the most powerful king of the Persians <note>original: "persarū," Latin *Persarum*</note>, marched by land and by sea against the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> with the intent to conquer them and all the land of Greece...

### Concerning Megulia, the 53rd Chapter.

<note>A decorative initial "E" is set within a square frame, though the text identifies the subject as Megulia.</note> Megulia <note>The transcription reads "Egulia," likely an error by the printer or rubricator who inserted the wrong initial letter.</note>, as I believe, was a noble Roman woman. And in the holy times of the poverty of this world’s existence—long before knighthood fell into proud, ostentatious, and costly adornment of clothing and other pleasures—she was named *Dotata* <note>Latin: "the endowed" or "the dowried."</note> by the ancient Romans. This was because, through the generosity <note>original: "geyden," likely referring to the "Güte" or "Gaben" (kindness/gifts) of her kin.</note> of her friends, she was endowed for her husband with a wonderfully large gift, far above other maidens according to the custom of that time. For *Dotata* is as much as to say "the endowed one." Therefore, for many years it remained the custom that whenever a maiden was gifted beyond the common rate, she was called "Megulia Dotata." And yet, her dowry was worth no more than a few thousand copper coins <note>original: "kupfer pfenning."</note>.

O good simplicity! O praiseworthy poverty! How has the strange—and perhaps even ridiculous—path of the world now become so common? For we have everywhere so far overstepped the proper goal that nowadays one can hardly find a leather-tanner, shoemaker, carpenter, tailor, merchant, or knight who is willing to take a wife with such a gift as was given to this Megulia, who was then called "highly endowed." And it is no wonder, for common women have...

For according to our desires, our haughty minds are never satisfied.

...of her father toward her, and the evil love of the fervent suitor <note>The "fervent suitor" refers to Appius Claudius, whose "love" is portrayed as a destructive, lustful obsession.</note>. For at the time when the ten men <note>The Decemviri, a commission of ten men appointed to codify Roman law around 450 BCE.</note> chosen to rule the city of Rome had already ruled for two years, this same maiden was betrothed by her father to a very stern young man called Lucilius Icilius, a tribune. And because of the business of the wars then running, Virginius was sent out by the Romans, which caused the wedding to be delayed. While matters stood thus, it happened to the misfortune of the maiden that one of the ten men, Appius Claudius—who remained to rule in the city alone with one of his companions named Spurius Appius <note>In historical accounts, this is usually Spurius Oppius.</note>—was moved by her great beauty with such fervent love that he would spare neither effort, labor, nor expense to obtain her.

But when he noticed that her tender, blessed, and maidenly spirit could be softened neither by flattery, nor prayers, nor by threats, nor by high-mindedness, nor by great gifts; and considering that it could not be done by open force, he became more and more inflamed in his love for her. He pondered how he might have her through evil cunning, and he arranged with one of his helpers named Marcus Claudius that, as soon as he conveniently could, he should attack the maiden in the street as if she were his own bondservant <note>original: "eygen menſch," referring to a slave or person owned as property.</note> who had been abducted and had fled from him, and thus lead her into his house. And if anyone should try to interfere with him in this, he should then demand a legal judgment before him <note>Meaning Marcus should appeal to Appius Claudius himself, who was the presiding judge.</note>.

A few days later, the deceitful man seized her and said she was his. She cried out and resisted to the best of her ability with the help of the other women who walked with her. Because of this struggle, a great crowd of people gathered. Among them also came Icilius, her betrothed, and after many exchanged words, it came to pass that they went before the judge, who loved her...

With these words, he plunged the knife into the maiden’s heart. To the great horror, lamentation, and sorrow of all the people present, the pure, unfortunate maiden soon fell to the earth, and her soul departed along with her chaste blood. The shedding of innocent blood was overcome through this history, and by bringing the case of Virginia and Icilius before the common people of Rome, it came to pass that the Ten Men <note>The Decemviri, a group of ten men who had been granted absolute power to reform Roman law but became tyrannical.</note> were forced to give up their power and the government of the Roman Empire. Thus, the city and all the people were set into freedom once again. 

Not long afterward, a day of trial was set for Appius Claudius regarding the matter through the efforts of Virginius <note>original: "virginey," referring here to the father, Virginius, who had become a leader of the people.</note>, who now ruled, having been elected by the common people. And when Claudius gave his answer, he was dragged into the dungeon by the command of Virginius and bound with chains so that he could not escape the death he deserved for the deceit he had committed against that pure heart. 

And soon thereafter, he was forced with a rope to send his soul out of his body and down to hell <note>Though the text mentions a rope, historical accounts vary on whether Appius Claudius committed suicide in prison or was executed.</note>. But Marcus Claudius <note>The client of Appius who had falsely claimed Virginia was his slave.</note> was not punished according to his guilt, for he was protected by flight; nevertheless, the property of both men was confiscated and placed into the common treasury. 

What is worse than an unfair and false judge? For as often as he intends to do wrong, it is inevitable that he perverts the entire order of justice, the power of the law is despised, all virtue is wounded, and all that is good for the common utility is trodden underfoot. Even if this were not already obvious, it was clearly brought to light through the history of the unjust judge Appius. 

Because he wished to satisfy his evil lust, he sought to give a free maiden into slavery by his false judgment. He would have made an adulteress out of a maiden and a concubine out of a bride. Through his evil ruling, he provided the cause for a father to arm his own hands against his dearest daughter, so that fatherly kindness was turned into a state of violence <note>original: "fraisame," an archaic term for a terrifying act or a state of deadly cruelty.</note>.

## Concerning Irene, Daughter of Cratinus
Irene was a daughter of Cratinus, the painter, and she is praised and honored so much more because she became more skillful than her master; for her name is still celebrated among painters. The great mastery of this Irene is proven by a portrait of a maiden on a panel painted by her, located in the city of Eleusis in Greece. Likewise, a portrait of the elder Calypso, and of Alcisthenes the fighter <note>The original text reads "absatem," likely a corruption of "Alcisthenes," a famous athlete often associated with her work in classical sources.</note>, which were all made with such mastery that it is a strange thing to hear of a woman. And for many long years, they were considered works of high art; therefore, I did not wish to pass her by without praise.

## Concerning Leontium, Chapter 59
Leontium, if I have considered it correctly, was a Greek woman who was highly esteemed in the times when Alexander the Great reigned. Yet she would have been honored much more for her sharp mind if she had maintained womanly shame and feminine modesty. The ancients write of her that she was so highly learned in all arts that, through feminine audacity <note>The original "gefürsakeÿt" implies a bold or forward nature.</note>, or perhaps out of a particular hatred, she dared to write reproachfully against the highest master of nature living at that time, Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus was a Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle in the Peripatetic school.</note>, seeking to wound and injure him and his art. However, since her writings have survived through so many years until our own time, it is well to remember that she must have been a mistress of great arts, even though she was envious and not born of low stock, but of noble blood. But her dishonorable character <note>This likely refers to historical accounts of Leontium as a *hetaira* (courtesan) as well as a philosopher.</note> has darkened her shining blood and her enlightening art, for—

<summary>This page profiles two notable women of antiquity: Irene, a Greek painter whose skill surpassed her father's, and Leontium, a philosopher who famously challenged Theophrastus.</summary>

of all the world, of the blood of Aeacus <note>original: "eacis"; Aeacus was a mythological king of Aegina and a legendary ancestor of the kings of Epirus.</note>. She had a brother, Alexander, the king of Epirus. And she was married to the king in Macedonia, Philip. And she bore—though not from Philip—a son, Alexander, whose deeds were so wonderful <note>or "miraculous"</note> that before him there was no one, nor was any born after him, who could equal him in worldly honors. This added no small luster to his mother, for mothers are always illuminated by their children. However, that luster could not remain entirely pure; it was somewhat darkened by her weak spirit <note>original: "waichen müt"; this suggests a fickle or emotional temperament often attributed to women in medieval literature.</note>, because nothing more shameful could happen to such a queen than the common suspicion that Alexander was born to her through adultery. Philip was so moved by this that he not only publicly accused her of adultery, but also drove her out from him. And he took in her place as wife Cleopatra, the daughter of King Alexander of Epirus <note>Note: Boccaccio/the translator here refers to Philip's marriage to Cleopatra Eurydice, though the genealogy provided in the text is simplified.</note>. How heavily Olympias bore this banishment was shown by her behavior; for except for that one evil <note>the reputation of adultery</note>, she had always been a highly dignified queen. And afterwards, her name was spread far into the world through many kinds of misdeeds; for it is found written of her that she incited the noble youth Pausanias (born of the family of Orestis) to the death of her husband Philip, also with the knowledge of Alexander, her unmarried son. This is also quite believable, because after Pausanias was crucified <note>While historical accounts of the assassination of Philip II usually describe Pausanias being killed while fleeing, this text follows the tradition that he was executed by crucifixion.</note> for the killing of Philip, one found the next day on his head, as he was hanging thus on the cross, a golden crown, which had been placed on him by her order. And in a few days thereafter, his body was taken from the cross, as Olympias had arranged, and was burned with royal honors and dignity upon the ashes of Philip, as was the custom in Macedonia, and to . . .

¶ **Of Flora the harlot, the wife of Zephyrus, the 64th chapter.**

**F**lora was a Roman woman; and as much as her shameful profit took away from her feminine dignity, so much did fortune spread her name even further. She was an exceedingly wealthy woman, as all the ancient masters say. However, there is disagreement as to how she acquired her wealth. Some, and the more accurate ones, say she abandoned all the grace and beauty of her blossoming youth and delightful body among common women, pimps <note>original: "ruffian"</note>, and others, defying the young men and tainting her body with shameful defilement. And now these, then those, she enticed to her with flattering, friendly words and impure deeds—as is the habit of such harlots—so that they were inflamed with evil love, so strongly that she could easily snatch all their goods from them. And as soon as one fell into poverty, he was abandoned and another taken in his place; through this, she gained such vast wealth that few could equal her. But others wished to speak somewhat more honorably of her and recounted a mocking story <note>original: "schimpfliche geschicht"</note>: how in Rome the high guardian of the temple of Hercules, in a moment of leisure, played a game of dice against himself. He played on behalf of Hercules, and the stakes were to be a fine dinner and a beautiful mistress <note>original: "bülen"</note> to sleep with the god that same night. Whatever he threw with his right hand would count for Hercules, and the throw of his left hand for himself. It so happened that Hercules won, and therefore the guardian brought Flora to him...

# The 91st leaf
## ¶ Of Romana, the young woman. Chapter 64

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the classic scene of "Roman Charity." On the left, a man sits on a bench working with a tool, perhaps a craftsman or a jailer. In the center, an official in a hat stands holding a sword or staff of office. On the right, behind a barred window, a young woman is seen breastfeeding an older person. Below the window, the label "romana" identifies the scene.</note>

Romana was a young woman who lived in the time of her great and virtuous deeds; for this reason, she is rightly to be placed among the highly honored women of worthy memory, even though the name of her lineage has been worn away <note>original: "verschliffen," meaning the name has been lost or obscured by the passing of time</note> by antiquity. She had a mother of honorable lineage who was unfortunate <note>original: "ungesellig," which usually means unsociable, but here implies she fell into a state of isolation or legal trouble</note>, for she was condemned to death by the judge—for what crime, I do not know. 

And she was given by the highest captain to the jailer in the prison, so that he might execute her during the night, as she was of noble birth <note>Nobles were often executed privately at night to avoid the public spectacle and shame of a common execution.</note>. But the jailer, when he was supposed to kill her, felt a certain pity for the noble woman, such that he could not kill her with his own hands; instead, he locked her alive in a cell with the intention of letting her starve to death there. 

It so happened that Romana, through many prayerful pleas, was allowed into the prison to see her mother; however, she was first thoroughly searched to see if she was bringing any kind of food against the prohibition. Finding nothing, the young daughter gave her hungry mother her own [breasts] to suckle...

# The 86th leaf

She did this daily for so long that the guard began to wonder how the condemned woman could live such a long time. And watching what the daughter was doing when she was with her mother, he saw how she nourished her mother from her own breasts. He told the triumvir <note>original: "dryman," referring to the *triumviri capitales*, the Roman officials in charge of prisons and executions</note> of the daughter’s great kindness toward her mother. The triumvir then reported this further to the praetor <note>A high-ranking Roman judicial official or magistrate; the text also equates him to a "burgermeister" or mayor.</note>; this same praetor told the whole council. And it was unanimously agreed that the guilty, condemned mother should be released to her daughter on account of her kindness.

¶ The great kindness of this daughter: for when one citizen saves another in battle and helps preserve his life, he is praised from the heart and crowned with unique honor. But what praise can we find that would be sufficient to crown this woman who nourished her mother with the milk of her own breasts? I do not know where to seek such words, even if I searched among all the trees <note>A poetic idiom meaning the author cannot find a comparable example anywhere in the world.</note>. This is not only a holy act, but a miraculous kindness. It is not even to be compared to natural gifts, but placed above them. For nature teaches us to nourish children in their tender youth because of their frailty; but this woman snatched her mother from death. Therefore, she is rightly honored as a model for all virtuous children.
