# The 87th leaf

## Chapter 65: Concerning Marcia Varronis

<note>A woodcut depicts two scenes of artistic creation: on the left, a woman labeled "Varronis" carves a statue with a mallet and chisel; on the right, a woman labeled "Marcia" works at an easel, painting a portrait. Although the labels suggest two people, they refer to one woman: Marcia, daughter of Varro.</note>

**M**arcia Varronis, the eternal virgin, is to be exalted so much above other virgins because she surrendered herself to constant chastity of her own free will, without being forced. She was not like those others who are pushed into the temple strongholds of the goddess or of Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the moon and hunting, associated with virginity. The author contrasts Marcia’s personal choice with the institutionalized celibacy of the Vestal Virgins.</note>, or who enter through vows and must therefore maintain their purity in consecrated garments.

This Marcia had, with her whole spirit, overcome the sharp sting <note>original: "angel," literally a hook or sting, used here to describe the lure of temptation</note> of the inciting flesh, which even the most eminent men often could not withstand; she kept her body undefiled by any stain until her death. And while this Marcia and such steadfastness are highly praised, she is nonetheless no less to be honored for the exercise of her spirit and reason, as well as her great art and the skillful use of her hands. 

For whether this skill came from teachers or from nature is unknown; however, because she completely disregarded the "useless" works of women <note>original: "weibliche wenig nütze werck," referring to traditional domestic tasks like spinning or weaving, which the author considers intellectually inferior to the fine arts.</note>, she devoted herself so entirely to sculpting and painting that she, with the brush to paint...

<summary>This page introduces Marcia Varronis, a Roman woman celebrated both for her voluntary lifelong virginity and her exceptional talent in the fine arts of painting and sculpture.</summary>
<keywords>Marcia Varronis, virginity, chastity, female artists, painting, sculpture, Diana, Roman history, Heinrich Steinhöwel, woodcut illustrations</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Roman artist Marcia (daughter of Varro), moving from her general artistic talents to her specific achievements in ivory carving and her personal moral philosophies regarding the depiction of the human form.</meta>

# The 87th leaf

...and to carve figures out of ivory. She surpassed Sopolis and Dionysius <note>original: "sopolī vñ dyonisiū." These were famous Greek painters mentioned by Pliny the Elder; Marcia is being compared to the masters of antiquity.</note>, who were the most excellent painters of that same era. And even if other good painters were found from time to time, none was ever seen to be more skilled in that art than she was.

This is even more worthy of praise: a great testament to her skill was a portrait she made, which was preserved for a long time after her death. In it, she had depicted her own likeness <note>original: "eygē gestalt," her own form or figure</note> with such precision in every limb and color that no one who had ever seen her could fail to recognize her.

To recount some of her particular habits, she made it a rule to only paint portraits of women or to carve them in ivory, and rarely or never created the image of a man. I believe that her maidenly modesty <note>original: "Junckfrawliche scham"</note> was the cause of this. For since ancient times, it was the custom to create figures that were almost entirely naked. It was her opinion that if she did not render the naked male form with the correct proportions of the limbs, she would be criticized for her ignorance. However, if she *did* craft them skillfully in all their members, she would have to cast aside her virginal shame to study them. Therefore, because she did not wish to fall into either of these unseemly positions, she thought it better to avoid both <note>That is, she chose to avoid both the "ignorance" of bad anatomy and the "indecency" of studying the male nude.</note> and leave the male figure unmade.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts two distinct scenes. On the left, the goddess Venus is shown standing in a tub or large shell, with doves—her traditional symbols—flying above her. On the right, a woman labeled "Iuphra" leads a group of women. While the text discusses the artist Marcia, these figures likely serve as mythological or moral counterpoints regarding womanhood and beauty. The label "Venus" identifies the goddess of love, while "Iuphra" may be a variant of a classical name like Euphrosyne.</note>

# The 88th leaf

## ¶ Chapter 66: Concerning Sulpicia, the wife of Fulvius.

**S**ulpicia was once judged the most honorable woman by the testimony of all the noble women of Rome. She maintained her chastity and honor with no less conviction than Lucretia <note>Lucretia was a legendary figure in Roman history whose suicide after being raped by the king's son sparked the revolution that established the Roman Republic; she was the Roman archetype of female virtue.</note>, who killed herself to give testimony to the purity of her heart. She was the daughter of Servius Paterculus and the wife of Fulvius, both of whom came from the most noble Roman families.

And at one time, as the Senate, through the Decemvirs <note>original: "die zehen man." These were the "ten men" or officials responsible for overseeing sacred religious duties in Rome.</note>, had ordered an image to be dedicated to the goddess Venus, the "Changer of Hearts" <note>original: "veneri der hercz verkererín." This refers to *Venus Verticordia*, the aspect of the goddess who turned women's hearts toward virtue and away from lust.</note>, according to the instructions found in the Sibylline Books <note>A collection of oracular utterances purchased by the last king of Rome, consulted by the Senate in times of crisis.</note>. This was done so that maidens and other women might not only turn away entirely from unchastity, but also so that they might more easily acquire and preserve womanly discipline and honor.

The Senate requested, following the order of the Decemvirs, that the single most pure and chaste woman among all the Roman women should dedicate this same image. And out of the immense multitude of noble and honorable Roman women, one hundred of the most highly praised were chosen; among these was Sulpicia. From those same hundred, ten of the most honorable were selected; among these ten was Sulpicia. Finally, by order of the Senate and through a common vote of the women, one was chosen unanimously—and that was Sulpicia. 

And although it was a very distinguished honor in those times to dedicate the statue to Venus, it is immeasurably more beautiful that she was esteemed the most illustrious in honor by such a great multitude of women. She was placed above all other women for her constant chastity and was looked upon by everyone as if she were a goddess descended from heaven. For she possessed within her, beyond all others, everything that belongs to a highly honored woman. For it is not enough for a truly honorable woman to merely guard herself against

<summary>This page describes the selection of Sulpicia, daughter of Paterculus, as the most chaste woman in Rome to dedicate a statue to Venus Verticordia. It emphasizes the rigorous multi-stage selection process used by the Roman Senate and the female populace to identify the supreme example of virtue.</summary>

# The 88th leaf

[...from] the embrace of other men or adultery; for a husband
is thus protected so that she cannot be forced to do such things. 
Therefore, so that a woman may be called entirely honorable, 
she must tame and cast down her wandering, unchaste 
eyes, and cover and protect them with veils <note>original: "hůlltůchern." These were traditional head coverings or veils worn by respectable married women as a sign of modesty and social status.</note>. 
She should also constrain her tongue, not only to 
honorable words, but to few and necessary 
speech. Idleness is harmful to chastity; 
it is an enemy that must be fled before all others. 
An honorable woman should withdraw from gluttony <note>original: "schleckmeilung," referring to a love of sweets or dainty, luxurious foods.</note>, 
for without excess and the pleasure of food, Venus <note>The goddess of love, here personifying sexual desire.</note> grows cold. 
Singing, dancing, and leaping are to be avoided 
as thieves of levity and unchastity. 
One should manage the household with 
moderation and the rejection of pleasure; 
one should stop up the ears against excessive talk, 
much chattering, and the telling of tales. 
She should guard against wandering about too much and despise 
excessive finery in clothing. Evil thoughts and harmful 
desire should be quickly trodden underfoot, while 
keeping watch in holy works and contemplations. 
And so that I do not recount everything: a 
praiseworthy, highly honored woman should honor 
her husband with the highest love and place all others 
behind him, yet love them with brotherly 
loyalty. She should also not come to the embrace 
of her own husband without a blush and a modest 
spirit, and always be in the hope of being 
pregnant by him—the purpose for which marriage 
was established. And since such 
circumstances were perhaps not found so purely and 
completely in all the others—not among the hundred, 
nor even among the ten <note>Following the guidance of the Sibylline books, the Romans narrowed a search for the most virtuous woman from one hundred candidates down to ten, and finally to Sulpicia alone.</note>—as they were in the 
unique Sulpicia <note>original: "Supplicia."</note>, she was 
rightfully preferred above all others in purity.

# The 84th leaf.

...to accomplish this so fully that she, undaunted by the sharpness of death's swords, would not flee. Nor would she say that she was not the one they sought to kill. But the dear Harmonia, while she was hidden, nevertheless saw the faithful constancy of the innocent maiden and her strong spirit even unto death through the wounds of her chaste blood-shedding. Because of this, she was justly and deeply pierced <note>The German *erstocket* means both "stabbed" and "choked/pierced with emotion." In this context, it refers to her profound grief and guilt.</note>. And although she might have escaped after the departure of the tyrants <term>wüttrich|A term for bloodthirsty rulers or those who rage with senseless violence; here referring to the revolutionary mob.</term>, she nevertheless began to reflect inwardly through the great goodness of her heart; and with tears in her eyes, she marveled at that constant faithfulness <note>original: "vntrew." She could not prolong her life any longer without repayment, and she resolved in her mind that it was better to travel to the underworld in the company of the faithful than to shudder through life in the company of treacherous citizens.

This ancient, good faithfulness, which had escaped from the murderers' hands, stepped forward and summoned back the bloody swords. She recounted the ruse of her governess and the innocence of the faithful maiden, and made herself known, offering herself up to the bloodletting; and through many wounds, she was willingly hacked to pieces. In this way, she showed her gratitude to the faithful maiden beside her dead body.

## Of Busa of Canosa from Naples, Chapter 68

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a woman labeled "Busa" standing on the right. She gestures toward a group of men seated around a circular table, some of whom are eating or drinking. The indoor scene portrays her hospitality toward the Roman survivors after their defeat.</note>

Busa, whom some call Paulina, originated from the city of Canusium in Apulia <note>The text mentions Naples in the heading, but correctly identifies the location as Canusium (modern-day Canosa di Puglia) in the body. This region was then part of the broader Kingdom of Naples.</note>. She was wealthy and of high nobility, as her eternally great deed—clearly shown to ten thousand <note>The OCR reads "lx weisen," but the historical record and the later text "zehetauſent" confirm this refers to the 10,000 Roman survivors of the Battle of Cannae.</note>—demonstrates. For as the ancients say, in the times when Hannibal harassed the Romans with heavy wars and ravaged all of Italy with fire and iron <note>A common idiom of the time, "fire and iron" refers to the "fire and sword" of total war.</note>, and stained the entire land with manifold blood-shedding, a battle was fought near Naples at the small town of Cannae. In this battle, Hannibal not only overcame the Romans, but he also broke almost the entire power of the whole land called Italy. It happened that from this battle and the great slaughter, some had escaped in flight. About ten thousand of them, traveling by various devious paths, came by night to Canusium, for that city was still in alliance with the Romans. These same miserable, weary, poor, and weaponless...

<summary>This page concludes the story of Harmonia's suicide and introduces Busa of Canosa, a noblewoman who provided refuge to 10,000 Roman soldiers following their disastrous defeat by Hannibal at the Battle of Cannae.</summary>
<keywords>Busa of Canosa, Paulina, Hannibal, Battle of Cannae, Roman History, hospitality, refugees, Apulia, Naples</keywords>

# The 85th leaf

...destitute, naked, and severely wounded, Busa received them into her own house with kindness. She remained undismayed by such great misfortune or the power of the victorious enemy <note>The enemy refers to Hannibal and the Carthaginian army, who had just devastated the Roman forces.</note>. Above all else, she comforted the spirits of the wretched men with the hope of better fortune, and she provided doctors and medicine for the injured with motherly loyalty and love. She clothed the naked and generally demonstrated her generosity toward every necessity. She gave them daily sustenance from her own wealth. 

And when the wretched men were well-rested and restored to good hope through her courtly kindness, and were now ready to depart from her, they were so well-provided by her with provisions for the road that her generous hands were never drawn back from any need. This is, without doubt, wonderful to tell and far more praiseworthy in a woman than in a man. 

For the Great Alexander, the conqueror of the world, is most highly praised by the ancient writers for his generosity, and because he gifted his friends not just with small things—precious stones, money, horses, and the like—but honored them with great principalities, mighty kingdoms, and vast empires. He even occasionally honored those who had been conquered by him. 

Oh, how beautiful and how lofty that is to praise in him! But in my opinion, it is not to be compared to Busa's praiseworthy great deeds. For Alexander was a man, while Busa was a woman—who are generally and by nature more inclined to be stingy <term>original: "zäher" (tough or tight-fisted)</term> and more prone to withhold <term>original: "entpfalten" (keeping something folded away)</term> than to give. Furthermore, she was the mistress of her own property in her own right, whereas he had taken what he possessed from others by force. She had possessed her wealth peacefully through inheritance and, had she wished, could have possessed it much longer. He, perhaps, feared that he might not be able to keep what he had won through war. He gave to friends and those who had served him well; she gave to strangers and un...

<summary>Busa of Canosa provides medical care, clothing, and food to 10,000 Roman refugees, outshining even Alexander the Great’s famous generosity because she gave from her own inheritance to strangers.</summary>

# The 96th leaf

...recognized. He <note>Referring to Alexander the Great.</note> gave to the fortunate, while she gave to the wretched who were surrounded by misfortune. He gave foreign things in foreign lands; she gave under her own roof among her own people. He gave for worldly fame and honor; she gave so that she might show help to the needy and miserable. Why should I say more? When we consider the inner spirit, the womanhood, and the nature of both cases, I have no doubt that an impartial judge would praise Busa and her generosity far more highly than Alexander’s acquisition of fame and honor through his great power. 

But every deed shall be praised according to the opinion of the judges; in my judgment, Busa made exceedingly good use of her own property. For nature did not draw and bring forth gold from the earth so that it should return back into it, as the greedy <term>original: "geytgen" (miserly or avaricious people)</term> do who bury it and keep it with such great care as if it were to be born again. Rather, gold was meant to be useful for the common good and to be seen in honorable use, helping us and our friends in the daily needs of living. 

And if there is anything left over, it should be shared with the lonely, the ruined, the poor, the countless oppressed, the prisoners, the sick, and all other needy people. This should be done generously from a free spirit, not for the sake of fame or worldly honor, but to provide help; not for gain, but out of goodness. And yet, this giving should be done with such discretion <note>The author cautions that charity should not lead to one's own ruin, a common theme in medieval and Renaissance moral philosophy.</note> that we do not fall into need ourselves through our giving, which would then require us to seek the help of others.

# Leaf 86

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts four figures. Above them are labels identifying Lelius, Masinissa, and Sophonisba. One figure is shown drinking from a cup, a reference to the poison Sophonisba would later consume to avoid being paraded in a Roman triumph.</note>

**S**ophonisba, although she was illustrious, being of the royal lineage of the Numidians <note>original: "numidarum"</note>, she has nonetheless been remembered far more brilliantly because of the harsh severity of her death, which she carried out herself. She was the daughter of Hasdrubal, whose father was called Gisco, the highest prince in Carthage. This was during the time when Hannibal harassed the Italian lands (then called *Welschland* <note>The term "wälsche lannd" was a historical German designation for Italy or other Romance-speaking territories.</note>). When she had grown into her blooming years, she was married by her father to the powerful King of the Numidians, Syphax. This occurred not only out of regard for the kinship of the royal lineage, but also so that her father might withdraw the king and his aid from the Romans, with whom the king had previously been allied. It was intended that through his daughter’s charms, she might make the king an enemy of the Romans, which indeed came to pass. For when the king had celebrated the wedding days, he was so fervently drawn and ensnared by the woman's lovely charms and great beauty into her love that he did not think to himself...

### Leaf 97

...that anything could be dear, pleasant, or joyful to him, except for his wife alone. But while the ill-fated king was thus burning with passion, it happened that the Roman general <term>Cornelius Scipio</term> <note>Scipio Africanus, the Roman general who would eventually defeat Hannibal at Zama.</note> intended to cross over from Sicily into Africa with his fleet <note>original: "hözē," literally "wooden things," referring here to his wooden ships.</note>. Sophonisba was warned of this by her father, and with flattering prayers and caresses, she moved the heart of her husband Syphax to her own will and desire. She persuaded him not only to abandon the Romans, to whom he was bound by oaths, and to pledge himself to the Carthaginians, but he even volunteered to become the commander of this foreign war. And so, in broken faith and violated vows—which he had recently promised to the same Scipio—he forbade him by letter from making the crossing into Africa.

But Scipio, the high-spirited youth, condemned the deceit of the unfaithful king and anchored his fleet not far from Carthage, intending first to drive out the faithless king. This indeed came to pass. Through King <term>Masinissa</term> <note>A Numidian prince and Roman ally.</note> and his companion Lelius—who had been sent with him by the Romans—the king's camps <note>The text likely refers to the "Battle of the Great Plains" where the Numidian/Carthaginian camps were famously burned.</note> were scattered, and he was captured, bound, and led before the city of his royal throne. There, weighed down with chains, he was displayed to his own citizens. By them, the city was opened and surrendered to the Romans.

But before Lelius arrived there amidst the sudden change and great turmoil, Masinissa entered the royal hall in full armor. He saw Queen Sophonisba coming toward him, contemplating her misfortune. And when she saw him, distinguished from the others by his noble form and well-adorned with weapons and armor, she immediately thought he was the king. She fell at his feet, considering how she might move him to mercy through gentle words, and spoke thus: "It is by God's will and your own good fortune..."

### Leaf 97

...look upon us, who but a short time before this were kings. You may now dispose of all things according to your will.

fortune than that with which <term>Syphax</term> <note>The King of Western Numidia and Sophonisba's husband, now defeated and captured by the Romans and Masinissa.</note> departed from it. I ask that you deal with me, an unfortunate woman, in a manner that your

<term>Hasdrubal</term> <note>Hasdrubal Gisco, a Carthaginian general; Sophonisba's marriage to Syphax was a political alliance intended to keep Numidia on Carthage's side.</note> of Carthage and the wife of Syphax, have a rightful dread of being their captive. And if all

Masinissa was also a Numidian, and like all others of that land, he was quick to physical desire. He was deeply 
moved by the lady’s great beauty. Furthermore, she had made her misfortune appear so lovely and charming 
that he received the prayers of the unhappy woman with even greater kindness and humanity. And before

and weeping of the other women surrounding her. Immediately, he betrothed her to himself as his lawful wife 
and celebrated the wedding feast amidst the din and tumult of weapons. By this act, he believed he had found 
a way to satisfy both his own physical desire and the desperate prayers of <term>Sophonisba</term> <note>A Carthaginian noblewoman whose life and forced suicide became a famous tragedy of the Second Punic War.</note>.

### Leaf 88

The next day thereafter, <term>Laelius</term> <note>Gaius Laelius, a Roman general and close friend of Scipio Africanus.</note> arrived; by his command, <term>Masinissa</term> <note>The King of Numidia and ally of Rome.</note> rode with him in all royal splendor and with others, bringing his new bride back into the camp. There they were first well received by <term>Scipio</term> <note>Publius Cornelius Scipio Africanus, the Roman commander in Africa.</note>, for they had successfully accomplished what had been commanded of them. Afterward, when Masinissa was rebuked with kind words because he had married a prisoner of the Romans for himself, he went away from them both into his tent, exceedingly sorrowful. And when he was sated with sighing and weeping—so much so that the neighbors could clearly hear his lamentation—he called for one of his servants whom he knew to be faithful and ordered him to prepare a strong poison, according to the misfortune allotted to <term>Sophonisba</term> <note>The Carthaginian noblewoman who preferred death to being paraded in a Roman triumph.</note>. 

And he commanded the servant to mix it into a drink, bring it to her, and say: that the loyalty and vow which he had willingly given her, he would gladly have kept if he could. But since his own will was broken by others, he would nonetheless grant her the safe-conduct she requested, so that she would not be led alive into the power of the Romans. And if she still desired this, he sent her this drink; yet she should be mindful of how she was the wedded wife of two kings and had been the queen of the land shortly before, and she should do as she deemed best. 

As soon as she heard this, she spoke with a courageous demeanor to the servant: "Thus I receive this <term>morning gift</term> <note>original: *morgengab*. A traditional gift given by a husband to his wife the morning after their wedding; Sophonisba uses the term with grim irony.</note>, and since nothing else could be given to me by my husband, I receive it with gratitude; yet tell him: dying would have been easier for me had I remained unchanged until the time I was to become a corpse." She gave no sharper words, but received the drink, and without any trembling, she drank it all up. She did not swell for long, but soon fell into the death she had desired. Truly, it would...

### Leaf 89

### ¶ Of Theoxena, daughter of Prince Herodicus. Chapter 70.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts several figures in a small boat on a choppy, turbulent sea. On the right side of the image, a figure identified by the label "theosena" is shown falling headfirst into the water, while the others in the boat look on in distress.</note>

**Theoxena** gave great evidence of her character, through her sweetness on one hand and through deadly severity on the other. She was the daughter of <term>Herodicus</term> <note>A prince of Thessaly whose family was systematically targeted by the Macedonian crown.</note>, the prince in **Thessaly**, during the time when <term>Philip</term> <note>Philip V (238–179 BCE), the King of Macedon.</note>, son of Demetrius, reigned in Macedonia. She had a full sister named **Archo**, born of the same father, who was also put to death through the malice of the same Philip. And thereafter, through his ever-growing betrayal, he had the husbands of both these sisters miserably stabbed to death...

<summary>The text transitions from the death of Sophonisba to the story of Theoxena, a Thessalian noblewoman whose father and brothers-in-law were murdered by King Philip V of Macedon.</summary>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Theoxena, following the introduction of her family and the murders of her father and brother-in-law by King Philip V. It details her selfless marriage to her sister’s widower to protect her nephews and the escalating tyranny of the Macedonian king.</meta>

### Leaf 89

...each left a son with his wife. When they were thus placed in the status of widows <note>original: "witwen stul," literally "widow’s chair," referring to the social and legal state of widowhood.</note>, **Archo** was first married to **Poris**, a prince of her people, and she bore him many sons.

But **Theoxena**, although she was entreatingly urged by many powerful lords, nevertheless maintained her status as a widow for a longer time with a strong spirit. However, when **Archo** passed away, **Theoxena** was moved by compassion toward her sister’s children and feared that they might fall under the power of another stepmother, or be raised by their father with less care. Therefore, so that those same children might be treated as her own, she took the same **Poris** as her lawful husband—for at that time there was no law against it <note>Referring to the practice of marrying a deceased sister's husband, which was not prohibited in this historical context.</note>.

And she began to raise them in the same way as if they had been born of her own body, so diligently that one could clearly recognize she had taken this man more to serve the children than for her own benefit.

At that same time, it happened that King **Philip**, through his restless mind and spirit, thought again of how to provoke war against the **Romans**, at a time when they were flourishing in fortunate prosperity. To protect himself against the Roman power, he commanded all his citizens to be driven out of his cities in **Thessaly**—which is **Macedonia**—into the land of **Emathia** <note>original: "emichiam." A region of ancient Macedonia.</note>, and he garrisoned his cities with people from **Thrace**, whom he considered more warlike and more faithful to him. 

Because of this, he suffered no small amount of resentment and words of reproach from those he had driven out. He then thought of the boys whose fathers he had killed, and believed his land could not remain in safety as long as they were not all put to death. He commanded that they be seized and cast into prison, not to be killed all at once, but rather over time...

### Leaf 100

[Those who] were stationed [by the King] saw the ship departing from the land. Suspecting their flight and intending to detain them, they set out with well-equipped and armed ships, following a command and order that they should not return without the fleeing vessel. 

But when **Poris** saw them coming toward him, he considered the future distress and the peril in which he stood. He urged the sailors to row with all their might, as fast as each man could; then he prayed to the gods to send him help in his time of need. 

When **Theoxena** saw **Poris** praying so fervently, she began to contemplate her own distress and anxious worry, realizing the time had come to carry out her deadly plan <note>original: "mortlichs fürnemen," referring to her earlier resolution to kill the children rather than let them be captured.</note>. Quickly, she mixed the deadly poison she had previously prepared with wine, and unsheathing the swords, she laid them before her own children and her sister’s children, speaking thus: 

"Dearest ones, we have neither comfort nor help, and no salvation is ordained for us. We cannot even avenge ourselves except through death. For this, you have these two ways: the sword and the drink <note>The "drink" refers to the poisoned wine she just prepared.</note>. Each may choose which he prefers, so that we may escape the haughty arrogance of the King. Therefore, dearest youths, show your noble spirit; manfully take up the weapons or drink the poison, while the raging sea still denies us our lives <note>original: "wütend mōz," likely a contraction for "Meer" (sea).</note>."

While she spoke these words, the King’s servants had drawn close to them. As the youths hesitated to carry out her command, she—the fierce instigator of such deadly evil—began to force and compel them. 

And when this was done, and the children now writhed in the throes of death from the drink and the wounds, **Theoxena** commanded that they be thrown out of the ship into the sea. She, who had raised her children so tenderly, preferred to have killed them rather than see them fall into the King’s possession <note>original: "eygenſchafft," meaning his ownership or bondage.</note>.

### Leaf 102

...to kill him and cut off his head, which was all fully accomplished according to her plan. At once she took the head in her lap and went away with good fortune, according to her timely deliberation. And when she had come home before the sight of her husband and told him of all the shame that had been inflicted upon her, she threw before his feet what she had brought in her lap and spoke: "Lord, look upon the reward for the insult, and the vindication of womanly shame and purity of mind."

Who would not praise her alone among other Roman women, and even place her on the level of **Lucretia**? <note>Lucretia was a legendary Roman heroine whose suicide after being raped by the son of the king led to the founding of the Roman Republic; she was the standard of female virtue in the Renaissance.</note> The prison still stood before her, and the chains; deadly weapons surrounded her. Yet she thought more of avenging the defilement <note>original: "vermeiligūg," meaning a staining or desecration of the body.</note> of her body than of her release from captivity. Although she feared she might be locked in chains again, thrown back into the dungeon as a prisoner, or even have her own head prepared for the sword, the woman nevertheless set her honorable, manly mind <note>In this period, "manly" (manlich) was a high compliment for a woman, signifying she possessed "masculine" virtues like courage, resolve, and strength of character.</note> with such great strength that she firmly commanded her servants to avenge the misdeed committed against her by the violator <note>original: "notzwinger," referring to the Roman centurion who assaulted her.</note>. 

Where could you ever find a more courageous person, a more manly prince, or a harsher judge over evildoers? She would rather have remained a prisoner forever or chosen death than return to her husband in unavenged shame. She also believed that she could not vindicate her defiled body except through great deeds and by demonstrating the purity of her mind; for through such actions, womanly discipline and honor are preserved, and that which was lost is restored. Thus, witness is given to a pure mind. In this, women who wish to strengthen the purity of their minds should look. For it is not enough to testify to the purity of the heart with tears, cries, and lamentations, or with words about the violence suffered...

### ¶ Concerning Tertia Aemilia, the wife of the first Scipio Africanus.
### The 74th Chapter.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts several figures. Above them are labels: **Emilia**, **Teras** <note>This likely refers to Tertia, though the spelling varies in early modern German</note>, and **Servant** (original: "seruo"). The scene likely illustrates the noblewoman within her domestic setting.</note>

Although **Tertia Aemilia** was highly ennobled by her birth from the lineage of the **Aemilii** <note>One of the most ancient and prestigious patrician families in Rome.</note> and also by her marriage to the most illustrious **Scipio Africanus the Elder** <note>The famous Roman general who defeated Hannibal at the Battle of Zama.</note>, she appeared far more distinguished because of her own great conduct. For although this same Scipio, when he was younger, had sent a captive maiden—who was in the flower of her youth and exceedingly beautiful—back home to her husband, the Prince **Luceius** <note>Likely a variation of Allucius, the Celtiberian prince mentioned in classical accounts of Scipio’s "continence" or self-restraint.</note>, releasing her from captivity with the wealth sent by her father; nevertheless, as he grew older, he could not withdraw himself from the damned impurity of carnal desire.

He fell in love and into an affair with his own maidservant. Since it is extremely difficult to hide such an outbreak of unseemly love within a marriage for long, it could not remain unknown to Tertia, who, through her modest character, eventually became aware of everything.

The 103rd Leaf

And who could doubt that she bore these things in her spirit with great difficulty? For some women truly say, without any shame, that nothing more disgraceful, nothing more unbearable, and nothing more painful can happen to a lawful wife than that the rights of her marriage bed should be granted to another woman. 

And by God, I can well believe it; for without doubt there was never a more suspicious creature than a woman. This arises either from the softness of her spirit or from the belief and worry that she might be held in lower esteem; for as soon as a man begins to flirt with another, they believe it all happens to the detriment of their friendship and love. 

But however heavy a burden this was, the noble woman bore it with such a strong spirit and kept it so secret and hidden that not only did no stranger learn of her husband’s guilt from her, but even her own husband could never notice from her that she had any suspicion that he had begun such things. 

For the wise woman <note>The original German "vernünfftig" implies she acted with reason and prudence, prioritizing social standing over personal grievance.</note> believed it would be quite unseemly for it to become public knowledge that such a man—who had made many kings and powerful lands and peoples subject to him—should be brought low by the love of a serving-maid. 

It did not seem enough to this virtuous woman to keep the ugliness of the situation hidden as long as her husband Scipio was alive. Instead, even after his death, she considered how the memory of such wrongdoing should be removed from such a high-ranking man and the sin entirely forgotten. 

And so that no reason should remain to remember such things, she felt it was unseemly that a woman <note>This refers to the maidservant Scipio had been involved with.</note> who had been shared by such a man should continue to live unfairly in bondage and servitude or be disgraced, nor any other further in unseemly...

The 103rd Leaf

[...desire mingled <note>The original German "ṽmüschet" (vermischet) suggests the girl's own youthful desires or potential for future scandals.</note>], by which she might be further weakened; therefore, she married that same companion of hers to a well-to-do man, by whom she was honorably provided for. O, the great desire and faithful love of this woman to protect her husband's honor! She released her own servant, who was her slave <term>göll: a term for a concubine or a female bondservant</term>, with a generous spirit and provided her with a husband. O holy lady, your praise is justly to be raised up even into the heavens! For you have patiently, with a gentle spirit, borne your husband’s guilt, and moreover, you released your slave from servitude. And just as such kindness is rare, so much more is it to be held as shining and great.

Another woman would have screamed, called all friends and neighbors together for counsel, and made an unseemly complaint: that she was abandoned, that she was despised, that she was brought to nothing by her husband, and held in no regard; instead, she would have claimed she had become a widow during her husband's lifetime and was ranked lower than a shameful bondservant and a vile harlot. She would also have treated the man poorly and accused him, not caring how she might have injured the praise and honor of such a great man, so long as she might recount her grievances with her gossip. But this Tertia overcame all adversity with her holy, strong spirit, through which she has earned eternal praise.

### Quinta Claudia

Claudia Quinta was a Roman woman. Although her ancestry is not fully clarified <note>original: "geleütert," meaning purified or clearly established</note>, she nevertheless achieved eternal renown through a particular bold act. At all times, she devoted great effort above all else to the special adornment of her body, so that she always appeared excessively costly and made-up <note>The original "aufgestrichen" suggests she wore heavy makeup or elaborate styling</note> compared to all others, even the most noble Roman women. Because of this, she was judged by the most honorable and eldest Romans not only as soft-hearted and unrefined, but she was even suspected of being shameful in her lack of purity.

And during the time when Marcus Cornelius and Publius Sempronius ruled in Rome—which was the fifteenth year of the second Roman war against Carthage <note>The Second Punic War, circa 204 BCE</note>—it happened that the Mother of the Gods <note>The goddess Cybele, also known as Magna Mater</note>, an idol of the Romans, was being brought to Rome upon the Tiber. To receive her, the man judged to be the "best," named Nasica <note>Publius Cornelius Scipio Nasica</note>, was sent by the senate of the entire city to the shore, along with all the noble Roman women. And when they had arrived, the ship containing the sacred relic <term>heyltumb: a holy object or relic, in this case, the black stone representing Cybele</term> came to a standstill; it was stuck so fast on the bottom that the...

<note>original: "ciiij blat," which is technically the 104th leaf, but continues the numbering from the previous page's translation.</note>

the sailors were in no way able to bring the ship to the shore. But when all the young men, using all their strength with ropes attached, could not move the ship, Claudia—who was among the other women and knew her own virtuous heart—fell down upon her knees. She humbly prayed to the goddess that if she recognized the purity of her heart, she should then allow the ship to follow her belt. And immediately she stood up boldly, in the hope of seeing what she had desired. She had her belt <note>The original "gürtel" refers to a lady's girdle or sash, often a symbol of chastity in this period.</note> tied to the ship and told all the young men to step away. She then pulled the ship entirely to where she wished—the same ship that no one before could move from its spot—to the immense astonishment of everyone.

From this great miracle it came to pass that this Claudia, who previously had been seen by everyone as frivolous and shameless, was now immediately regarded with great praise as the most worthy and honorable of all Roman women. And she, who had come to the shore in a shameful state of perceived soft-heartedness <note>The original "waichmütikeyt" suggests she was judged as being of weak character or morally loose due to her fine clothing.</note>, returned to her house adorned with purity and great honor.

But although this slander against Claudia ended according to her wish, no person, however innocent, should attempt such things again—trying to prove their innocence through things that are supernatural. For such acts are more a matter of tempting God than of washing away the stains of sin cast upon one. We should perform holy works; we should live holily; and if we are judged to be evil, God will not leave it unrewarded. He allows us to be tried with adversity so that He may test our patience, remove pride from us, and so that we may practice virtue and rejoice within ourselves when we know we are innocent.

of those things for which we are challenged. For it is enough for us—it is indeed quite a lot, even exceedingly great—that God is our witness that we live well and rightly. Therefore, if people do not hold us as such, we should not care so long as we live rightly. And it is better that we let them think ill of us than that we actually commit evil.

### Concerning Hypsicratea, Queen of the Great Sea
### The 75th Chapter.

**H**ypsicratea was the consort of the great Mithridates <note>Mithridates VI, King of Pontus.</note> and a queen of the sea <note>The "Great Sea" refers to the Pontus Euxinus, or the Black Sea region.</note>, exceedingly beautiful and possessed of an invincible love for her husband—a love so great that her name has been made eternal because of it. For her husband, Mithridates, was engaged in constant, great wars against the Romans; and although he had many wives according to pagan custom, she was ignited by the torch of love more fervently than all his other wives. She never wished to leave him, neither on the sea nor on any kind of perilous expedition, because she believed no one could care for him as well as she alone. She also knew that the service of servants was for the most part unfaithful.

And although accomplishing such things is rightfully difficult for a queen, she nevertheless set her mind firmly upon it. So that she might do this more appropriately, she had her golden-colored hair cut off, laid aside her rings, clasps, and other womanly ornaments, and covered her head with a helmet. She clothed her whole body in a knightly manner with a cuirass, chainmail, and leg armor <note>original: "peingewand," referring to greaves or plate armor for the legs</note> down to her feet. Thus she exchanged golden rings for the bow, precious brooches for shields, her head-veils <note>original: "kückel," a type of hood or woman’s head covering</note> for the lance, and soft cushions for the hard saddle. In an instant, a gentle, soft-living queen was seen transformed into a stern knightly figure. And

<summary>This page concludes the story of Claudia Quinta with a moral lesson on divine witness versus human judgment, then introduces Queen Hypsicratea, who disguised herself as a knight to follow her husband Mithridates into war.</summary>

->The 105th Leaf<-

she set out in diligent service with her husband, over rough mountains and valleys, always undeterred, to lie in caves and wildernesses—she who had previously dwelt in soft bridal chambers. She never allowed herself to be driven from him by either a decisive victory or a flight. She was also manly in witnessing and hearing all the works of the harsh struggle <note>original: "höre streit," referring to the army's combat.</note>, whether it were wounds, bloodshed, or killings; these she also bore unafraid. This was to such an extent that when her husband was defeated by Gnaeus Pompeius <note>Pompey the Great, the Roman general and rival of Julius Caesar.</note>, she never wished to leave him, but followed him with a few of her friends through Armenia and many regions and wildernesses, to comfort him and incline him toward good hope. O sweet holiness of this marriage! O bottomless power of true friendship! With what great strength you have fortified the mind of this woman. Without doubt, no woman has ever suffered so much for her husband. Therefore, it is not unjust that the ancients praised her works, so that those who follow after may marvel at them.

But for this exceedingly great service, she received an unseemly reward <note>original: "lon," meaning a wage or payment, here used ironically to mean "requital."</note>. For after he had come home again, in a fit of rage he had her son killed—the son she had conceived by him. Because of this, he was also hated by the Romans and driven to the main fortress of his kingdom. There, his son Pharnaces <note>Pharnaces II of Pontus, who eventually usurped his father's throne.</note> laid siege with the help of the Romans to drive him out because of his cruelty <note>original: "wütterey," referring to a murderous rage or tyrannical madness.</note> toward his children and friends. But when Mithridates noticed the destruction of his kingdom, as well as his own downfall and that of his legal wives, concubines <note>original: "zü weiber," meaning secondary wives or companions.</note>, and children, he had Hypsicratea—who had shown him so much love—killed with a poisoned drink, so that she would not outlive him. Without doubt, that was a great ingratitude on the part of Mithridates. Yet, he could not diminish the honor and glory of this woman, though he [destroyed] the mortal body with the poison prematurely <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at "dʒ", likely standing for "das".</note>

### ¶ Concerning Sempronia the Roman, the 76th Chapter.

**S**empronia was a beauty who far surpassed many other noble Roman women. And she was of such great wit and reason that her equal was not to be found. For she was so masterfully learned and practiced in the Greek and Latin tongues that she could compose and set skillful poems in both languages; and whatever she heard or saw, she grasped quickly. Moreover, she spoke so eloquently that, through her fine delivery, she could easily move anyone toward jest or earnestness, joy or sorrow. She was especially talented in matters of tenderness <note>original: "waichmütikeit," implying a soft-heartedness or susceptibility to emotion, often in a romantic context.</note> and carnal desire whenever she wished, to which she herself was particularly inclined. 

She could also sing laudably to all manner of stringed instruments, which well becomes a young woman when it is used rightly and decorously. But more commonly it is seen that such singing and string-playing become helpful tools for unchastity <note>The author here transitions from praising her intellectual talents to criticizing her moral character, a common theme in Renaissance biographies of "famous" women.</note>. And as soon as a stain has fallen therein, and maidenly purity is defiled—and the modesty <note>original: "töte des angesichtes," literally the 'stillness' of the face, referring to a bashful or reserved countenance.</note> of the face is cast aside—then one no longer needs to woo them; instead, they will run toward men, enticing them. 

Furthermore, this same Sempronia was so exceedingly greedy that, for the sake of winning gold, she made herself subject to the lust of everyone. On the other hand, she was so extravagant <note>The text uses "geÿtig" (greedy) but follows with "vnnüczlich... verzeret" (uselessly consumed), suggesting she was as quick to squander money as she was to crave it.</note> that she uselessly squandered in vanity and wickedness everything she had unchastely won. Thus, no measure was set for her greed or her spending. Now, the greed of women is a deadly evil and wickedness, and a public testimony of a defiled...

->Leaf 106<-

...[of their] mind; likewise, excessive generosity <note>original: "miltekeit," which usually means mercy or liberality, but here implies a wasteful extravagance.</note> is almost entirely destroyed. For as often as such wasteful generosity possesses a stingy heart—as all women are <note>A common misogynistic trope in medieval and Renaissance literature, suggesting women are naturally prone to extremes of either greed or wastefulness.</note>—contrary to nature, no hope of salvation can come except to fall into poverty. It is entirely over for honor and goods. For they cannot stop until they have come to the greatest shame and poverty. 

Women should be thrifty, and it is their duty to faithfully keep what was earned by the men. And just as much as excessive greed and wasteful generosity are blamed in women, so much is the diligence of women praised when they well preserve the goods earned by the man. For she increases wealth in a mannerly way; she honors the household goods healthfully; she is a true witness of a whole and faithful mind, also a consolation for labor and a powerful foundation for descendants. 

However, let all the history of this Sempronia be entwined in an evil way. At the time when the rebellious, malicious Catiline <note>Lucius Sergius Catilina (108–62 BCE), a Roman politician who led a famous conspiracy to overthrow the Republic.</note> along with many others planned destructive discord against the Romans, she involved herself among them to better carry out her unchastity. But as he with his followers—through the grace of God and the help of Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the famous Roman orator who exposed the conspiracy.</note>—was suppressed along with his henchmen, and several of them were killed, she was also struck down by a shameful death among them. 

There was another Sempronia, a daughter of Tiberius Sempronius Gracchus and Cornelia (the daughter of the great Scipio Africanus <note>The Roman general who defeated Hannibal.</note>), and a sister of Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus. She was of such a steady mind that she would rather die than allow herself to be forced by the judge to take a husband who was not her equal <note>original: "genoß," meaning a peer or person of the same social rank.</note>. Concerning this, I will leave off writing for the sake of brevity.

¶ Concerning the women of the Cimbri, who are the Kurwalhen <note>original: *kurwalhen*; a term used in early modern German to identify certain Germanic or Alpine tribes, sometimes associated with the inhabitants of the Chur region or interpreted here as the ancestors of the Flemish.</note>, or as some would have it, the Flemish. The 77th Chapter.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the aftermath of a battle in a rural landscape. Two women are shown hanging by nooses from trees, having committed suicide to avoid capture. One woman kneels over a fallen body while holding an infant, and another stands over a corpse. Several other bodies lie scattered on the ground, illustrating the desperate tragedy of the Cimbrian defeat.</note>

A story of such great, constant, praiseworthy, and faithful marriage, worthy of being extolled to the heavens, has never been heard as that of the great number of women who remained widows following the slaughter and flight of their husbands, the Cimbri <note>original: *Cimbrorum*</note>—whom we call Kurwalhen—which occurred at the hands of Gaius Marius <note>Gaius Marius (157–86 BCE), a Roman general and statesman famous for his military reforms and his victory over the invading Germanic tribes.</note> the Roman in a fierce battle. For as the number of women was so large, they are all the more worthy of being highly praised. While we may read of a few individual women who remained in the proper guard of their chastity, we have never, or only rarely, heard of such a great multitude gathered together for that purpose. 

At the time when the Romans were in the midst of their flourishing reign <note>original: *plüender regierung*; literally "blooming government," referring to the height of the Roman Republic's power.</note>, the Germans—who in Latin are called the Cimbri—and several other opposing regions rose up and swore an alliance against the Romans. This alliance was so strong and powerful that no one thought anyone would be able to withstand them. They also brought with them their wives and children and all their household goods on wagons, and suddenly overran...

[occupied] the whole of Italy with three armies. <note>original: *hōren*; an archaic form of *Heeren*, meaning armies.</note> To defeat them, Gaius Marius <note>A successful Roman general and seven-time consul (157–86 BCE) who saved Rome from Germanic invasions.</note> was sent out. At that same time, all the hope of the Romans was placed in him. First, the fierce leaders of the Germans came against them, against whom they fought for a long time in vain—now one side winning, now the other, as fortune shifted. And just as the Germans had fled before, he also overcame these in a double battle with excessive bloodshed and slaughter.

When the women noticed this, they did not follow their husbands in flight, but instead they formed a wagon-fort <note>original: *wagenburg*; a defensive circle of wagons used as a mobile fortification, common among migrating Germanic tribes.</note> from the great multitude of wagons in which they had carried their household goods, and they enclosed themselves within it. Thus they intended—though foolishly <note>The author likely views their resistance as "foolish" because of the overwhelming power of the Roman legions, despite the women's courage.</note>—to defend their freedom and chastity and to protect themselves from enslavement <note>original: *eigenſchafft*; here referring to the legal state of serfdom or slavery.</note> as long as it was possible for them. 

But as soon as the Roman army came before them in a broad formation, they began to feel that their defense was ineffective. Therefore, their desire was to reach a settlement with the commander <note>original: *keiſer*; while usually "emperor," in this early modern context it refers to the Roman general or leader.</note>. For they had unanimously set their minds upon this: since they had lost their husbands, their ancestral homes, and all their goods through the battle, they might still in some way keep their free life without enslavement and preserve the chastity of their bodies. For this reason, they requested with one mind: not to be reconciled with their fleeing husbands; not to return home to their houses; not to have their losses restored; but that they should all be placed in temples <note>original: *klōſter*; though meaning "cloister," the author uses it here to describe a Roman sacred sanctuary or house for chaste women.</note> among other chaste virgins. But when such requested prayers—which were truly evidence of a pure spirit—could not be granted, they became even more hardened in their purpose and preferred the following inhuman...

### The 108th Leaf

to fulfill this outcome; for they did not wish to live in shameful enslavement <note>original: *eigenschafft*; the legal status of a serf or slave.</note> with their children, amidst the mockery and impurity of their enemies. Other women might perhaps have come weeping and pleading for the release of their husbands, or for their property and freedom, and perhaps might have even achieved it. But then they would have been driven about like cattle, forgetting their womanly honor. These women, however, preferred with steadfast spirits to maintain their honor even unto death, so that through them the glory of their people would not be diminished.

# Of Julia, daughter of the Emperor Gaius. The 78th [Chapter].

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a woman on the left, identified as Julia, and several men on the right. One man, identified as Pompey, is kneeling and holding a blood-stained garment. This scene depicts the tragic moment Julia fainted—leading to a fatal miscarriage—after seeing her husband's clothes stained with blood from a sacrifice, mistakenly believing he had been assassinated.</note>

**A**lthough Julia, by her lineage and her husband, was one of the most illustrious women in the whole world, she became far more renowned through her most holy love for her husband and her sudden fate, shining out above all other women. She was a daughter <note>The text breaks off here; she was the daughter of Julius Caesar and the wife of Pompey the Great.</note>

<summary>The page concludes the tragic account of the Cimbrian women choosing suicide over Roman slavery and introduces Julia, the daughter of Julius Caesar, noted for her devotion to her husband Pompey.</summary>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Julia, daughter of Julius Caesar, picking up from the previous page's introduction of her noble lineage and her marriage to Pompey the Great.</meta>

->### Leaf 108<-

**S**he was the daughter of Gaius Julius the Emperor and Cornelia, whose father was Cinna <note>original: *prima*; likely a transcription error or variant for Cinna, as Julia’s mother was the daughter of Lucius Cornelius Cinna.</note>, who was four times the highest Roman official, called the consul. This same Julius had his ancestry from the most ancient Trojan, Aeneas, and his heirs, passed down through many kings and other rulers. But Julia was wedded to the Great Pompey of that time, the most illustrious Roman. His equal in the governance of the city and in victorious battles against many lands, kingdoms, and kings of the entire world—whom he powerfully appointed, deposed, or newly elected according to his will—had never existed before him.

Because of this, he was able to easily win and maintain the eternal favor of all Romans. The high-born lady loved this same Pompey so fervently—even though she was young and he was older—that she met an untimely death because of it. For at one time, as Pompey was offering a living sacrifice in the temple according to their custom, and as the animal was stabbed, it struggled so violently in his hands, thrashing to and fro from its wound, that it heavily bespattered his clothes and defiled them with blood. For this reason, he sent the clothes home to put on others; and by chance, the pregnant Julia encountered the servant who was carrying them. 

As soon as she caught sight of her husband's bloody clothes, before she could even ask what had happened, she fell into a terrible suspicion that some violence had befallen her husband. Immediately, her vision failed her, as if she could no longer bear to live after the death of her beloved husband. With clenched hands and a stifled heart, she suddenly fell down and gave up her spirit, to the great distress not only of the Romans, but of the whole world.

# ¶ Of Porcia, daughter of Cato of Utica
# The 79th Chapter.

<summary>This page recounts the tragic death of Julia, daughter of Julius Caesar, who died from the shock of seeing her husband Pompey's blood-stained clothing, mistakenly believing he had been killed.</summary>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the biography of Julia, daughter of Julius Caesar, to the life of Porcia, daughter of Cato the Younger. Both women are depicted as models of Roman wifely devotion during the civil wars of the first century BCE.</meta>

->### Leaf 109 [117]<-

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts several key figures from the end of the Roman Republic. The figures are labeled: **Porcia**, **Julius Caesar**, **Cassius**, **Brutus**, and **Porcia** again. The scene likely illustrates the interconnected lives and fates of these historical figures during the Roman Civil War.</note>

**Porcia** was the daughter of **Cato** <note>Marcus Porcius Cato Uticensis, a Roman statesman known for his stubborn integrity and Stoic philosophy.</note>, who was a partisan of **Pompey**. During the time when Cato led the remnants of Pompey’s army out of Egypt through the burning heat of the sun, across Libya and into Africa, he received word that **Julius Caesar** had attacked and defeated Pompey’s military forces. He was so unwilling to endure this victory <note>original: *vngedultig*; Cato refused to live under Caesar's tyranny or accept his clemency.</note> that he killed himself in the city of **Utica**, which is why he was called *Uticensis* <note>A title meaning "of Utica," marking the site of his death.</note>. 

This same highly noble woman never strayed from her father’s strength and sternness. To say nothing of her many other exceptionally clear virtues, she held her husband **Brutus**—to whom she was given by her father—in such great honor and love, so completely and with such purity, that her devotion to her husband far surpassed all other womanly industry and concern. Nor could she hide the fitting flames of love within her heart when she found a suitable time and place; she revealed her spirit through her actions.

<summary>This page introduces Porcia, the daughter of Cato the Younger, highlighting her noble lineage and her Stoic strength inherited from her father, who committed suicide at Utica. It focuses on her deep, virtuous devotion to her husband, Brutus.</summary>

<keywords>Porcia, Cato the Younger, Brutus, Julius Caesar, Pompey, Roman Republic, Utica, Stoicism, wifely devotion, Civil War</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Porcia, focusing on her involvement in the conspiracy against Julius Caesar and her demonstration of Stoic fortitude to her husband, Brutus.</meta>

->### Leaf 104<-

In the times when the deadly storms of war and the discord among the Roman citizens were stilled, and the followers of **Pompey** <note>The political and military rival of Julius Caesar.</note> were everywhere suppressed by **Emperor Julius**, the Emperor believed he would now remain in the steady and unchanging government of the Roman Empire. However, many of the most powerful Romans conspired against him. Among them was **Brutus**, the husband of **Porcia**. Brutus had no doubt regarding his wife's steadfastness; therefore, he revealed to her the deadly plot against **Emperor Julius**. This occurred on the morning of the day the Emperor was to be killed by the conspirators <note>original: *mit geschwornen*; literally "those sworn together."</note>.

As Brutus went out from his bedchamber, Porcia took a razor <note>original: *schermesser*</note> as if she intended to trim her fingernails and intentionally let it fall into her foot, whereby she was severely wounded. When her maid saw the blood, she began to scream. Because of this, Brutus returned to see what the screaming meant and began to scold Porcia for trying to do a <term>barber's<gloss>In the Roman era, barbers often performed minor surgical tasks and personal grooming that required sharp instruments.</gloss></term> work herself. 

But Porcia sent her servants away and spoke to him: "What has happened was not done by me without forethought, as you think. I have done it so that I might test how I could kill myself with a sword and endure death, should your undertaking against the Emperor come to a different end than you hope." 

For you have before you—O great strength! O inexhaustible love! O blessed man to have such a marriage! 

But what followed? The conspirators carried out the murder, yet they did not escape unpunished. For although it went according to their will, the murderers were nevertheless condemned by the remaining part of the Senate. Therefore, they were forced to become fugitives <note>original: *landreümig*; meaning they were banished or forced to flee the country.</note> and were scattered across many lands. But **Brutus** and **Cassius** traveled with a great multitude into the East, intending to stand against **Emperor Octavian** <note>The future Emperor Augustus, Caesar's heir.</note>.

<summary>Porcia proves her loyalty and Stoic resolve to her husband Brutus by self-inflicting a wound, preparing herself for the potential failure of the plot against Caesar. After the assassination, the conspirators are declared outlaws and Brutus flees to the East.</summary>
<keywords>Porcia, Brutus, Julius Caesar, conspiracy, Stoicism, Roman Senate, Octavian, Cassius, exile</keywords>

...and Antony, who were the heirs of Emperor Julius <note>referring to the Second Triumvirate, the political alliance between Octavian (the future Augustus) and Mark Antony</note>, set themselves against the conspirators. Octavian and Antony raised great armies and fought against them, overcoming them and destroying their entire host. When Porcia heard of this, she contemplated a death even more difficult to endure than the wound she had previously suffered from the falling razor. And she quickly returned to her former resolution; and as she sat by the fire and could not find any kind of weapon to kill herself as quickly as she desired, she took the burning coals in her hands without trembling and threw them into her throat. With these she so burned her gullet and throat that her heart failed from a lack of breath <note>Porcia’s death by swallowing hot coals after learning of Brutus's defeat at the Battle of Philippi is a famous, though debated, account of Stoic suicide in Roman history.</note>. Who could doubt that, as strange and unusual as this death was, her love for her husband is to be esteemed even more greatly, and is worthy of eternal memory.

<meta>This page continues the collection of biographies of famous women. Having concluded the tragic death of Porcia, the text moves to Leaf 110 to discuss the oratorical skill and political bravery of Hortensia during the Roman Civil Wars.</meta>

->### Leaf 110<-

**H**ortensia was a daughter of the eloquent Hortensius <note>Quintus Hortensius Hortalus (114–50 BCE), a celebrated orator and rival of Cicero</note>, who with worthy praise is rightfully to be highly exalted. For she had not only kept her father’s arts in her spirit, but she knew how to express them with a clear voice, so elegantly and masterfully according to the true art, and so lovely and agreeably, that it was often too difficult even for many of the most highly learned in that same art. It happened at the time when the Roman government was placed upon three men <note>The Second Triumvirate, consisting of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus</note> that, because of a necessity concerning the common good, a great sum of money was demanded from the noble Roman women. Through this they were so burdened that their clothes and jewelry <term>kleinat</term> <note>refers to precious ornaments, gems, or finery</note> would not have been sufficient to pay such a sum. They also could find no one who would or dared to speak a word against the order of the three men to mitigate it. 

Only Hortensia was so daring that she undertook to protect the women before the three men as an advocate with a strong spirit. And she spoke so well, so lovely, and so strikingly that everyone marveled at it, thinking her father Hortensius had risen again from the grave and been transformed into a woman. And this was not unsuitably felt; for just as her speech was in no part deficient, so her request was not diminished by the three men in any part. Instead, a much greater part of the tax was remitted, considering what overwhelming wisdom the woman possessed—which is to be praised secretly and in silence—and how elegant it is when it is brought to light. Because of this matter, Hortensia was rightfully celebrated and highly exalted with great praise and honor by all Romans. For the remaining money, of which there was little, they could then very easily pay.

**S**ulpicia, the noble Roman woman, is rightly placed among other illustrious women because of her great love for her husband. For at the time when the three men ruled in Rome <note>The Second Triumvirate: Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus, who seized power in 43 BCE</note>, her husband Trullio <note>original: "trustellio"; historically known as Publius Cornelius Lentulus Crusturnio or Sulpicius Rufus</note> was also proscribed and condemned by them. But through a quick flight, he escaped to Sicily; there he remained in misery and poverty. When Sulpicia became certain of this, her spirit told her that she wished to suffer toil and labor with her spouse. For it seemed quite unseemly to her to sit in great honor, to live in luxury, and to maintain a happy existence while her husband wasted away in misery and hunger. However, it was not easy for her to fulfill her intention, for she was guarded with the utmost diligence by her mother Julia so that she would not follow the misery of her husband. But there was never any guard so great that true love could not deceive it. Therefore, she waited for a favorable time for these things, and put on the clothes of a servant <note>Sulpicia famously disguised herself as a slave to escape her mother's house and the city guards</note>, and took with her no more than two maids and as many servants, and deceived the watchfulness of her mother and the other guards. And she left her father's house and all her possessions and followed after the misery of her spouse, even though, according to their established laws, she could have been divorced from that same man and held a wedding with a new husband and lived in high honor. But the illustrious lady preferred, out of special love for her husband, to despise the storms of the sea, to endure the heat of the burning sun, and not to be frightened by the wilderness of the desert, mountains, and valleys, traveling through many unknown regions and suffering thousandfold care and anxiety to find her husband, who had been cast out by fortune. For she did not wish to pass her time in her house in luxury, softness, and a gentle life. This, however, was not of a womanly spirit, but rather of a manly thor-

For this, they shall be illustriously praised and honored, for they overcome physical lusts, a soft life, and pride with steadfastness, honor, and purity; from this comes to them high fame and eternal glory.

### ¶ Of Mariamne. The 82nd Chapter.

->### Leaf 102<-

...to bodily desire, so that their prayers might be more readily granted. When Antony saw them <note>Referring to portraits of Mariamne and Aristobulus sent to him in Egypt</note>, he stood at first in long wonderment before them. Thereafter he said: "These are truly, for God’s sake, beautiful children; I have never and nowhere seen their like, not only in beauty but in such noble stature." But to speak of Mariamne alone: if she was radiant with unheard-of beauty, she appeared even more remarkable for the great strength of her spirit. For when she had reached marriageable years, she became the consort of that wretched man Herod <note>Herod the Great</note>, son of Antipater, King of the Jews. On her account, he was quite unfortunate, yet he loved her excessively for her delightful beauty. He was so consumed by her that he possessed this heavenly beauty only for himself, falling into such anxiety that no other should ever share in it. He intended, therefore, that she should not outlive him. 

To bring this to pass: when he was first summoned by Antony to come to Egypt to answer why he had killed Aristobulus, Mariamne’s brother; and again later, when he was summoned by Octavian <note>The future Emperor Augustus</note> after Antony had died, to answer for why he had sent aid to Antony against him—he left her in the care of his kinswoman Cypros <note>original: "Ciprinne"</note> and certain other friends of his. If they heard anything from Antony or Octavian, or however else it might happen, that his life was coming to an end, they were to kill Mariamne immediately and without delay, so that after his death she would belong to no one else. 

As Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus, the 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian whose *Antiquities of the Jews* is the primary source for this account</note> describes it fully: O, the mocking madness of a lustful King! Do you wish to be envious even after your death for the sake of a stranger's benefit—which perhaps might not even come to pass? And although this was done very secretly...

->### Leaf 112<-

Nevertheless, it was made known to Mariamne <note>Mariamne was the last princess of the Hasmonean dynasty; she learned of Herod's secret execution orders from the very men charged with guarding her.</note>. And when she had now conceived unspeakable hatred against Herod because of the murder committed against her brother <note>Aristobulus III, the High Priest, whom Herod had drowned in a swimming pool</note>, she thought well that her husband loved her for nothing other than to perform wanton works with her, and she was moved ever more to anger. For she considered how he had twice ordered her to be killed, whether he should perish at the hands of Antony or Octavian. She carried this so resentfully in her heart that she would never again be obedient to her loving husband in carnal works <note>original: "leiplichen wercken," referring to marital intimacy</note>, although she had borne him two noble youths, Alexander and Aristobulus. She began to despise him in many ways and to speak shamefully against him. Furthermore, she said openly and proudly before everyone that Herod was not worthy of his power; that he was a low-born man of no lineage; that he was no Jew but an Idumean <term>Idumean: A person from Edom; Herod's family had converted to Judaism, but the old Judean aristocracy viewed them as foreign usurpers</term> and unworthy of a royal wife. In addition, she called him a violent, faithless, murderous, and inhuman tyrant, accusing him in the harshest possible terms.

Although Herod bore this with great difficulty, his burning love forced him to endure these and other insults from her without punishing her. But at last, as these matters grew daily in malice, Herod's mother <note>Cypros</note> and his sister Salome—who were always at odds with Mariamne—sought to tame her. They bribed one of Herod's cupbearers to denounce Mariamne to him in this manner: that she had asked him to assist her in giving Herod certain potions she had prepared for him. And furthermore, they claimed she had recently sent her portrait out to the Roman leaders to move them to illicit love, by which she intended to drive him out. When Herod heard such things from the cupbearer...

was said, he was easily moved to believe it <note>Herod was influenced by the slanderous accusations brought by his own sister Salome and his mother, who hated Mariamne</note>. For her impetuousness in both words and deeds showed great displeasure toward him. He was highly provoked by this and ignited with such anxious rage that he accused her before all her friends. By the common counsel of those same friends, she was condemned to death as one who had grievously sinned against the royal majesty <term>royal majesty: A legal term for treason, suggesting Mariamne was accused of plotting against the king’s life or authority</term>. 

When she heard this, she so emboldened her noble spirit that she completely despised death. With an unchanged countenance, she looked upon all the others who were weeping with a man's courage and dry eyes. Not only did she not tremble, but she went eagerly, like one who had won the prize of a battle. Without any hope for her life, she stretched out her neck for the executioner <note>original: "richter," referring to the officer carrying out the sentence</note> to strike off her head. By this act, she has gathered many more years of memory <note>meaning her fame and legacy survived through history</note> than she might have earned if she had gained a longer life through a prayer to Herod. Furthermore, she left Herod with eternal vengeance; because of his jealousy, he was forced to live the rest of his days in mourning.

<meta>This page concludes the biography of Mariamne, the wife of Herod, and begins the account of Cleopatra VII of Egypt. ->### Leaf 113<-

[Large initial C] Cleopatra was an Egyptian woman, and whether she traced her origin through many kings back to Ptolemy—the son of Lagus and king of the Moors <note>original: "mohren künig," a medieval geographical descriptor often used for North African rulers, though Ptolemy I was actually Macedonian</note>—or, as others would have it, she was the daughter of King Ptolemy Dionysus or King Mineus, she nonetheless came to rule the kingdom through wicked malice. Furthermore, despite the beauty of her body, she possessed no true nobility or inner light <note>original: "durchleüchtigkeit," meaning brilliance, transparency of character, or the "shining" quality expected of royalty</note>. Instead, she was viewed shamelessly by the entire world for her greed, her unchaste works, and her murderous evils.

Her father, Dionysus or Mineus, was a supreme friend of the Romans and of Julius Caesar during the first year of his reign. When he was near death, he left a testament <note>a legal will</note> declaring that his eldest son, named Lisania <note>Ptolemy XIII</note>, should take his sister Cleopatra as his wife—she being the eldest daughter—just as they were permitted to do by their own laws. It was intended that they both should rule after his death. Such shameful decadence was common in Egypt that no blood kinship <term>blood kinship: Biological family ties; the text refers to the Ptolemaic practice of sibling marriage</term> would prevent a marriage, except for parents with their own children; and so, this was carried out. 

But in those days, as her burning desire to rule the kingdom alone began to grow, she fell into such murderous evil that she had that same youth—who was only fifteen years old, her brother and her husband—killed with a poisoned drink. She did this so that she might rule the kingdom alone. But shortly thereafter, it happened that Pompey the Great conquered nearly all of Asia and marched with his army into Egypt. There, he appointed the younger son <note>Ptolemy XIV</note> as king and ruler of the Egyptian realm. Cleopatra was so aggrieved by this that she prepared her weapons to fight against him again. In the midst of these matters, Julius the Emperor <note>Julius Caesar</note> marched...
