Thus, Cleopatra kept the kingdom with clever agility and gave herself over to such governance that she lived at all times according to her bodily desires. 

And she was so greedy for gold and jewels that, in order to obtain them, she was seen as a common woman by the kings of the East <note>The text suggests she prostituted herself or used her charms for financial and territorial gain</note>, through which she brought many hearts to poverty. And not only with those sins, but she also robbed the temples and took from them all ornaments of gold and precious stones, leaving them standing empty. 

After some time, when the Emperor Julius was killed, and Brutus and Cassius (who had killed him) were also defeated, Antony <note>Marc Antony, the Roman general and triumvir</note> moved into Syria. As soon as Cleopatra heard of this, she set out so joyfully and beautifully adorned to meet him, so that through her beauty and her seductive eyes—with a captivating manner—she might easily draw that impure man into her love. 

In this love, he was so passionately ignited that, for the sake of her requests—she having previously killed her brother and husband with poison <note>Ptolemy XIV, whom Cleopatra reportedly poisoned in 44 BCE</note>—he strangled her sister Arsinoe with his own hand in the Temple of Diana in the city of Ephesus, where the unfortunate woman had fled for safety. 

That was the first reward Cleopatra received from her new lover <note>original: "bülen," meaning a paramour or illicit lover</note> for their adultery: that she would have even less cause to fear interference in her rule. And when that wicked woman, Cleopatra, came to know Antony's character, she was not ashamed to demand from him the kingdoms of Syria and Arabia. But yet, when this seemed to him too much and an excessively great burden—that he should satisfy every desire of the woman he loved...

He satisfied her; in both realms <note>The Roman and Egyptian spheres of influence</note>, he gave her fine pieces of land, peoples, markets, and cities, and everything that lies between the great river Eleutheros <note>original: "elentheu"; the Eleutheros River in modern-day Lebanon/Syria</note> and Egypt at the end of Syria, except only for the kingdoms of Sidon and Tyre, which he kept for himself. When she had taken possession of all this and Antony wished to march into Armenia, she accompanied him in the manner of a lover, following him as far as the river Euphrates. 

And as she intended to travel back through Syria to Egypt, she was very well and honorably received by Herod <note>Herod the Great</note>, the son of Antipater, who was king of the Jews at that time. She was not ashamed to solicit him through go-betweens to have intimacy with her, seeking to try—should that happen—to strip him of his kingdom, which he had nevertheless earned through the help of Antony and had not possessed for long. 

But when Herod noticed this, he resisted such carnal advances, not only out of honor for Antony, but also so that he might remain free from the suspicion of such an impure woman. He would have had her killed if his advisors had not argued against it. And yet, in honor of Antony, he gifted her the revenues of Jericho <note>original: "jerethante"</note>, where the balsam <note>A highly prized aromatic resin used in medicine and perfume</note> grew; she took it with her to Babylon in Egypt <note>A Roman fortress and settlement near modern Cairo</note>, where it still grows to this day. 

Thus she traveled with these and other great gifts from Herod back into Egypt. Afterward, however, when Antony returned from the Armenians—whom he had put to flight—and summoned her to meet him (so that she might receive him with her embrace all the more desirously), he presented her with Artavasdes <note>Artavasdes II, King of Armenia</note>, the king of Armenia, along with his sons and all his advisors and all his treasure, all of whom he had captured through treachery and evil cunning. At this, the greedy woman was overjoyed and received her passionate lover so desirously, so tender-heartedly, and with such enticing flattery, that he [neglected] his wife Octavia, who...

->### Leaf 115<-

abandoned the sister of Emperor Octavian, wishing to separate from her because he loved Cleopatra so passionately <note>original: "jmerlichen," meaning intensely or miserably</note> and desired to take her as his wife. And on a time when they sat together at the table and lived in such luxury with excessive food and drink—so great that nothing for bodily pleasure or desire could be imagined that was not sought out and found—Antony said to her: "What could possibly equal our daily magnificence and costly hospitality?" He spoke as if no costlier food could be prepared than what was served to them daily.

That unchaste and impure woman <note>This moralizing language is typical of early modern biographies of Cleopatra, who was often portrayed as a temptress.</note> replied that she knew how to prepare a dish so that she alone might consume at a single supper a thousand times the value of his entire feast. Antony said that this could not be possible, yet he wished to see it and witness the spectacle. He appointed Lucius Plancus <note>original: "lucium plantum"; a Roman senator and diplomat who eventually defected from Antony to Octavian.</note> as a judge to decide the matter. 

And when the King's dishes the next day were not increased beyond the usual habit, Cleopatra laughed and commanded her servants to bring her dish. The servants brought nothing but a vessel of extra-sharp vinegar, as she had instructed them beforehand. Immediately, she took a pearl <note>original: "berlin," a variant of "perlin" or pearl.</note> which she wore in her ear as an ornament according to her custom—the cost of which was beyond telling—and placed it in the vinegar. As soon as it dissolved therein, she drank it up. 

Straightaway, she wished to have the other pearl, equally costly, from her other ear consumed in the same way. However, Lucius Plancus intervened by giving the judgment that Antony was defeated. Thus, the second pearl was saved, and the Queen won the wager. That same pearl was later divided into two and taken to Rome, placed in the high temple of the Pantheon <note>The famous Roman temple "of all the gods," where the pearls were said to have been placed on the ears of the statue of Venus.</note>.

It was done as lightly and thoughtlessly as if it were a shepherd's hut.

Those survivors sought to win Octavian's favor, but that could not be. Therefore, Antony became so desperate that he went into his private chamber and killed himself with a sword. 

When Cleopatra realized she was defeated, she thought, according to her old habit, to move Octavian to impurity <note>Meaning she intended to seduce him.</note> by using flattering charms and being adorned most beautifully, just as she had previously brought the Emperor Julius <note>Julius Caesar</note> and Antony to her side. But Octavian sensed her impure intent; because of this, she became so despairing that she went to her Antony in her costly garments and lay down beside his dead corpse. She had the veins of her arms opened and placed poisonous asps <note>original: "ÿpnales" — likely referring to the Egyptian asp or "hypnale," a snake whose venom was thought to cause a fatal sleep.</note> upon the wounds to bite her; thus she ended her life. ### Of Antonia, the daughter of Antony — Chapter 84

**A**ntonia the Younger—so named because she had an older sister who was also called by that name—was a graceful example to all women of future generations who wish to maintain their state of widowhood in pure chastity. She was the daughter of the aforementioned Marc Antony, the triumvir <note>original: "drey manes" — literally "of the three men," referring to the Second Triumvirate, the political alliance of Octavian, Antony, and Lepidus.</note>, yet she was born of Octavia, the sister of Octavian. And she was given as a wife...

# The 117th Leaf

...in great beauty and blooming age, she was placed in the state of widowhood. She believed it was enough for an honorable woman to have entered the married state once. And she could be moved by no one to marry again; instead, she lived all her days in her mother-in-law’s house, in her husband’s bedroom, so chaste, so pure, and so holy that her state of widowhood surpassed the lives of all other widows who have ever been written about. And she is all the more to be praised because she remained steadfast despite the most impure lineage and the uncleanness <note>original: "vnreynigkeit" — a reference to the moral scandals and profligacy traditionally associated with her father, Marc Antony.</note> of her father, Marc Antony.

### Of Agrippina, the wife of Germanicus — Chapter 85

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts three figures. On the left, a man in robes and a crown-like hat holding a staff is labeled vertically as the Emperor "Tiberius." In the center, a woman labeled "Agrippina" is being restrained by two men, representing her arrest and eventual exile under the Emperor's orders.</note>

**A**grippina was a daughter of Marcus Agrippa, born of Julia, the daughter of Emperor Octavian <note>Known to history as Augustus.</note>. However, Gaius Caligula—the son of this same Agrippina—at the time when he became Emperor, wished to be ashamed that he should be born from the common <note>original: "gebauren" — literally "peasant" or "low-born." Marcus Agrippa was of humble origins before rising to become Augustus's right-hand man.</note> lineage of Agrippa. He therefore invented a dishonorable birth for his mother, claiming she was born of Julia but not of Agrippa, but rather of Octavian himself <note>Caligula reportedly spread the rumor that his mother was the product of incest between Augustus and his daughter Julia, preferring a "divine" (if scandalous) lineage over a plebeian one.</note>.

<summary>Antonia the Younger is praised as a paragon of widowhood for her refusal to remarry; the text then introduces Agrippina the Elder and her son Caligula’s attempts to distance himself from his grandfather Agrippa’s common origins.</summary>

# The 118th Leaf

...conceived sinfully by her father. The fool <note>original: "d' thor" — referring to her son, Gaius Caligula.</note> believed himself to be more noble if his mother had been born through such a shameful act of a father with his daughter <note>Caligula preferred the scandal of incestuous imperial blood over the "common" blood of his actual grandfather, Marcus Agrippa.</note>, rather than being sired by a low-born man in the holy state of matrimony according to the law. But I shall let be the question of whose daughter she truly was.

She was betrothed to a brave and noble youth of her own age who always promoted the common Roman good, named Germanicus, whom the Emperor Tiberius had taken as his own son. Through this, she was seen as sufficiently illustrious by everyone; however, she was placed in eternal memory far more through her own deeds, with which she utterly thwarted that hostile intent. For after she had borne three sons to Germanicus—one of whom was Gaius Caligula, who was later chosen as Roman Emperor—and as many daughters, of whom one was named Agrippina, [wife] of the Em[peror]... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the "Agrippina" mentioned here is Agrippina the Younger, mother of Nero.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion of Caligula's false claims about Agrippina the Elder's ancestry and details her marriage to the celebrated Roman general Germanicus. # The 118th Leaf

Emperor, and she firmly set her mind to end the distress brought upon her honor through her own death, escaping the tyrant <note>original: "wüttrichs" — a strong term for a bloodthirsty oppressor or tyrant, referring here to Emperor Tiberius.</note>. Since she could not find a more suitable way to bring this about, she wished to do so through hunger; immediately, she began to fast from all food. When Tiberius was told of this and perceived that she did it so that she might withdraw herself from his long-lasting cruelty, he began to threaten her, and even to force her with blows to consume food. He did this so that he might preserve her life for further torment, but it had no effect on her; she remained steadfast. Tiberius was so driven by this that he ordered food to be forced down her throat.

Yet the more she was pressured and coerced, the less she would abandon her resolve, until she finally overcame the injustice of the evil prince through her death. She proved that while he could easily kill many people whenever he wished, he could not, with all the power of his rule, keep a person alive who was determined to die. Through this death, though Agrippina earned much praise, honor, and glory among her kin, she left behind even more dishonor and infamy for Tiberius.

# The 119th Leaf

### Of Paulina the Roman, Chapter 86

**P**aulina was a Roman woman, and through her simple and remarkable devotion <note>original: "einfeltigē spötlichen andacht" — This suggests a piety that was perhaps naive or singular enough to be noteworthy.</note>, she established an eternal memory for her name. This occurred during the time when the Emperor Tiberius reigned in Rome. In the same way that this Paulina surpassed other young women in the beauty of her body and her good manners, she also became a special ornament of womanly honor once she was given in marriage to Saturninus <note>original: "saturnio" — referring to the Roman aristocrat Visellius Saturninus</note>. She also had no greater concern than how she might please her husband in all things. To accomplish this, she desired the help of the idol Anubis <note>The jackal-headed Egyptian god of the dead, whose cult had become popular among the Roman aristocracy.</note>, whom she honorably praised every day, just as all the Egyptians did. She did this because he was believed to have the power to create goodwill and unity between married people.

But, as it is commonly the case everywhere that the most beautiful women are most loved by young men—especially by those who are particularly diligent regarding finery, good behavior, discipline, and honor—so it was with Paulina. Because of her beauty, a young man named Mundus <note>Decimus Mundus, a member of the equestrian order.</note> was so fervently captivated by her that he could not contain himself. He showed his desire first with his eyes, then with gestures, then with flattering words, then with gifts, then with pleas and many kinds of offers, to see if he might in any way achieve what he so passionately desired. But all of this was in vain, for this most chaste woman, who had set all her love upon her husband, blew all other flattering words into the air. 

When Mundus realized that the lady's constancy could be weakened neither by words nor by gifts, he began to set his mind upon wicked cunning and trickery. And when he noticed how this lady went every day into the temple to join the idol Anubis with her constant offerings, the [opportunity] became...

...fervent love <note>original: "inbrünstig liebe"</note>, he devised an unheard-of trick to gain access to her. He thought that no one could be more helpful to him in this matter than the priests of the same god <note>referring to Anubis, whose cult Paulina followed</note>. These priests he brought over to his way of thinking with exceedingly large gifts. Through his instruction, it was arranged that the most honorable and worthy priest—when Paulina, according to her custom, came again into the temple—went to her and spoke to her gently. 

He told her that the god Anubis had come to him the previous night and commanded him to tell her what great pleasure he took in her devout prayer, and that he desired to speak with her himself during the time of rest <note>i.e., during the night</note>. When Paulina heard this, her spirit was lifted up in great glory, thinking that the god desired her because of her holy constancy <note>original: "heylige stätigkeit" — referring to her unwavering virtue and marital fidelity</note>. She believed these words as firmly as if the god himself had spoken with her. 

She then told all these things to her husband. He was trusting <note>original: "torot" — likely a variation of "töricht" (foolish/naive) or "getrost" (trusting/confident) in this context</note> toward his wife and granted her permission to spend the night in the temple. For the same Claudius was the brother of her father Germanicus. To make such a thing acceptable <note>original: "zeuerlimpffen" — to gloss over, justify, or make a scandalous act seem favorable.</note>, Vitellius—who was a friend to Agrippina and her son—brought it about that the highest Roman authority, called the Senate, was begging and pleading with Claudius to take Agrippina. And a general decree was issued that it would be permissible for any man to take his niece <note>original: "nisstel"</note> as a wife.

He gave him medicines so that the foul poison might leave him from below through the purging of the bowels and from above through vomiting. In addition, he had feathers dipped in oil thrust down his throat <note>A common medical practice in antiquity to induce vomiting.</note>. 

But when Agrippina realized that Claudius might recover through the medicine and that her evil plan might be thwarted, she smeared the feathers with an even more lethal poison and thrust them into his throat, pretending to help him. And she continued this in his bedchamber until her wicked will was fulfilled upon him. 

Once he had died, she kept the death secret for a long time and hidden from everyone except herself and her accomplices, until—through the efforts of her friends—Nero was elected Emperor and Britannicus <note>Britannicus was the biological son of Claudius and his previous wife, Messalina. Nero was so greatly pleased that he granted his mother—as one who had well deserved it—authority over all matters of government, both private and public. This went so far that while he held only the title, she was the one possessing the actual order and rule. Thus, all Roman power was manifest in a single woman. Yet this great splendor was stained by a shameful mark. For she raged for a long time with killings and the condemnation of citizens into exile. 

Furthermore, everyone believed she held power from her son not because of the natural affection a child should have for a mother, but because of a wicked love and unnatural, shameful acts that he was accustomed to commit with her <note>Ancient historians like Tacitus and Suetonius recorded rumors of an incestuous relationship between Nero and Agrippina, though modern scholars often view these as political character assassination.</note>.

...she met her well-deserved death. For she became burdensome and contrary to her son Nero in many matters, which caused her to fall into such great hatred from him that he completely stripped her of all honors and imperial majesty. 

At this, she became resentful and often said, out of feminine agitation <note>The original German "weiplichez reytzung" implies a mixture of spiteful provocation and emotional stirring.</note>, that it was she who had elevated her son. She claimed that with her cunning she could just as easily ensure he was cast down. Nero became terrified by this, for he knew her to be malicious. Furthermore, she held much favor with the people because of the memory of her father, Germanicus <note>Germanicus was a beloved Roman general; his enduring popularity made Nero fear that a public execution of Agrippina would cause a revolt.</note>, so that he did not wish to kill her publicly. 

However, on three separate occasions, he had poison served to her, which nevertheless brought her no harm; for through her foresight, she neutralized the malice of the poisons with corresponding medicines <note>historical accounts suggest Agrippina regularly took "mithridates," or universal antidotes, to build immunity to common toxins.</note> so that she was not injured. 

Finally, when Nero noticed that she could not be caught in any of the many snares he had laid for her, he thought it necessary to seek a more cunning way. He summoned Anicetus, the chief commander <term>patron: here meaning an admiral or overseer</term> over the entire fleet, who was privy to many of the king’s secrets because he had raised Nero from childhood. This man told him how a collapsible ship could be built that would be fatal to Agrippina and yet appear unsuspicious. 

This advice pleased Nero. And once, when she had come from the city of Antium <note>original: "ancio"; a coastal resort city where Nero was born.</note>, he feigned total friendship toward her and received her beautifully, embracing her in his arms in every manner, as if he had set aside all envy and ill-will toward her. He escorted her all the way to her house. 

Afterward, when the ship had been prepared for her death, she set out one evening with several of her servants toward a city located not far away. And as they reached the open sea, the watchmen gave the signal for the roof of the ship—in which much lead was hidden—to fall. It collapsed and struck down several of her servants, but Agrippina...

[He] <note>Referring to Volusius Proculus, the naval commander mentioned on the previous page.</note> wanted to try whether he might earn the emperor’s favor through his services; and as soon as he came to him, he revealed to him everything that Epicharis <note>original: "epitharis"</note> had told him. However, he could not explain the matter sufficiently to him, because the woman was yet so clever that she would not identify any of the conspirators <note>original: "sacher"</note> by name. She was captured and questioned as to who had sworn to bring about the death of the emperor, but she could not be compelled to point out any of them. At last, while she lay imprisoned, it happened that the matters were revealed by the plotters <note>original: "sehern," likely a variant or error for "sacher" (doers/perpetrators).</note> themselves. And now that the emperor knew their names, he had the woman brought to torture again to see if she would speak as the others had. 

But however much she was tormented, even beyond ordinary torture, they could never soften her firm spirit so that she would ever name any of the conspirators; the secret had to remain locked away secretly in her heart. She was then locked up to be interrogated again on the following day. And as she had now been tortured so that she could no longer walk upon her feet and her body had become weak, she feared the future torture—that her spirit might follow the frailty <note>original: "blödikeit"; in this period, the word meant physical or mental weakness/fragility rather than modern "stupidity."</note> of the body and she would speak. She untied the bandages with which she was girded, and made from them a cord, and hanged herself from the chair upon which she sat imprisoned, so that she might not reveal her secret. 

This acted contrary to the common proverb by which we are taught that no secret should be hidden with women <note>The text refers to the misogynistic trope of the era that women are incapable of keeping secrets.</note>; for what they do not know, that is kept secret by them <note>A satirical historical remark: the author implies the only thing a woman can keep secret is something she was never told.</note>. Thus the powerful emperor could not overcome the woman. And although the steadfastness of this woman is to be esteemed as great and high, she is also rightly exalted and praised above the spirit of all men when one considers that this conspiracy against Nero...

[It] was revealed by those themselves who had sworn together; and a woman kept the secret hidden until death. Where would there even be a man who, to save his own life, could suffer and endure as much torture as this woman suffered to keep other people alive? Therefore, she should rightly be placed in eternal memory.

### ¶ About Pompeia Paulina, wife of Seneca. Chapter 89.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts the Roman philosopher Seneca and his wife, Pompeia Paulina, sitting in separate wooden tubs. Their veins have been opened, a traditional Roman method of suicide, and their blood flows into the water. An attendant stands behind the woman to assist. Labels above the figures identify them as "Paulina," "Pompeia," and "Seneca."</note>

**Pompeia Paulina** was the wife of Lucius Annaeus Seneca, who was the tutor <note>original: "zuchtmeÿster"; Seneca was the famous Stoic philosopher who served as an advisor and tutor to the young Emperor Nero.</note> of Nero. Although we do not know her origin or the lineage of her ancestors, her kind love for her husband proves that she was of a highly noble and enlightened spirit. This same Seneca—as the greatest wise men have said—was killed by the Emperor Nero through his cruelty <note>original: "wütterey"; implying a tyrannical or bloodthirsty rage.</note>, not because he had deserved it in any way, but because he [Nero] suspected him of having been aware of the previously described conspiracy <note>original: "mit schwerūg," literally "with swearing," referring to the sworn oath of the Pisonian conspirators</note> aimed at his death. And although that was not the case...

<meta>This page continues the account of Seneca’s death and the devotion of his wife, Pompeia Paulina, following the introduction to her character on the previous page.</meta>

->The 125th leaf<-

...yet Nero rejoiced that he had found a reason to act against this most virtuous and honorable man and to put him to death. For he harbored an old hatred toward him on account of his wisdom and teaching, his discipline, honor, and virtue, to which he [Nero] was an enemy by his own innate malice. Therefore, he commanded Seneca to choose for himself in what manner he wished to die. 

As soon as **Pompeia** noticed that he was preparing to carry out the emperor’s command, she set aside all sweet words and comforts regarding any hope for his life. Through the compulsion of her fervent love for her husband, she steeled her spirit to drive away her great sorrow and distress by sharing the same death, wishing to die as willingly with her husband as she had lived in harmony with him. 

And in the same manner as her husband, she remained undaunted; with a strong spirit, she had her veins opened in lukewarm water, letting the blood flow out until death. But when Nero heard of this, he immediately commanded that she should be saved, for he bore no particular ill-will toward her. He also did this so that his cruelty <note>original: "wütterey"; implying a tyrannical or bloodthirsty rage.</note> might be less widely publicized. 

However, the servants could not arrive quickly enough to restore her blood. She had bled so much that her lasting paleness gave eternal witness that she had shed much blood and the spirit of life along with her husband. Afterward, she mourned her husband with honorable remembrance, remaining for her few remaining years in the pure state of widowhood <note>original: "reÿné witwen stül"; literally "pure widow’s chair," referring to the social station or "seat" of a widow who does not remarry.</note> until she ended her days. 

O God, what is more noble or greater than the sweet love of a holy marriage? This woman followed the ancient, holy custom of married women who shunned not only a third, fourth, or perhaps even a sixth marriage, but even a second one. For after the death of their first husband, they were quick to embrace holy chastity and a worthy, laudable, eternal memory. Not as many...

### Concerning Sabina Poppaea, the 90th Chapter

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a man in a violent act, kicking a pregnant woman. The woman is labeled "Sabina," and the man performing the kick is labeled "nero" in a small box at the bottom left. To the left stands another male figure labeled "popea," likely representing her grandfather or perhaps her former husband. Another couple stands to the right, observing the scene.</note>

**Sabina Poppaea** was one of the most illustrious Roman women, named after her maternal grandfather, Sabinus Poppaeus. She would have lacked nothing in all feminine gifts, had she been of a more noble spirit; for she was of an unseen beauty, surpassing all other women, with soft words, a sweet voice, flattering speech, and lovely gestures. She was endowed with exceptionally sharp and agile senses, had she only used them well. Her constant habit was to behave modestly in public, while secretly practicing unchastity <note>original: "vnkewscheyt"; referring to sexual impropriety or infidelity.</note>, as unfortunately many do who nonetheless wish to be highly honored. And although she rarely went out, she nevertheless used her enticing cunning; for when she noticed that many men, and especially the highest authorities...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Sabina Poppaea. Note that while the previous page was identified as the 135th leaf, this OCR identifies itself as the 126th leaf (cxxvi); such discrepancies are common in early printed editions due to pagination errors.</meta>

->The 126th leaf<-

...were moved to carnal desire by her beauty, she always covered the greater part of her face. This was not because she did not wish to be seen, but rather so that they would not be satisfied by seeing her beauty, and henceforth would be all the more enticed to see her completely. To omit many of her other habits: she reached a point where she cared little for her honor, and wherever the greatest profit <note>original: "nucz"; meaning advantage or utility</note> was to be found, there she inclined her unchaste mind. In these matters, this notorious woman had such a great following that in a short time she acquired such great wealth that the status of her birth could be well maintained, just as her ancestors had done.

And she was first wedded to a knightly young man named Rufus Crispus. After she had borne him a son, she was moved to love a wealthy and powerful young man named Otho, who was a favorite of the Emperor Nero. And shortly thereafter, when she had known him illicitly <note>original: "vneclich"; literally "un-weddedly," referring to their adulterous affair</note> many times, Rufus died; then Otho took her as his wife. 

Not long after, Nero was enticed to love Sabina through the words of the same Otho. Whether this happened through Otho’s own careless words—out of a fervent love for his wife—or because he could not bear her soft-minded, unchaste habits and wished to leave her, or whether this was simply Poppaea's destiny <note>original: "gelück"; fortune or luck</note>, I do not know. For at one time, when the emperor’s highest councilors rose from the table and wished to deliberate on something, Otho said to the emperor: "I am going back home to her who is gifted by the gods above all other women. With such great nobility, praised habits, and divine beauty; in these three things alone resides all desire, all joy, and all the pleasures of the human race." Through such words, Nero’s unchastity was easily enticed into a desire for such a gifted woman.

...and immediately, messengers were sent from him to her and her intermediaries <note>original: "vnder kössel"; likely a corruption of "under-gesellen," meaning associates or sub-agents who facilitated the meeting</note>, through whom she was led to Nero not long after with eager desire. ...moreover, there was no resistance. She began to lay manifold snares and set nets in which she might catch Nero, so that he would take her as his wife. And at the time when Memmius Regulus and Virginius Rufus were the chief counselors <note>These men were the Roman consuls in 63 AD, serving as the official time-markers for the year.</note>, she bore him a daughter. He received the child with exceedingly great joy and named her Augusta Poppaea <note>The title 'Augusta' was a high honor usually reserved for the Emperor's wife; bestowing it upon a newborn was a sign of Nero's obsession.</note> after her mother. Then Poppaea first began to speak boldly with him, saying: "I reflect that I have been with you for two nights, during which I was waiting for the status of a spouse. I am not of low birth, I am fertile, and I am also gifted with beauty; I believe I am well worthy of an imperial marriage." With these words, she moved the Emperor so that he eagerly began to think of ways to seek how he might properly marry her. And first, he had his wife Octavia—who was the daughter of the Emperor Claudius—sent away to an island, though she was innocent of any fault. And in the twentieth year of her age, he had her put to death through the instigation of Poppaea <note>Octavia was first exiled to Pandateria and then executed, her head famously being sent back to Poppaea.</note>. After this, he took Poppaea as his wife. But she did not long enjoy this high status, which she had nevertheless pursued with great effort and many evil deceits. For shortly thereafter, when she was again pregnant, it happened that Nero, moved by anger, kicked her in the stomach, from which she met her final day. Then Nero did not want her to be burned—as was the custom in Rome—but according to the custom of other kings, he had her carried to the grave with a great following of the people, expensively embalmed, and buried therein according to imperial dignity <note>Tacitus notes that Poppaea was not cremated in the Roman fashion but stuffed with spices and embalmed after the manner of foreign monarchs.</note>. There Nero praised her with a long speech, and most of all for her beauty and her enticing practice of unchastity <note>original: "vnkeüsch"; the author here moralizes, suggesting Nero perversely praised her seductive charms as if they were virtues.</note>, in which she was well-skilled. This he valued as a great brilliance of virtue, though such things are a poisonous injury to a man's mind; she had no true goodness within her.

### Of Triaria, the spouse of Lucius Vitellius, the 91st chapter

Triaria was the wife of the brother of the Roman prince Aulus Vitellius <note>Her husband was Lucius Vitellius the Younger; her brother-in-law Aulus was briefly Emperor in 69 AD.</note>. She is widely celebrated solely for her exceptional love and manly fighting for and on behalf of her husband. For at the time when Vitellius the Emperor and Vespasian strove against each other for the rule of the empire, and a great multitude from both parties had come together in the city of Terracina <note>Original: "Taracinam", a coastal city in Italy.</note>, and were now arrayed with weapons of war, Triaria armed herself so boldly, so chivalrously, so manfully, and so defiantly to protect her husband and ensure his victory, that by her hand, more than by others, much blood was shed. For this reason, her memory is highly praised and established for eternity; and since nothing more is written of her other deeds, I have also shortened this account of her unwomanly fighting <note>The author reflects a common medieval and Renaissance sentiment that women engaging in combat was "unwomanly," even if praised for its loyalty.</note>.

### Of Proba, the spouse of Alypius or Adelphius, the 92nd chapter

Proba was "Proba" by name and by deed; for "proba" means "upright" or "pious" in German <note>The author is translating the Latin name *Proba* (meaning honest/virtuous) for the reader's benefit.</note>. Thus, this lady was upright in all her works. Above all others, she is most worthy of eternal memory because of her great skill. From whom she learned her craft, I do not know, but she was a great mistress of the seven liberal arts <note>The medieval curriculum: grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy.</note>, as is clearly evident from her poems. Furthermore, she was so thoroughly and completely learned in the books of Virgil that they could never leave her memory, as her book called *Centona* <note>Referring to the *Cento Vergilianus de laudibus Christi*, a poem composed entirely of fragments from Virgil’s works rearranged to tell the story of the Bible.</note> clearly proves. In this work, she describes the Old and New Testaments <note>Original: "ee", meaning "law" or "covenant."</note> so purely, so lovely, so sweetly, and so gracefully that it would be a wonder for a woman even to conceive of such a thing, let alone to complete it. And so she interprets from the creation of the world and the first man, until the holy...

[...Pentecost, and] the sending of the Holy Spirit to his disciples. And this was done solely from the verses of Master Virgil <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, the Roman epic poet of the *Aeneid*.</note>, taking some whole, some half, sometimes the beginning, the middle, or perhaps the end, according to how they served her purpose. And she did this so skillfully that anyone ignorant of these matters might easily mistake her for a prophetess or an evangelist, rather than the poet Virgil, from whose works she gathered this book. She also researched other books of the Holy Scripture so thoroughly—surpassing many of the most highly learned doctors of the law—that it is right to mourn how manhood is so negligent in learning that it should be surpassed by a woman. 

And the more I read over this little book of hers, called the *Centona* <note>A "Cento" (from the Greek word for patchwork) is a poem made of verses from other poems; Proba's *Cento Vergilianus* is her most famous work.</note>, the more I find that she and her book are worthy of eternal memory. I also cannot help but think that, since she lived during a time when she could have written many other books, perhaps they have—to our disadvantage—been despised and lost through lack of preservation. 

Yet some claim she used the same skill to create a *Homerocento* <note>Original: "homericentonam", a poem composed of fragments from Homer's *Iliad* or *Odyssey*.</note> from the books of Homer, just as she made the *Centona* from Virgil. If this is true, her praise should rightly be expanded, for it would be certain evidence that she was as learned in the Greek arts as she was in the Latin. It is a wonderful thing to say that a woman should possess such high intellect that she could create such unique books on the life of Christ out of all the Christian books, so orderly, so elegantly, and without any error in the poetic meter, syllables, or words. It is even greater and more to be esteemed that she took these from pagan poems. 

Oh, how diligently she cast aside all the sluggishness of her spirit so that she might find the eternal light! Oh, that the women of our time—who are servants to all manner of evil pleasures—would only look upon this example!

### Of Faustina, the wife of Marcus Antoninus the Pious· Chapter 93·

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a woman in a bed, likely representing the Empress Faustina. She is surrounded by several figures, including men wearing robes and crowns resembling a king and a bishop—an anachronistic medieval depiction of Roman royalty. The names "faustina" and "anthoninus" are printed vertically as labels on the right side of the scene.</note>

Faustina Augusta, who was afterwards sanctified <note>The Roman process of "consecratio," where a deceased emperor or empress was officially declared a deity by the Senate.</note> by the Romans, was highly honored and esteemed during her lifetime and after her death, more because of the goodness of her husband than for her own merit. She was a daughter of Emperor Antoninus the Pious <note>Emperor Antoninus Pius, reigned 138–161 AD.</note>, born to his wife Faustina <note>Faustina the Elder.</note>, and she was given as a wife to Marcus Antoninus the Pious <note>Marcus Aurelius, known for his "Meditations" and his Stoic philosophy.</note>, whom the Emperor had taken as a son through adoption. And as soon as the father died, they both reigned, and by the common decree of the Senate—which is the highest authority of the Romans—she was named Augusta <note>A supreme title of honor for the wives and mothers of Emperors.</note>.

called [Augusta] <note>The title "Augusta" was the highest honor for an empress.</note>, which before her had been granted to none by the Senate, although other empresses had also held the name Augusta from their husbands, but not by the recognition of the highest authority. And "Augusta" is as much as to say an "increaser" <note>Original: "merezin." This refers to the etymology of *Augustus/Augusta* from the Latin *augere*, meaning "to increase" or "to enrich."</note>. Furthermore, she was of such surpassing beauty that it was estimated by many that something of divine ornament was mixed with the mortal, and that the same beauty would not be destroyed or pass away through age or death. It so happened that her likeness, during the times of her blooming youth, was struck upon gold, silver, and copper coins <note>Original: "pfening."</note>, which are still seen to this day, from which one may well observe her beauty. 

But as far as her beauty was spread through the whole world, so much was she stained by shameful degrees of unchastity <note>Original: "vnkeüſcheit," referring to sexual immorality or infidelity.</note>. For it is held of her that she was not content with one lover besides her husband, but that she came to many a man's unchaste embrace. Among these, several were slandered by name for adultery with her, such as Serulus, Orphicus, and Modemcius. But favored above the others was Tuculus, whom the emperor once caught with her during the evening meal. To these Marcus Verus <note>The author likely means Lucius Verus, who was Marcus Aurelius's co-emperor and married Faustina’s daughter, Lucilla.</note> was also counted, although he was her son-in-law. 

And what is worse than all that, it is said of her that she was so inflamed with love for a knight <note>Historical sources, such as the *Historia Augusta*, identify this "knight" as a gladiator.</note> that from excessive desire for him she fell into a mortal illness. And so that she might recover her health, she revealed to the Emperor, her husband, the intensity of her love for the knight. He, by the advice of a physician, had her anointed with the warm blood of that same knight—drawn from his heart—to quench the turbulent heat of his wife. Yet it is said...

--- Page 278 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Empress Faustina the Younger, wife of Marcus Aurelius (here called Antoninus). The previous page discussed her beauty and her notorious infidelities with various men.</meta>

->Leaf 119<-

It is said by some that she was pregnant at that time and gave birth to a son named Commodus Antoninus <note>Commodus would eventually succeed Marcus Aurelius as Emperor, famously ending the era of the "Five Good Emperors."</note>, whom everyone judged to be the son of that same gladiator <note>Original: "ritters." While literally "knight," in this context it refers to a gladiator, as historical rumors suggested Commodus was fathered by a fighter from the arena rather than the Emperor.</note> and not of the Emperor. But when Faustina's shameful conduct became so widely known, his friends advised Antoninus that he should have her killed, or if he wished to act more humanely, he should divorce her. 

But Antoninus was kind, and although he bore his wife's adultery with a heavy heart, he nevertheless resisted the advice of his friends. Solely so that no greater shame would arise from the matter, he preferred to endure this. He gave no other answer to his friends who had advised him to divorce her than to say that one would have to return the woman's dowry to her upon separation. With these words, he gave them to understand that he possessed the Empire through Faustina <note>Marcus Aurelius gained his claim to the throne largely through his marriage to Faustina, the daughter of the previous emperor, Antoninus Pius; to lose her was to lose the "dowry" of the throne itself.</note>. For this reason, many such things must often be endured in hopes of improvement, for honorable people are often seen to fall into heavy trials, yet they emerge from the darkness of the clouds back into the light. 

However, when Antoninus had traveled at one time to the Eastern kings to manage many affairs for the common good, it happened that Faustina—at the foot of the mountain called Taurus—ended her final day due to a great illness. Through the prayers of Antoninus, she was deified <note>Original: "geheyliget," meaning consecrated or made holy.</note> by the Romans and was henceforth always called *Diva Faustina*—that is, the Divine or Holy Faustina—which before her had happened to no other woman by the Romans. 

<margin>A small red cross is present in the left margin next to the mention of her title and temple.</margin>

Then Antoninus had her named *Matrem Castrorum*, which means a "Mother of the Camp" <note>The title *Mater Castrorum* was a significant military honor, branding the Empress as a symbolic mother to the Roman legions.</note>, and he built a temple for her at the place where she had passed away. He also had beautiful images carved in her likeness to honor her and established a community of maidens called the *Faustinianas* <note>These were the *Novae Puellae Faustinianae*, a charitable foundation for orphaned girls named in her honor.</note>; and so, for a time, she was regarded as holy.

### Chapter 94. Of Semiamira.

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a group of people in 15th-century attire. Several figures are seated at a long table, likely representing a banquet or a council. Two specific figures are identified by a floating label in the center as Eliogabolus (the Emperor Elagabalus) and Semiamira (his mother, Julia Soaemias).</note>

**S**emiamira, born in Messana in Greece <note>The text identifies her birthplace as Messana in Greece, though historically Julia Soaemias was from Emesa in Syria.</note>, born of Empress Julia, who was once the wife of Severus Pertinax <note>Referring to Septimius Severus. Historically, Soaemias was the niece of Empress Julia Domna, not the daughter of Septimius Severus himself.</note>, was for a long time a dishonorable woman. 

But afterward, she was held in high esteem because of the brilliance and supreme power of her son over the Romans. She was the mother of Varius Elagabalus, who was for a time a priest of the god Phoebus <note>Referring to the sun god El-Gabal, from whom the Emperor took his name.</note>, and was afterward elected Emperor. 

She claimed to have conceived him by the Emperor Antoninus Caracalla, with whom she had previously had an affair. Because she made her body common to many men, he was called "Varius Elagabalus" by other children during his childhood, for he was born of manifold <note>original: "mangerley"</note> fathers with whom she practiced constant unchastity, and of no certain father. For "Varius" means as much as "manifold" <note>The author provides a folk etymology, linking the name Varius to the Latin *varius*, meaning diverse or changing, to insult her character.</note>. Furthermore, he was of very magnificent form and well-known for his priesthood, and also because people gave belief to his mother’s words, he...

...was claimed to be the son of Emperor Antoninus Caracalla. It was also an easy thing to do, because at that time the house of the Antonines <note>original: "geschlächt anthoninorum"</note> was so great and well-liked in Rome that everyone desired nothing else than for one of their lineage to hold the government of the empire.

And it soon came to pass that he became emperor and was named Antoninus. We [speak of the events] near Antioch. In such victory, Elagabalus came to Rome and was eagerly and highly received by the entire Senate with great praise. ...allowed nothing to be ordered or managed except by her command. And on the day he arrived in Rome and the Senate was assembled, he asked his mother to enter the Senate. She complied and was seated with such laudable finery as the others in the highest councils to govern the Roman Empire and to pass judgments, just as was customary for the Senate; yet this was never before granted to any woman by the Romans. 

O shameful Carnival mockery! <note>The author uses "fasnacht schimpff" (a Shrovetide or Carnival scandal) to describe a situation where the natural social order is turned upside down, much like the revelry before Lent.</note> That among the wisest, most serious, and most prominent men, a woman—one who has emerged from a public brothel <note>The text uses "frawenhauß" (house of women/brothel) to attack her character, suggesting she rose from a life of vice to a position of power.</note>—should be seen giving counsel and passing judgment. O ancient Roman liberty! O former holiness! O laudable dignity of our ancestors, who drove all frivolity and lives stained with shameful vices from their assembly of honorable, wise men. 

Now you see a woman without honor shamefully defiling the place of the most noble Romans: the Curii, the Fabricii, the Scipiones, and the Catos <note>The author invokes the names of legendary Roman statesmen—Curius Dentatus, Fabricius Luscinus, Scipio Africanus, and Cato the Elder—who were symbols of traditional Republican virtue and austerity.</note>. But why should I complain about a woman? For we see now that the notorious enemies of the common good—at times the youngest, most deadly, most foreign, and unknown—are found among the many powers of the Roman emperors, princes, lords, and cities as the highest and most well-regarded. 

But what happened further? Elagabalus never went into the council unless his "holy" mother went with him. And blind fortune <note>original: "blind säligkeÿt"</note> gifted her so that she was held in such high regard and honored by the common rabble that the wise Sibyls <note>The Sibyls were famous prophetesses of the ancient world; the author implies the public preferred the Empress's whims over sacred wisdom.</note> were esteemed less than she by the frivolous people. Although these things are tedious to hear, there follows a mockery: this woman was held in such high dignity by her unwise son that he [placed] her in the position of the most noble...

### ¶ Of Zenobia, the Queen of the Palmyrenes <note>The Palmyrenes were inhabitants of Palmyra, a powerful city-state in modern-day Syria.</note>, the 95th chapter.

**Z**enobia, the Queen of the Palmyrenes, was of such high virtue that she far surpassed other pagan women. Her origin was the royal lineage of Egypt, who were all called the Ptolemies. From childhood, she despised all womanly work, and as soon as she had gained some physical strength, she spent her time in the woods, well-equipped with a girded quiver and all manner of bows and arrows. With these, she hunted deer, roebuck, and all wild game, at which she became so masterful and certain that few were found to be her equal. 

In a short time, she became so manly <note>The term "manlich" (manly) is used here as a compliment to her strength, courage, and rejection of traditional gender roles.</note> that she dared to fell, and also caught and killed, bears, leopards, wild boars, and even lions. Furthermore, she was bold to ride, run, climb, and maneuver through mountains and valleys, stone walls, and all marshes, without any fear. She would sleep at night in the open fields, enduring heat, cold, rain, wind, and snow beyond what others could bear. 

She despised the company and common society of people and honored the purity of her body <note>This refers to her commitment to chastity and physical discipline.</note> above all other things. Yet, she was so hardened in manly works that she far surpassed other youths of her age in wrestling, fighting, and other knightly and manly deeds. When she had reached marriageable years, by the advice of her friends, she was given as a wife to the noblest youth, Odenathus <note>Original: "Adamato." This is a corruption or variant of Odenathus, the ruler of Palmyra.</note>, who was similarly practiced in knighthood.

<summary>The text recounts the ignominious end of Elagabalus before pivoting to the early life of Zenobia of Palmyra, emphasizing her physical prowess, rejection of domesticity, and skill as a hunter.</summary>

She was exceedingly beautiful, yet brown or dark-skinned, as all those people are from the excessive heat of the sun, with black eyes and snow-white teeth, very well-adorned. At one time, her husband Odaenathus <note>original: "adanatus"</note> was captured by Shapur <note>original: "sapore"</note>, the King of Persia, and held in harsh servitude. As soon as she had heard of this—not forgetting the old hardiness she had practiced in her youth—she laid aside her womanly finery and set out, well-equipped with the strength of an army and the help of her stepson Herod, to fight against Shapur.

And she did not cease until she had captured him, along with all his wives and children, freed her husband, and made all of Mesopotamia subject to her command. She also conquered many of the Romans' lands, which she held and ruled so powerfully that the Emperor Gallienus, and after him Claudius, did not easily dare to attempt to take them back from her. Likewise, the Eastern kings of Egypt, Armenia, and Arabia feared her; everyone feared her power and desired nothing more than to keep what they still had.

Her husband Odaenathus was eventually murdered, along with his son Herod, by the queen's cousin; some believe this happened so that her own children might rule the kingdom after their father's death, as it would otherwise have fallen to Herod. I will leave that matter as it is. Zenobia was such a protector of purity that she was not only guarded against strange men, but was also never intimate with her husband Odaenathus except for the hope of offspring. And once he had slept with her one time, she would not be joined with him again until she was certain whether she had conceived; and once she had conceived, she would not be touched by him again until...

O, the laudable wisdom of this woman, who knew so well that nature has instilled desire in us mortal humans only so that the human race might remain in existence and not perish; and whatever happens beyond that is superfluous, useless, unclean, and shameful. But where are more such women to be found? Not here. 

She kept a grand and costly court according to royal status; yet she did not have daily servants except for the old and the very discreet <note>original: "verschmitten"—in this context, meaning those who were experienced and clever enough to avoid being compromised</note>, so that frivolous temptation from any person might be all the better avoided. She also conducted her affairs according to the custom of the Roman emperors, with golden vessels and adorned with many kinds of noble gems, following the custom of Cleopatra, as she had heard. And although she was a great keeper of the treasury, none was ever found more generous in giving for the sake of honor.

And although in her youth she was diligently devoted to the hunt, she nevertheless learned Greek, Egyptian <note>original: "heydnisch", literally "heathen"; historically, Zenobia was said to be fluent in Egyptian as well as Greek and Aramaic</note>, and Latin so well that she could read and understand all ancient histories in every language. Aurelian <note>the Roman Emperor Aurelian, who defeated Zenobia in 272 CE</note> gloried and adorned himself no less than if he had captured the most powerful prince and the supreme enemy of the Roman power. Near the palace of Divine Hadrian <note>original: "diui adriani"; the Villa Adriana</note>, she ended her life there.

Afterward, when her companion had passed away in death, she recognized her own aptitude for learning and felt the sweetness of the arts. She desired no longer to have improper intimacy with any other, but rather to remain in constant practice of the arts, staying in man's clothing so as not to be recognized as a woman. She practiced so diligently, day and night in her studies, that in a short time she was esteemed marvelously high above all others in the **seven liberal arts** <term>the *artes liberales*: grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy, which formed the core of medieval education</term> and Holy Scripture.

Beyond her skill in the arts, she always appeared to lead a good, honorable, and holy life, and was regarded by everyone as a man and highly respected. This was so much the case that when **Pope Leo V** <note>The legend historically places Joan after Leo IV (d. 855), though the text here names the fifth</note> paid the debt of the flesh <note>a common euphemism for dying</note>, she was unanimously elected Pope by the most worthy assembly of all the cardinals and named **John VIII**.

### Leaf 135

<insert>139, [143]</insert>

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts two figures. On the left stands a man in royal robes and a crown, labeled Charlemagne <note>original: "Karolus magnus"</note>. On the right sits a woman in regal attire, labeled Irene <note>original: "Irene"</note>.</note>

**Irene** was the noblest woman of Athens, shining with exceptional beauty. Once, the Emperor Constantine <note>Constantine V</note> summoned her and wed her to his son, named Leo <note>Leo IV</note>. And after the death of Constantine, she became Empress and bore a son by her husband Leo, whom she named Constantine <note>Constantine VI</note> after her father-in-law. Finally, when Leo's days were ended, she reigned with her young son Constantine for ten years, splendidly and well. 

However, when he had grown into the age of a young man, he believed that the Empire should belong to him alone, and he cast his mother out of his company for eight years. Finally, because the high-minded woman was greedy to rule, and since she had fallen into discord with her son, she devised through womanly cunning a way to return to power. She planned how she might capture him and, having deposed him from his authority, keep him locked in prison. 

All of this occurred exactly as she planned, and she came to occupy the throne alone—the same throne from which, shortly before, the whole world had received its laws—and she reigned...

# Leaf 135

...but [he ruled] better than other mortal princes in great glory for five years. Afterward, however, through the petitions of his friends and the help of the people of Armenia, it came to pass that Irene was restored to the imperial throne and Constantine was released from prison, yet elevated to his father's throne. He was much kinder toward his mother than she had been toward him; he did not cast her into a dungeon, but placed her in the Palace of Eleutherios <note>The Eleutherios Palace was a grand residence in Constantinople that Irene had commissioned for herself</note>, which she herself had built. There, he provided for all her needs according to royal honors, though he sent all her friends and supporters into exile <note>original: "ellend," meaning a state of misery or being in a foreign land</note>.

He then became harsher and more cruel <note>original: "fraissamer"</note> in his governance than he had been before. Therefore, the nobles planned to depose him from power and install his uncle <note>original: "vetter," referring to Nikephoros, the half-brother of Leo IV</note> Nikephoros in his place. But as soon as he became aware of this, he fell into a shameful rage; he had the tongues of Nikephoros and his brother Christopher torn from their throats, and he had the eyes of Alexios, the high commander of Armenia, gouged out. He also forced his own wife, Maria, to enter a convent to become a nun, and took the chambermaid Theodote in her place, crowning her immediately.

Because of such misconduct by her son, Irene was moved. Although she had been forced to lay aside the good fortune of ruling the empire, she maintained her great, manly spirit <note>original: "manlich gemüt," a common historical compliment for a woman possessing political resolve</note>. She conceived a hope that if she revealed her treasures of gold and silver—which she had secretly hidden in her palace—and distributed them generously and abundantly among the nobles, she might change their minds and thus return to the empire. It happened through these gifts that those who had previously deposed Irene captured Constantine and deprived him of his sight. Thus, Irene possessed the empire once more, just as she had intended. Briefly...

<summary>This page describes the power struggle between Empress Irene and her son Constantine VI, detailing Constantine's transition from a lenient ruler to a cruel tyrant, and Irene's successful plot to reclaim the throne by bribing the nobility and blinding her son.</summary>
<keywords>Empress Irene, Constantine VI, Byzantine Empire, Eleutherios Palace, Nikephoros, political intrigue, blinding, Armenia</keywords>

<meta>The previous page detailed the cruel actions of Constantine VI, who mutilated his uncles and commander, and his controversial marriage to a chambermaid, which alienated his mother, Irene.</meta>

# Leaf 136

Afterward, Constantine fell into a deadly illness and ended his days. But later, after she <note>Empress Irene</note> had ruled for five years, Nicephorus <note>referring to Nikephoros I, who usurped the throne in 802 AD</note> set himself against her and besieged her palace, the Eleutherios. He had now received the crown of the empire from the Patriarch at Constantinople with the consent of the highest councilors, whom she had previously enriched with many kinds of gifts. 

And it happened that Nicephorus entered to the Empress in a humble manner, and with soft, kind words and many vows and promises, he persuaded her to reveal all her hidden treasures to him. But the rogue, as soon as he had brought the treasures forth, he banished the deceived woman to Lesbos into exile <note>original: "ellend," referring here to her forced relocation to the island of Lesbos</note>. There she spent her old age honorably and in good works. 

Yet some say that because the mother and the son were at odds, and a certain division of the Roman Empire had grown out of it, the Romans had Charles the Great <note>Charlemagne</note>, King of France, summoned and gave him the empire to bring it back into unity. Thus, for the first time, the imperial power came from the hands of the Romans, and Irene was betrothed to Charles. Yet at last, she was forced by Aetios <note>original: "enticio," a powerful eunuch and advisor</note> and Nicephorus into a nunnery.

### Chapter 98: Of Engeldruda the Virgin

**E**ngeldruda, of the most noble lineage of the Lords of Ravenna (who had once come from Florence), is also not unjustly counted among the virtuous women because of the greatness of her spirit. Through this spirit, she firmly and manfully <note>In this context, "manly" (original: "manlich") was a common medieval term of high praise for a woman's courage or strength of character</note> protected her purity before the Roman prince. For at the time when the Roman Emperor Otto IV had come to Florence by chance, many of the most honorable women were assembled in the Church of Saint John, who...

<summary>This page recounts the downfall and exile of Empress Irene of Constantinople following the coup by Nicephorus, and the subsequent shift of imperial power to Charlemagne. It also begins the biography of Engeldruda of Ravenna, praised for her virtue.</summary>

<keywords>Empress Irene, Nicephorus I, Charlemagne, Translatio Imperii, Lesbos, Engeldruda of Ravenna, Emperor Otto IV, Florence, Byzantine history, medieval virtue</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Engeldruda, whose noble character was introduced at the end of the previous page. The setting is a festival involving a high-ranking ruler, likely Charlemagne, following his arrival in Italy.</meta>

Leaf 136

...where once a temple had been built to the idol Mars <note>original: "abgot marci"</note>. Because the Emperor <note>likely Charlemagne, as suggested by the previous page</note> wished to make that same festival more festive and more dignified with his presence, he entered into it with a very great multitude of his princes and lords. When he was seated in the highest place according to his dignity, he beheld the decorations of the temple, the gathering of the citizens, and especially the women sitting around. However, his eyes rested longer upon Engeldruda than upon the others. 

As he observed for a while her beauty—her simple, modest clothes compared to the other women, her honorable bearing, and her maidenly gravity—he praised her, being filled with wonder. He then asked one of the citizens of the city named Bilicio, an honorable and knightly man who had been assigned to him by the city, who the maiden sitting opposite him might be; for he thought Bilicio would know best, and she was the most beautiful of all the women he saw. 

Bilicio answered him with a smile and a certain boastful eloquence: "Most illustrious prince, let her be who she may, she is such a one that she would grant you a friendly kiss if I were to command her to do so." 

But as soon as the maiden heard these words, she was filled with indignation and took it heavily to heart that her father should display her constancy <note>original: "stättikeyt," referring to her steadfast virtue</note> and maidenly shame in such a manner. She could no longer endure this; instead, before the prince could give an answer to the father's words, she stood up, her face flushed with the red of a blooming rose. With her eyes raised slightly toward her father and then lowered toward the earth, she spoke with a full, gentle voice: "Stop, my father, do not speak so! For unless violence be done to me, by God, to no one—save for him to whom you have sacredly betrothed me—shall this thing be given by me, which you have so freely offered."

<summary>During a festival in a former temple of Mars, the Emperor admires the beauty and modesty of a young woman named Engeldruda. When her father boastfully offers the Emperor a kiss from her, Engeldruda boldly speaks out to defend her honor and maidenly virtue.</summary>

### Leaf 137

...offered by me? O good God! How could such a beautiful saying be erased from a wise man’s heart?" The Emperor stood in wonder for a while. Yet, at last, he gathered from her words the maidenly, chaste spirit of this young woman and the steadfastness <note>original: "stättikeit," a key medieval virtue implying both consistency and moral firmness</note> of her holy purpose. And when he had praised with many words the maiden's protest against her father and her noble answer, he called for Guido <note>original: "Gwidonem"</note>, a noble youth. 

Because the Emperor wished that the maiden should not long be without someone to whom she might offer her kiss in modest friendship if she so desired, he betrothed her to him in the presence and by the great will and favor of her father. This youth was noble and well-gifted, truly worthy of the maiden’s great virtue and the steadfastness of her pure heart. Thus, she who had gone into the temple as a maiden for the sake of the total purity of her modest spirit, returned home as a bride amidst the great joy of all her friends and everyone else. Afterward, she bore many children by him, who even to this day retain their name from the marriage of Guido. 

I wanted to write this in praise of maidenly steadfastness, which is rightly rewarded according to its virtue, and as a reproach to those many frivolous maidens who pour out their kisses so easily <note>original: "ringklich," meaning lightly or without thought</note> that I do not know whether their behavior more resembles the customs of a brothel <note>original: "offner frawen heüser," literally "houses of open women"</note> or the maidenly state.

### Leaf 137

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts a scene of baptism and birth. In the foreground, a child is being baptized in a font labeled "Frederick" (Fridericus). **C**onstance appeared as an empress. However, because that same dignity of rank has been granted to so many women, many people hold it in somewhat low regard unless other remarkable deeds are also present. Therefore, I say that she has been placed into long-lasting memory because of one single birth. 

She was the daughter of William, who was once the best king in Sicily. To her birth, as it happened, came Abbot Joachim of Calabria <note>Joachim of Fiore (c. 1135–1202), a famous mystic and theologian known for his apocalyptic prophecies</note>, a blessed servant of God. He said to King William: "This birth is the destruction of the kingdom of Sicily." 

At such a prophecy, the king was stifled and terribly frightened, for he believed in it. He pondered in his mind by what means such a thing could happen because of a woman, but he could find no answer.

Therefore, when many years later her father, King William, and also her brothers had ended their days, and the kingdom—by right of inheritance—fell to her, though she was now old. It happened that Tancred, the petty ruler, and after him his son William, took the title of the kingdom. Because of this, great wars and the devastation of the land arose from the lords and the nobility. These conflicts, caused by the change in kings, were so great that the land was nearly destroyed by fire and iron. 

Therefore, it occurred to the minds of some, out of compassion for the kingdom in such a misfortune, that Constance should be given as a wife to a formidable and mighty prince, through whose deeds and power such storms in the land might be stilled. This was indeed accomplished, though with great effort, and by the permission of the High Priest <note>The Pope, as the "highest priest," provided a dispensation for Constance to leave her monastic vows</note> Constance consented to it; for she had wished to remain constant in what she had vowed to God to keep, and especially because she was now old. 

But when the matter had proceeded so far that there was no honorable way out of it, she was married to Emperor Henry <note>Henry VI (1165–1197), son of Frederick Barbarossa</note>, who was the son of the first Emperor Frederick. And so the wrinkled old woman was taken out of the holy cloister and, after her veil was laid aside, she was dressed in imperial garments, led before everyone, and given to the Emperor. She went into the princely hall, to the wedding bed, and laid down her virginal purity, which she had previously offered to the Lord Jesus Christ and had devoted to Him irrevocably and eternally. It happened—not without great wonder from all those who hear of it—that the old woman, in the fifty-fifth year of her age, bore a child. And since for some time this was unbelievable to many, and was judged by some to be a deceitful trick <term>lisakeyt</term> <note>Middle High German *lisikeit*, meaning cunning or a deceptive ruse</note>, it was prudently considered how this suspicion might be removed from the people. And as the time of the birth drew near, it was arranged by the command of the Emperor that all the noblest ladies of the Kingdom of Sicily should be summoned, those who wished to be present at this strange birth. 

Thus, many women, even from afar, were gathered there, before whom the old Empress gave birth to the child named Frederick. He thereafter grew into a wondrous man, and was seen to be a destructive rogue <note>original: "schelm"; in this period, the term carried a much harsher weight than "rascal," implying a villain or a source of ruin</note> not only of the Kingdom of Sicily, but of Italy and Calabria, according to the evidence of the abbot’s prophecies <note>referring to the prophecies of Joachim of Fiore, who reportedly warned that Constance's offspring would be a "viper" to the world</note>. Who is there now who would not consider the pregnancy and the birth of Constance to be miraculous, since, apart from this instance, nothing like it has been seen in our times? Also, since Aeneas of Troy first came to Italy, such a thing has never been heard—save for Elizabeth, the wife of Zechariah, from whom through God’s special working John was born, the likes of whom (and even greater) will never again come among all the children of women.

### ¶ What the marks of punctuation mean and how one should read according to them. Chapter 100.

[U] For a better understanding of this little book and others that I have translated from Latin into German, it is to be noted that various distinctions of points <note>punctuation marks</note> are placed by different writers. Now it is to be known that three kinds of points are usually placed in all speech to provide distinct understanding; these are such as a virgule <term>virgel</term> <note>a diagonal slash mark (/) used in early printing and manuscripts as a comma or pause</note>, a little...

The author here explains the "new" system of punctuation marks to clarify the reading of the text.</meta>

[Top right corner]

->Leaf 139<-

...hanging, such a little point or dot with a stroke drawn beside it like this: / and a little point or dot like this: . The first stroke is called a <term>virgula</term> <note>Latin for "little rod"; in this period, the slash (/) functioned as a comma</note> like this: /; it signifies that certain words are joined together rightly and in order, but they do not yet conclude a complete, understandable sense. The second little point is called a <term>coma</term> <note>From the Greek *komma*, meaning a piece cut off; here referring to a dot used as a colon or semi-colon</note> like this: .; it signifies that a sensible thought is concluded, but more hangs upon it, which increases the meaning and gives something further to understand, as is indicated in this speech. The third little point is called a <term>periodus</term> <note>The full stop</note> or <term>finitivus</term> <note>the finisher</note> or <term>infimus</term> <note>the lowest point</note> like this: [.] and it signifies that the sense of the speech is finished and whole, and for better understanding, nothing more hangs upon it.

But there are yet three other kinds of points which are commonly maintained by all the ancients. These are two "little moons" <note>original: "mönlin"; a charming historical term for parentheses</note> facing one another like this: ( ) between which a speech is enclosed; and they are called the sign of <term>parenthesis</term>. They signify that the speech enclosed between them is an interjected speech, without which the sense of the rest of the speech in which it is enclosed is not changed. The second is a little point with a crooked little stroke drawn upward and forward like this: ? and it signifies that the preceding speech is a question. The third point consists of two strokes drawn forward and a little upward like this: // and these are not used except at the very end of a line where a word is divided, where one part of a word ends the line and the other part begins the next line <note>The ancestor of the modern hyphen</note>. 

Although some now place a small point in the middle of the line for the *virgula* like this: · and for the *coma* set two small dots like this: : and for the *periodus* a little point drawn downward like this: . or like this: . — nevertheless, I have well-maintained the first order in this little book, wherever it has not been changed by the printers. I have now also...

...certain words are placed without distinction for the better understanding of various languages, such as when I set "sy" <note>original: "sy"; the author is using this as an example of a word that might be pronounced as one or two syllables depending on the linguistic context</note> for one syllable, and "sy" for two syllables or more; a short mark where it is not meaningful, and a long mark where it signifies something, as in this speech: "I would that you might accept my little book with thanks; that would bring me the highest joy." This book is full of examples of this instruction. Therefore, no more shall be written further concerning this to avoid lengthiness, but rather to give praise, thanks, and honor to God, who has granted us the grace to finish this little book.
